Quantitative differentiability on uniformly rectifiable sets and applications to Sobolev trace theorems Thanks: J.A. was partially supported by Basque Center for Applied Mathematics (BCAM) while on a research visit to M.M. M.M. was supported by IKERBASQUE and partially supported by the grant PID2020-118986GB-I00 of the Ministerio de Economía y Competitividad (Spain) and by the grant IT-1615-22 (Basque Government). M.V. was supported by a starting grant of the University of Oulu and by the Academy of Finland via the project “Higher dimensional Analyst’s Traveling Salesman theorems and Dorronsoro estimates on non-smooth sets”, grant No. 347828/24304228
Jonas Azzam, Mihalis Mourgoglou and Michele Villa

Abstract
. We prove L p L^{p} quantitative differentiability estimates for functions defined on uniformly rectifiable subsets of the Euclidean space. More precisely, we show that a Dorronsoro-type theorem holds in this context: the L p L^{p} norm of the gradient of a Sobolev function f : E → ℝ f:E\to{\mathbb{R}} is comparable to the L p L^{p} norm of a new square function measuring both the affine deviation of f f and how flat the subset E E is. As a corollary, given a corkscrew domain with uniformly rectifiable boundary, we construct a surjective trace map onto the L p L^{p} Hajłasz-Sobolev space on the boundary from the space of functions on the domain with L p L^{p} norm involving the non-tangential maximal function of the gradient and the conical square function of the Hessian.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification

28A75, 46E35, 26D15,

1.
Introduction and main results

A basic fact of Lipschitz functions is that they are differentiable almost everywhere. This is Rademacher’s theorem. For a real valued Lipschitz function
f
, a point
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and a scale
r>0
, define

(1.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{f}(x,r):=\inf_{A}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{|A(y)-f(y)|}{r}\,dy,

where the infimum is taken over all affine maps
A:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, by Rademacher’s theorem,
\lim_{r\to 0}\Omega_{f}(x,r)=0
almost everywhere. Can we quantify this? That is to say, how many scales can effectively be
bad
in the sense that
\Omega_{f}(x,r)\gtrsim 1
? To illustrate
1
1

1

We take this example from
[
Sem01
]
.
, for
\delta>0
, consider
f_{\delta}(x):=\delta\sin(x/\delta)
. Then, when
r\gg\delta
,
\Omega_{f_{\delta}}(x,r)
is small, simply because
f_{\delta}
is small compared to
r
(and we can always take
A\equiv 0
); when
r\approx\delta
, then
\Omega_{f_{\delta}}(x,r)
is large, because the oscillations will be of ‘height’
\delta
; when
r\ll\delta
, then
\Omega_{f_{\delta}}
will be small again, because now the smoothness of
\sin
is felt. Thus, there is essentially
just one
bad scale for
f_{\delta}
. This cannot hold in general (take a sum of
f_{\delta}
for different
\delta^{\prime}s
), though one might hope that the number of bad scales is bounded. This is in fact what Dorronsoro proved (see
[
Dor85
, Theorem 6]
). If we fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
\#{\rm Bad}_{\varepsilon}(x)
be the cardinality of integers
j
so that
\Omega_{f}(x,2^{-j})>\varepsilon
, then
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\#{\rm Bad}_{\varepsilon}(x)dx\lesssim C(\varepsilon)\|f\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}}
. Dorronsoro’s theorem implies this estimate; it is in fact stronger and, importantly, it extends the above discussion to Sobolev functions. For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
f\in L^{q}
, define

(1.2)

G_{q}f(x):=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Omega_{f}^{q}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\Omega^{q}_{f}
is an
L^{q}
averaged version of the coefficients in (
1.1
), see the definition (
6.13
) below. Recall that
p^{*}=\tfrac{pd}{d-p}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
1\leq d\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<p<\infty
. Let
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be a real valued function. Then
f\in W^{1,p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
if and only if
\|\mathcal{G}_{q}f\|_{L^{p}}
is finite, where
q
is in the following range:

•

If
d=1
, then
1\leq q\leq\infty
.

•

If
d\geq 2
, then
2
2

2

We interpret
2^{*}=\infty
when
d=2
.

1\leq q<p^{*}
whenever
1<p<2
; and
1\leq q<2^{*}
whenever
2\leq p<\infty
.

In all these cases, we have

(1.3)

\displaystyle\|G_{q}f\|_{L^{p}}\approx\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}},

where the implicit constant depend on
d,p,q
.

Note that an immediate consequence of this theorem is the following estimates for compactly supported Lipschitz functions:

(1.4)

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,dx\lesssim_{{\rm Lip}(f)}r_{B}^{d},

whenever
B
is a ball with
r_{B}\leq\diam({\rm spt}(f))
and
q
is in the appropriate range. The bound on ‘bad scales’ mentioned above is a consequence of (
1.4
).

The purpose of this article is to prove a version of Theorem
1.1
for subsets
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
which are uniformly
d
-rectifiable.
Recall that a
d
-Ahlfors regular set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

is
d
-uniformly rectifiable
or
d
-UR
if and only if it contains “Big Pieces of
Lipschitz Images" (“BPLI"). This means that there are positive constants
c
and

L
, such that for each

x\in E
and each
r\in(0,\diam E)
, there is an

L
-Lipschitz mapping
f=\rho_{x,r}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that

\mathcal{H}^{d}\Big(E\cap B(x,r)\cap f(B_{d}(0,r))\Big)\geq cr^{d}.

Recall that a set
E
is
d
-Ahlfors regular if there is a constant such that

(1.5)

C^{-1}r^{d}\leq\mathcal{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r))\leq Cr^{d}

for any
x\in E
and
0<r\leq\diam(E)
.
David and Semmes proved an estimate like (
1.4
) for
Lipschitz functions
on UR sets, see
[
DS93
, Proposition III.4.2]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
E
be UR and
f:E\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be
1
-Lipschitz. If
1\leq q<\frac{2d}{d-2}
(or
1\leq q\leq\infty
if
d=1
). Then
\Omega_{f}^{q,N}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dxdr}{r}
is a
C(N)
-Carleson measure, where
N>1
and
\Omega_{f}^{q,N}(x,r)=\inf_{A}\Omega_{f}^{q}(x,r,A)
where the infimum is over all affine maps with
|\nabla A|\leq N
.

Here, we show that, in fact, a version of Theorem
1.1
holds for
L^{p}
Sobolev spaces on this class of subsets - that is, uniformly
d
-rectifiable sets. Before stating our result, let us introduce the notion of Sobolev spaces we will use.
By
M^{1,p}(E)
we denote the Hajłasz-Sobolev space on
E
.
For
1\leq p<\infty
, we let
M^{1,p}(E)
the set of functions
u\in L^{p}(E)
(the measure here is
\sigma=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
) for which there exists a nonnegative
g\in L^{p}(E)
so that

(1.6)

\displaystyle|u(x)-u(y)|\leq|x-y|(g(x)+g(y))\mbox{ for }\mu\mbox{-a.e.}\,x,y\in X.

We refer to any
g\in L^{p}(E)
satisfying (
1.6
) as a
Haiłasz upper gradient
.
The function
g
satisfying (
1.6
) and with mininimal
L^{p}
norm is denoted by
|\nabla_{H}f|
, and is called the
Hajłasz gradient
. See Definition
4.1
.

Theorem A
.

Let
n\geq 2
, fix
1\leq d\leq n-1
and
1<p<\infty
.
Suppose that
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a uniformly
d
-rectifiable set and
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
. Then, if
q
is satisfies:

•

If
d=1
, then
1\leq q\leq\infty
.

•

If
d\geq 2
, then
3
3

3

We interpret
2^{*}=\infty
when
d=2
.

1\leq q<p^{*}
whenever
1<p<2
; and
1\leq q<2^{*}
whenever
2\leq p<\infty
,

we have the bound

(1.7)

\|\mathcal{G}^{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}\lesssim\|\nabla_{H}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}.

where
|\nabla_{H}f|\in L^{p}(E)
is the minimal Hajłasz upper gradient of
f
. The theorem holds with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}f
, a variant of
\mathcal{G}^{q}f
. See (
1.11
).

We refer to a
q
satisfying the constraints in Theorem
A
as being in the
Dorronsoro range
.

Theorem B
.

Let
n\geq 2
and fix
1\leq d\leq n-1
and
1<p<\infty
. Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a uniformly
d
-rectifiable set and
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz. Then for
1\leq q\leq\infty
,

(1.8)

\|\nabla_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}\lesssim\|\mathcal{G}^{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)},

where
\nabla_{t}f
is the tangential gradient of
f
. The theorem also holds with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}f
, a variant of
\mathcal{G}^{q}f
. See (
1.11
).

We define
\mathcal{G}^{q}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
. For a ball
B
, we introduce the quantities
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
: for
1\leq q\leq\infty
, a ball
B
centered on
E
and an affine map
A
, set

(1.9)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(x,r):=\inf_{A}\left\{\Omega^{q}_{f}(B(x,r),A)+|\nabla A|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(x,r)\right\}.

where the infimum is taken over all affine maps
A:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Here
\Omega_{f}^{q}(x,r;A)
is an
L^{q}
-averaged version of (
1.1
), where the difference is taken with respect to the fixed affine map
A
. Also, put

(1.10)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B):=\inf_{A}\left\{\Omega_{f}^{1}(B(x,r),A)+|\nabla A|\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(x,r)\right\},

where
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(x,r)
is Tolsa’s coefficient defined in terms of Wasserstein distance between measures, see (
6.3
). See also Definition (
6.13
).
Now we set

(1.11)

\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)=\left(\sum_{Q\ni x}\gamma^{q}_{f}(B_{Q})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}f(x)=\left(\sum_{Q\ni x}\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B_{Q})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Here
Q
is a Christ-David cube (see Section
5
).

We list two first applications of our results. The first one is immediate, and it’s an application of Theorems
A
and
B
in the special case where not only
E
is
d
-rectifiable, but moreover it supports a
Poincaré inequality
.

Theorem C
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
2\leq d\leq n-1
and
1<p<\infty
. Suppose that
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable and that it supports a weak
(1,p^{\prime})
-Poincaré inequality, for
1\leq p^{\prime}<p
. Let
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz. Then for
q
in the Dorronsoro range, we have

(1.12)

\|\mathcal{G}^{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}\approx\|\nabla_{t}f\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\approx\|\nabla_{H}f\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

The implicit constants are independent of the Lipschitz norm of
f
.

See Definition
4.4
below for the precise definition of a set supporting a Poincaré inequality. The proof of Theorem
C
is immediate from Theorem
A
,
B
and
[
MT21
, Lemma 6.5]
, the latter result stating that, when the hypotheses of Theorem
C
,
\|\nabla_{t}f\|_{p}\approx\|\nabla_{H}f\|_{p}
.

The second one has to do with extensions of Sobolev functions on the boundary. In the coming discussion,
E
will be the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
of a domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
.
Let
{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
be a bounded domain satisfying the local John condition (see Definition
2.3
) and with uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary. Our second corollary establishes continuity and surjectivity of the trace map onto
M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
, the Hajłasz-Sobolev space on
{\partial\Omega}
, from a space of smooth functions in
\Omega
whose gradient has non-tangential maximal function in
L^{p}({\partial\Omega})
. Since it is rather long, we state this corollary in detail in Theorem
D
, together with the relevant notation and definitions; see Section
2
.

1.1.
Remarks on Theorems
A
and
B

Recall that if
g\in L^{p}(E)
is an Hajłasz upper gradient of
f
, then the pair
(f,g)
supports a weak
(1,p)
-Poincaré inequality (PI), that is, for each ball
B
centered on
E
we have

(1.13)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}|f-f_{B}|\,d\sigma\leq Cr_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}g^{p}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where from now on
d\sigma=d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
. See also Proposition
4.2
in Section
4
.
That a Poincaré inequality holds for
f
and whatever objects one might consider as ‘gradient’, is fundamental. For example, in a statement like Theorem
A
, one might be tempted to use the tangential gradient
\nabla_{t}f
(see Definition
10.1
), instead of
g
. But consider the following example:

Example.
Let
P\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be a
2
-dimensional affine plane. Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be two unit squares in
P
that are
\varepsilon>0
apart from each other. Join
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
by an
\varepsilon
-thin and
\varepsilon
-long strip. So now
E
is connected. We define
f
so that, as before,
f\equiv 0
on
Q_{1}
and
f\equiv 1
on
Q_{2}
. We then define
f
on the strip so that the resulting function is Lipschitz continuous, with constant
\varepsilon^{-1}
. Note that at all points
x\in E
and at all scales
1\geq r\geq\varepsilon
we will have
\Omega_{f}(x,r)\gtrsim 1
, while
\nabla f\equiv 0
in
Q_{1},Q_{2}
. It can then be checked that

\displaystyle\int_{E}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Omega_{f}^{1}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\right)d\mathcal{H}^{2}|_{E}\gg\|\nabla f\|_{2}^{2}.

The real issue is that in general UR sets do not support a PI between the pair
(f,\nabla_{t}f)
. As we will see below, if we assume this
a priori
, Theorem
A
holds for
\nabla_{t}f
as well. It is true, however, that if an Ahlfors
d
-regular set supports a
(1,d)
-PI, then it is uniformly rectifiable, by a result of the first author
[
Azz21
]
.

Remark 1.3

(The square function
\mathcal{G}^{q}
and the new coefficients
\gamma_{f}^{q}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
)
.

The square functions appearing in Theorems
A
and
B
and defined in (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) are
not
the same as that of Dorronsoro’s theorem. Let us see why our results do not hold if we were to use Dorronsoro’s coefficients as they are.

Example.

Let
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be compactly supported in
B(0,1)
, smooth and with
\nabla g\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Let
G_{0}
be the subset of
{\rm spt}(g)
where
|\nabla g|>0
and assume that
\mathcal{L}^{2}(G_{0})>0
, where
|\cdot|
is the Lebesgue measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. We let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be the graph of
g
. Then clearly
E
is Ahlfors
2
-regular and it supports a weak
(1,2)
-Poincaré inequality. Now let
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by
f(x)=\langle x,e_{3}\rangle
, where
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
is the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Set

\displaystyle G:=\{x\in E\,|\,x=(p,g(p))\mbox{ and }p\in G_{0}\}.

Take
p_{0}\in G
; we can assume that
p_{0}=0
. Let
\gamma_{i}
,
i=1,2
be the curves given by
t\mapsto(te_{i},g(te_{i}))
. Then note that
\tau_{i}:=\dot{\gamma}_{i}(0)
is a basis for
T_{0}E
, the tangent plane of
E
at
0
. Then we see that (see
[
Sim83
, 4.16]
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}f(0)=\sum_{i=1,2}(D_{\tau_{i}}f(0))\tau_{i},\mbox{ where }D_{\tau_{i}}f(0)=\tfrac{d}{dt}f(\gamma_{i}(t))|_{t=0}.

Now,
\tfrac{d}{dt}f(\gamma_{i}(t))|_{t=0}=\tfrac{d}{dt}g(te_{i})|_{t=0}=\langle\nabla g(0),e_{i}\rangle
,
i=1,2
. We conclude that it might very well be that
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(E)}^{2}>0
. However, note that for all
x\in E
,
r>0
,
\Omega_{f}(x,r)=0
.

The message here is that we need to use coefficients that ‘see’ the geometry of
E
. Hence the definition of
\gamma^{q}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}
in (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). Note on the other hand that if
E
is flat, then our coefficients are just Dorronsoro’s original ones.

1.2.
Uniformly rectifiable sets

There is a roughly analogous story for rectifiable sets. Recall that a set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
\mathcal{H}^{d}(E)<+\infty
is
d
-rectifiable
4
4

4

We refer the reader to the comprehensive recent review on rectifiability by Mattila
[
Mat21
]
.
if there is a countable family of Lipschitz maps
f_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
so that

\mathcal{H}^{d}\left(E\setminus\bigcup_{i}f_{i}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\right)=0.

The analogy goes as follows: if we set
5
5

5

These are the well-known
\beta
numbers, the first version of which is due to
[
Jon90
]
.

(1.14)

\beta_{E}^{d,q}(x,r):=\inf_{P}\left(\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{B(x,r)}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P)}{r}\right)^{q}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

which is the natural version of
\Omega_{f}^{q}
for sets, it might be checked that
if
E
is
d
-rectifiable, then
\beta_{E}^{1}(x,r)\to 0
as
r\to 0
. In fact, this is essentially a consequence of Rademacher’s theorem. We then ask: can we quantify this asymptotic information and obtain a version of Theorem
1.1
for sets? Yes, if we are willing to restrict our attention to
uniformly
rectifiable sets. Uniform rectifiability is a strengthening of the
qualitative hypothesis that a set E is rectifiable: in any ball centered on a uniformly rectifiable set
E
, a
quantitatively large part of E can be covered with just one Lipschitz image. This was mentioned above. Another important characterisatio of UR sets is the so-called
strong geometric lemma
(see
[
DS91
, C3]
); that is, a set
E
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable if and only if

(1.15)

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}(x)\lesssim r_{B}^{d},

for any ball
B
centered on
E
.
This is one of the most influential characterisations of uniform rectifiability (see Section
6.2
). It’s the natural counterpart of (
1.4
) for sets; in fact, (
1.4
) is essential to the proof of (
1.15
). In this sense,
Dorronsoro’s result is a cornerstone of the David-Semmes theory.

Now, it is shown in Federer’s book
[
Fed14
]
that Rademacher’s theorem holds for Lipschitz functions defined on
d
-rectifiable sets, in the sense that
f
is (tangentially
6
6

6

See Section
10
for a definition.
) differentiable at
\mathcal{H}^{d}
-almost all points. The corresponding quantitative result is the above mentioned Proposition
1.2
, which, we recall, say that if
f
is Lipschitz on a uniformly rectifiable set, then

(1.16)

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}(x)\lesssim r_{B}^{d},

where
\Omega_{f}^{q}
is defined as in (
1.1
) except that the integral is with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
. To summarise, we have the following table:

Qualitative

Quantitative

f
Lipschitz:
\lim_{r\to 0}\Omega_{f}^{1}(x,r)=0

\|\nabla f\|_{p}\approx\|\mathcal{G}^{q}\|_{p}
. A consequence: if
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
Lipschitz, Carleson measure estimate (
1.4
).

E
rectifiable:
\lim_{r\to 0}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(x,r)=0

E
uniformly rectifible: Carleson measure estimate (
1.15
).

E
rectifiable and
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
Lipschitz: Rademacher’s holds.

E
UR and
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
Lipschitz: Carleson measure estimate (
1.16
).

Theorems
A
and
B
show that the (Euclidean space)
L^{p}
estimates shown the top right cell also holds on UR sets (i.e. in the bottom right cell).

1.3.
Remarks on the literature

The result closest to Theorem
A
is, to the authors’ knowledge, the Carleson measure estimate by David and Semmes Propostion
1.2
. As already pointed out, the novelty of our Theorems
A
and
B
compared to Proposition
1.2
, is that we have
L^{p}
estimates for Sobolev functions. This is of course fundamental in applications, for example Theorem
D
.

As far as we know, there are four proofs of the original Dorronsoro’s result.

•

The original one by Dorronsoro in
[
Dor85
]
, which uses fractional Sobolev spaces and complex interpolation.

•

A second one might be found in the appendix of
[
Azz16
]
, but it was known since the ’90s; for example it appears as an exercise in
[
Chr91
]
. It works for a smaller range of
q
’s, since it is based on an
L^{2}
Fourier calculation.

•

A third one is by Hytönen, Li, and Naor
[
HLN16
]
, who work in the much more general setting of Banach spaces and focus on estimates for Lipschitz functions (no Sobolev spaces involves).

•

Finally, a fourth one is by Orponen
[
Orp21
]
, where he obtains the Carleson measure estimate (
1.4
) via integralgeometric methods. Notably, he can extend his proof to parabolic spaces. Dorronsoro’s theorem was extended to Heisenberg groups by Fässler and Orponen
[
FO20
]
.

Dorronsoro’s theorem is not the only quantification of Rademacher’s one may think of. Of course, a standard reference on differentiability properties of functions, and quantifications thereof, is the book by Stein
[
Ste16
]
. A more recent result is that of Alabern, Mateu, and Verdera
[
AMV12
]
. They essentially prove Theorem
1.1
using

C_{f}(x,r):=\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{r}\,dy\right|

instead of
\Omega_{f}
numbers.
These coefficients measure the symmetry properties of
f
and the cancellations happening around
f(x)
. In fact, it is important that the absolute value remains
outside
the integral (a difference to Dorronsoro’s coefficients). The result in
[
AMV12
]
was recently proved on the sphere by Barceló, Luque, and Peréz-Esteva
[
BLPE20
]
. It would be of interest to prove versions of Theorem
A
and
B
with variants of the
C_{f}
coefficients, since they are perhaps more natural quantities to consider in settings where there is no linear structure. To this end, the role played by
\alpha
and
\beta
number in the definition of
\gamma_{f}
will be taken by the center of mass, see
[
Vil22
]
. Another open problem is whether a converse of Proposition
1.2
is true. It is known for one-dimensional sets, see
[
DS93
]
.

Finally, let us mention that variants of the
\gamma^{q}_{f}
coefficients, inspired by this work, recently appeared in a paper by the second author and Tolsa
[
MT21
]
on the
L^{p}
-regularity problem for the Laplacian (resolving an old conjecture by C. Kenig).
An
L^{\infty}
variant of the coefficients
\gamma^{q}_{f}
has also been used recently in a very interesting upcoming work of Bate, Hyde and Schul in the context of metric spaces, see
[
BHS23
]
.

1.4.
Overview of the proof

A few words about the proof of Theorem
A
. We first reduce matters to a good-
\lambda
inequality (Lemma
7.4
): for all
\alpha>0
there is an
\varepsilon>0
so that for all
\lambda>0
we have

(1.17)

\big|\big\{x\in E\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda,\;\mathcal{M}(\nabla_{H}f)(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\big\}\big|<0.9\big|\big\{x\in Q_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\lambda\big\}\big|.

Here
\mathcal{M}(\nabla_{H}f)
is some maximal function of the Hajłasz upper gradient. Theorem
A
follows almost immediately. To show (
1.17
), we define
E_{0}=\{\mathcal{M}(\nabla_{H}f)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}
, and note that we may assume that
|E_{0}|>0.5|E|
, for otherwise there is nothing to prove. Using the fact that
\nabla_{H}f
is the Hajłasz upper gradient, we conclude that
f
is approximately
\varepsilon\lambda
-Lipschitz on
E_{0}
. We extend
f
to
F
over all
E
with the same Lipschitz constant. We now see, using David and Semmes’ Proposition
1.2
, that the square function of
F
is small. With some rather delicate estimates we transfer this to
f
; so
\mathcal{G}^{q}f
is small on
E_{0}
, which has large measure. Since the left hand side of (
1.17
) is a subset of the complement of
E_{0}
, we conclude.

The proof of Theorem
B
also goes through a good-
\lambda
inequality, but it’s more technical and involves a stopping time algorithm. Again, we want to show that

(1.18)

\big|\big\{x\in E\,|\,\mathcal{M}(\nabla_{t}f)\geq\alpha\lambda,\;\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\big\}\big|<0.9\big|\big\{x\in Q_{0}\,|\,\nabla_{t}f(x)|>\lambda\big\}\big|.

Consider a maximal cube
R
of the set
\{|\nabla_{t}f|>\lambda\}
. We define a greedy algorithm, where we stop whenever we meet a cube
Q
for which the best approximating affine function
A_{Q}
in
\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})
has ‘bad gradient’, meaning that
|\nabla A_{Q}|\gtrsim\alpha\lambda
. If we call
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
the family of the stopped cubes, then it suffices to show that
\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}|Q|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}|R|
: indeed, the set in the left hand side of (
1.18
) is contained in the union of
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
over all maximal cubes
R
’s. Showing the packing condition is done by building an approximating Lipschitz function
F
at the level of
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
which has small Lipschitz constant and small square function
\mathcal{G}^{q}F
. This construction is similar to that of David-Semmes-Lèger.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

The paper is structured as follows: Section
2
introduces a corollary on extension of Sobolev functions. Sections
4
,
5
and
6
contain the preliminaries on Sobolev Space and Poincaré inequalities, Christ-David cubes and the various coefficients used, respectively. The remainder of the paper is split into two parts. Part 1
I
contains the proof of Theorem
A
. In Section
7
we show how to prove it via a the good-
\lambda
inequality mentioned above, in Section
8
we prove the good-
\lambda
inequality via a square function estimate and finally in Section
9
we prove this estimate. Part
II
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
B
. Section
10
contains some preliminaries on tangential gradients. Section
11
we prove Theorem
B
via the good-
\lambda
estimate mentioned above. Section
12
we define our stopping time procedure. In Section
13
we construct the approximating Lipschitz graph. In Section
14
we prove the packing condition on ‘bad gradient’ cubes via a square function estimate and finally in Section
15
we prove the square function estimate. Part
III
contains the proof of the extension result.

2.
A corollary on extensions for Sobolev functions

In this section we introduce the corollary on traces mentioned in the introduction.
Given a domain
\Omega
, a ‘domain’ function space
X({\Omega})
and a ‘boundary’ functions space
Y({\partial\Omega})
, it is often important to understand the trace map
\mathcal{T}:X({\Omega})\to Y({\Omega})
. We face two different issues: first, we would like to check that the trace map in fact exists from
X({\Omega})\to Y({\partial\Omega})
, and has good norm bounds
\|\mathcal{T}u\|_{Y({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim\|u\|_{X({\Omega})}
; and two, wether the trace map is surjective - that is, for any given
f\in Y({\partial\Omega})
, can we find
u\in X({\Omega})
so that
\mathcal{T}u=f
? This reduces to show an estimate of the type
\|u\|_{X({\Omega})}\lesssim\|f\|_{Y({\partial\Omega})}
, where
u
is a function
constructed
from
f
, or, in other words, an extension. Our Theorem
D
provides a bounded and surjective trace map
Tr
onto
M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
from the space of functions
u
on
{\Omega}
with non-tangential maximal function of the gradient in
L^{p}
. To be more precise, we introduce some notation. Set
d_{\Omega}(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,{\partial\Omega})
. For
\alpha>0
, put

\Gamma(x)=\left\{y\in\Omega:\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot|x-y|<d_{\Omega}(y)\right\}.

For a vector field
F:{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
,
1\leq m<+\infty
, the non-tangential maximal function of
F
is given by

\mathcal{N}(F)(x)=\sup_{y\in\Gamma(x)}|F(y)|.

Then set

\mathcal{V}_{p}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in C^{1}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

For later use, we also introduce the conical square function:

\mathcal{S}(F)(x)=\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}|F(y)|^{2}\,\frac{dy}{\delta_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\right)^{1/2},

and the corresponding space

\mathcal{V}_{p}^{S}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in C^{2}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\,\mbox{ and }\,\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\nabla^{2}u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

Many of the conclusions in our theorem below will hold for corkscrew domains with uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary.

Definition 2.1
.

Following
[
JK82
]
, we say that an open subset
{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
satisfies the corkscrew condition, or that it is a corkscrew open set (or domain), if there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
x\in{\partial\Omega}
and all
r\in(0,\diam({\Omega}))
, there exists a ball
B\subset B(x,r)\cap{\Omega}
so that
r(B)\geq cr
.

However, for the trace map to satisfy good bounds we need some quantitative connectedness of the domain. We choose to assume that our domain satisfies the local John condition. This condition is rather weak, but we currently don’t know if it is sharp.

Definition 2.2
.

Fix
0<\theta\leq 1
. Let
x,y\in\overline{{\Omega}}
. We say that a rectifiable curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to\Omega
is a
\theta
-
carrot path connecting
x
to
y
if

(1)

\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
.

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\gamma([0,1])\leq\theta^{-1}|x-y|
.

(3)

\theta\cdot|\gamma(t)-x|<\delta_{\Omega}(\gamma(t))
for all
t\in(0,1]
.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a bounded open subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
satisfies the
local John condition
(or that
\Omega
is a local John domain, LJD) if there exists a
\theta>0
such that the following holds true. For all
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
and
0<r<\diam(\Omega)
, we can find a point
x_{\xi}\in B(x,r)\cap{\Omega}
such that

(1)

B(x_{\xi},\theta r)\subset{\Omega}
;

(2)

for each
\xi^{\prime}\in{\partial\Omega}\cap B(\xi,r)
there is a
\theta
-carrot path connecting
\xi^{\prime}
to
x_{\xi}
. That is, we can find a rectifiable path
\gamma_{\xi^{\prime}}=\gamma_{\xi^{\prime},x_{\xi}}:[0,1]\to\overline{\Omega}
of length
\leq\theta^{-1}r
and such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\xi^{\prime}}(0)=\xi^{\prime},\hskip 11.74988pt\gamma_{\xi^{\prime}}(1)=x_{\xi},

\displaystyle\mbox{and }\,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\gamma_{\xi^{\prime}}(t),{\partial\Omega})>\theta|\gamma_{\xi^{\prime}}(t)-\xi^{\prime}|\,\mbox{ for all }\,t>0.

Our result reads as follows.

Theorem D
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
be a corkscrew domain with uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary
{\partial\Omega}
. Then the following holds.

(1)

For any
f\in M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
we can construct an extension
u
of
f
so that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}^{S}
with the bound

(2.1)

\|{\mathcal{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}+\|\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\,\nabla^{2}u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})},

for any Hajłasz
p
-upper gradient of
f
.

(2)

The trace
\mathcal{T}u
of any
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
is well defined
\sigma
-almost everywhere in the sense of Whitney averages.

(3)

If, on top of the current hypotheses, we assume that
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}({\partial\Omega})
, then

(2.2)

u\mbox{ is Lipschitz on }\overline{{\Omega}},

and

(2.3)

\nabla u\to\nabla_{t}f(x)\,\,\textup{non-tangentially}\,\,{\sigma}\textup{-a.e}.\,\,x\in{\partial\Omega},

where
\nabla_{t}f
stands for the tangential gradient of
f
.

If
\Omega
is a local John domain with constant
\theta>0
and uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary then the following also holds.

(4)

The trace map
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{V}_{p}({\Omega})\to M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
is well defined with norm bound

(2.4)

\|\mathcal{T}u\|_{\dot{M}^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim_{p,\theta}\|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

(5)

The trace map
\mathcal{T}
is surjective, and given any
f\in M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
there exists a function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
so that
\mathcal{T}u=f
, so, in particular,
u
coincides with
f

\sigma
-a.e. on
\partial\Omega
in the sense of non-tangential convergence of Whitney averages.

Let us survey some recent literature, (but mind that we will just skim the surface of a very broad and well studied area). In fact, we will mostly focus on the literature from the ‘UR world’.

Motivated by the corona problem in higher dimension, Varopoulos
[
Var77
,
Var78
]
proved that
{\rm BMO}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
can be characterised by the fact that each
f\in{\rm BMO}
in this space can be extended to a function
F
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, so that
|\nabla F|dtdx
is a Carleson measure. A main tool in Varopoulos argument was an
\varepsilon
-approximability
result, stating that a bounded analytic function in the upper half plane can be
\varepsilon
-approximated by a
C^{\infty}
function whose gradient defines a Carleson measure (see also Theorem 6.1, Chapter VIII in
[
Gar07
]
). If we fast forward forty years, we find out that, in fact, the
\varepsilon
-approximability of bounded harmonic functions actually characterise corkscrew domains with UR boundary
[
HMM16
,
GMT18
]
. In 2018, Hytönen and Rosèn introduced an
L^{p}
version of Varopoulos’
\varepsilon
-approximability: they showed that
any
weak solution to certain elliptic partial differential equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
is
\varepsilon
-approximable in their
L^{p}
sense (
[
HR18
, Theorem 1.3]
) - Varopoulos notion concerned
harmonic
functions. They show the same for dyadic martingales (see
[
HR18
, Theorem 1.2]
). Shortly after, it was shown that the
L^{p}
notion of
\varepsilon
-approximability (of harmonic functions) characterises corkscrew domains with UR boundary (
[
HT20
,
BT19
]
, see also
[
HT21
]
and
[
MZ23
]
). Back to
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
, Hytönen and Rosèn used their
\varepsilon
-approximability to construct a bounded and surjective trace map onto
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
from a space of functions
u
of locally bounded variation on the half space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
, so that
\|C(\nabla u)\|_{p}
and
\|\mathcal{N}u\|_{p}
are finite. Here
C
is the Carleson functional

C\mu(x):=\sup_{Q\ni x}\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{\widehat{Q}}d|\mu|(x,t),

the supremum is over dyadic cubes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
|\mu|
is a locally finite measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
;
\mathcal{N}
denotes the non-tangential maximal function.

Finally, to our corollary. There we show that the trace map is surjective onto the
Sobolev space

M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
, from the space of functions
u
on
{\Omega}
so that
\|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\|_{p}
and the non-tangential square function of the Hessian of
u
are finite. Note that we don’t work in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
but, rather, in the more general case of a corkscrew domain with UR boundary. A similar extension was constructed by the second author and Tolsa in
[
MT21
]
to solve the regularity problem for the Laplacian - however only for
p
close to one. Remark also that the extension in
[
MT21
]
was in fact borrowed from the current work.

3.
Notation

We write
a\lesssim b
if there exists a constant
C
such that
a\leq Cb
. By
a\sim b
we mean
a\lesssim b\lesssim a
.
In general, we will use
n\in\mathbb{N}
to denote the dimension of the ambient space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, while we will use
d\in\mathbb{N}
, with
d\leq n-1
, to denote the dimension of a subset
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
For two subsets
A,B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we let

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(A,B):=\inf_{a\in A,b\in B}|a-b|.

For a point
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a subset
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,A):=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\{x\},A)=\inf_{a\in A}|x-a|.

We write

B(x,r):=\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,|\,|x-y|<r\},

and, for
\lambda>0
,

\lambda B(x,r):=B(x,\lambda r).

At times, we may write
\mathbb{B}
to denote
B(0,1)
. When necessary we write
B_{n}(x,r)
to distinguish a ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
from one in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, which we may denote by
B_{d}(x,r)
.
We denote by
\mathcal{G}(n,d)
the Grassmannian, that is, the manifold of all
d
-dimensional linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. A ball in
\mathcal{G}(n,d)
is defined with respect to the standard metric

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{G}}(V,W)=\|\pi_{V}-\pi_{W}\|_{{\rm op}}.

Recall that
\pi_{V}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to V
is the standard orthogonal projection onto
V
.
With
\mathcal{A}(n,d)
we denote the affine Grassmannian, the manifold of all affine
d
-planes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
The set of all affine maps
A:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
will be denoted as
\mathcal{M}(n,1)
. Finally,
\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure restricted to
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

4.
Preliminaries: Sobolev spaces and Poincaré inequalities

We use this section to mention the results we will need about Sobolev spaces in metric setting and Poincaré inequalities.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a metric measure space. For
1\leq p<\infty
, we let
M^{1,p}(X)
the set of functions
u\in L^{p}(X)
for which there exists a
g\in L^{p}(X)
so that

(4.1)

\displaystyle|u(x)-u(y)|\leq|x-y|(g(x)+g(y))\mbox{ for }\mu\mbox{-a.e.}\,x,y\in X.

For
f\in L^{p}(X)
, denote by
{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
the set of
L^{p}(X)
functions
g
which satisfy (
4.1
). We also denote by
|\nabla_{H}f|
the function
\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
so that

(4.2)

\|\nabla_{H}f\|_{L^{p}(X)}=\inf_{g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)}\|g\|_{L^{p}(X)}.

We call
\nabla_{H}f
the Hajłasz gradient. If
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
, we will refer to it as a
Hajłasz upper gradient
.

We refer the reader to
[
Hei05
, Section 5.4]
for an introduction to Hajłasz-Sobolev spaces. A very useful fact about
M^{1,p}(X)
is that pairs
(f,g)
, where
f\in M^{1,p}(X)
and
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(X)
, always admit a Poincaré inequality.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a metric measure space. Let
1\leq p<\infty
,
f\in M^{1,p}(X)
and
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
. Then for each
1\leq p^{\prime}\leq p
,

(4.3)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B}|f-f_{B}|^{p^{\prime}}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\leq 2r_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B}g^{p^{\prime}}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}.

See
[
Hei05
, Theorem 5.15]
or
[
MT21
, Proposition 2.1]
for a proof.

Hajłasz upper gradients should not be confused with what are commonly referred to simply as
upper gradients
.

Definition 4.3
.

Given a metric measure space
X
and a function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
measurable, we say that
\rho:X\to[0,\infty]
is an upper gradient of
f
if, for
x,y\in X
,

|u(x)-u(y)|\leq\int_{\gamma}\rho

for any rectifiable curve
\gamma
connecting
x
to
y
in
X
.

Now, if the space
X
is so that a Poincaré holds for
f
and
all of its upper gradients
(something that comes for free when using Hajłasz upper gradients), then we say that
X
admits a Poincaré inequality. More precisely:

Definition 4.4
.

For
p\geq 1
, a metric measure space
(X,d,\mu)
admits a
weak
(1,p)
-Poincaré inequality
for all measurable functions
f
with constants
C_{1},\Lambda\geq 1
if
\mu
is locally finite and

(4.4)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}|f-f_{B}|d\mu\leq C_{1}r_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{\Lambda B}\rho^{p}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

where
\rho
is any upper gradient for
f
.

Spaces supporting a weak Poincaré inequality enjoy quantitative connectivity properties in the sense that subsets of
X
which are disjoint continua are connected by quantitatively many rectifiable curves. See
[
HK98
]
. In general, these spaces can be geometrically quite irregular and lack any (Euclidean) rectifiable structure. The Heisenberg group is one such standard example. When they are Ahlfors regular subsets of Euclidean space, however, we have the following result, due to the first author.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
Azz21
]
)
.

Let
n>d\geq 2
be integers and
X\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an Ahlfors
d
-regular set with constant
c_{0}\geq 1
supporting a weak
(1,d
)-Poincaré inequality with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{X}
with constants
C_{1},\Lambda\geq 1
. Then
X
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable
(
with constants depending on
C_{1}
and
\Lambda
)
.

The following theorem says that, if
X
admits a Poincaré inequality in the sense that we just described, then the notion of upper gradients and Hajłasz upper gradient essentially coincide.

Theorem 4.6
.

Suppose
(X,\mu)
is a locally compact doubling space admitting a weak
(1,p)
-Poincaré inequality with constant
\Lambda
. Then for every
u\in L^{p}(X,\mu)
, if
g
is an upper gradient for
u
, then for almost every
x,y\in X
,

(4.5)

|u(x)-u(y)|\lesssim|x-y|((\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda|x-y|}g(x)^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}+\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda|x-y|}g(y)^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}})

This follows from (
4.4
) and
[
HK00
, Theorem 3.2]
. One other remark is that, if
X
is a
d
-rectifiable subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then the
tangential gradient
, which will be defined later in Section
10
is an upper gradient.

Recall that a metric measure space
X
is doubling if for any ball
B
,
\mu(2B)\leq C\mu(B)
, where
C
depends on the metric. Given
s>0
, consider the following condition:

(4.6)

\frac{\mu(B)}{\mu(B_{0})}\geq C\left(\frac{r_{B}}{r_{B_{0}}}\right)^{s}.

where the center of
B
is in
B_{0}
, and
r_{B}\leq r_{B_{0}}
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be a doubling metric measure space such that
\mu
satisfies (
4.6
) for some
s>0
. Assume that the pair
f,g
satisfies a
p
-Poincaré inequality,
p>0
.

•

If
p<s
, then for all
0<q<\frac{ps}{s-p}
, we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f-f_{B}|}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{p}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

Moreover, for any
p<q<s
, we have

(4.8)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f-f_{B}|}{r(B)}\right)^{q^{*}(s)}\,d\mu\right)^{\tfrac{1}{q^{*}(s)}}\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{q}\,d\mu\right)^{\tfrac{1}{q}},

where
q^{*}(s)=\frac{sq}{s-q}
.

•

If
p=s
, then

(4.9)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B}\exp\left\{\frac{C_{1}|f(x)-f_{B}|}{r_{B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right\}\,d\mu(x)\leq C_{2}.

•

If
s>d
, then

(4.10)

\esssup_{x\in B}\frac{|f(x)-f_{B}|}{r_{B}}\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{p}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

The constants
C,\,\Lambda\geq 1
depends on
s
, the doubling constant,
p
and
q
.

Remark 4.8
.

Of course, (
4.9
) implies that

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f-f_{B}|}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int g^{p}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for any
1\leq q<\infty
.

5.
Preliminaries: dyadic lattices

Given an Ahlfors
d
-regular measure
\mu
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we consider
the dyadic lattice of “cubes” built by David and Semmes in
[
DS93
, Chapter 3 of Part I]
. The properties satisfied by
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
are the following.
Assume first, for simplicity, that
\diam(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu)=\infty
). Then for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
there exists a family
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
of Borel subsets of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu
(the dyadic cubes of the
j
-th generation) such that:

(a)

each
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
is a partition of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu
, i.e.
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu=\bigcup_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}}Q
and
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
whenever
Q,Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
and

Q\neq Q^{\prime}
;

(b)

if
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
and
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
with
k\leq j
, then either
Q\subset Q^{\prime}
or
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
;

(c)

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
, we have
2^{-j}\lesssim\diam(Q)\leq 2^{-j}
and
\mu(Q)\approx 2^{-jd}
;

(d)

there exists
C>0
such that, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
, and
0<\tau<1
,

(5.1)

\begin{split}\mu\big(\{x\in Q:\,&\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu\setminus Q)\leq\tau 2^{-j}\}\big)\\
&+\mu\big(\{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu\setminus Q:\,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q)\leq\tau 2^{-j}\}\big)\leq C\tau^{1/C}2^{-jd}.\end{split}

This property is usually called the
small boundaries condition
.
From (
5.1
), it follows that there is a point
x_{Q}\in Q
(the center of
Q
) such that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu\setminus Q)\gtrsim 2^{-j}
(see
[
DS93
, Lemma 3.5 of Part I]
).

We set
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
.

In case that
\diam(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu)<\infty
, the families
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
are only defined for
j\geq j_{0}
, with

2^{-j_{0}}\approx\diam(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu)
, and the same properties above hold for
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:=\bigcup_{j\geq j_{0}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
.
Given a cube
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}
, we say that its side length is
2^{-j}
, and we denote it by
\ell(Q)
. Notice that
\diam(Q)\leq\ell(Q)
.
We also denote

(5.2)

B(Q):=B(x_{Q},c_{1}\ell(Q)),\hskip 23.49976ptB_{Q}=B(x_{Q},\ell(Q)),

where
c_{1}>0
is some fix constant so that
B(Q)\cap\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu\subset Q
, for all
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
. Clearly, we have
Q\subset B_{Q}
.
For
\lambda>1
, we write

\lambda Q=\bigl\{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\mu:\,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q)\leq(\lambda-1)\,\ell(Q)\bigr\}.

The side length of a “true cube”
P\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is also denoted by
\ell(P)
. On the other hand, given a ball
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, its radius is denoted by
r_{B}
or
r(B)
. For
\lambda>0
, the ball
\lambda B
is the ball concentric with
B
with radius
\lambda\,r(B)
.

6.
Preliminaries: uniform rectifiability; the
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\Omega
coefficients

We gather in this section some basic about the various coefficients that will appear in the proofs below. We assume throughout that
E\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an Ahlfors
d
-regular set and that
\sigma=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
.

6.1.
Ahlfors regularity; uniform rectifiability

Recall that we introduced Ahlfors
d
-regularity in (
1.5
). Since
d
is fixed throughout the paper, we will often simply say Ahlfors regular or AR. The following fact about Ahlfors regular sets will come handy over and over again.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
1\leq d\leq n-1
and
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be Ahlfors
d
-regular. There is a constant
0<c<1
, depending on the AR constant, so that for any ball
B
centered on
E
we can find balls
B_{0},...,B_{d}
centered on
E
and with radii
cr_{B}
, so that
2B_{i}\subseteq B
and

(6.1)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{B_{i+1}},\,{\rm span}\{x_{B_{1}},...,x_{B_{i}}\})\geq 4cr_{B}.

This is a standard fact. See
[
DS91
]
, Lemma 5.8.

We briefly recalled the definition of uniform rectifiability in the introduction. Let us be more precise here.

Definition 6.2
.

We say that an Ahlfors
d
-regular set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable if it contains "big pieces of Lipschitz images" (BPLI) of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. That is to say, if there exist constants
\theta,L>0
so that for every
x\in E
, and
0<r<\diam(E)
, there is a Lipschitz map
\rho:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(depending on
x,r
), with Lipschitz constant
\leq L
, such that

\mathcal{H}^{d}\left(E\cap B(x,r)\cap\rho(B(0,r))\right)\geq\theta r^{d}.

We might often simply say uniformly rectifiable or UR sets. There is a well developed theory of uniformly rectifiable sets. We refer the interested reader to the original monographs
[
DS91
]
and
[
DS93
]
.

6.2.
The geometric coefficients
\alpha
and
\beta

6.2.1.
Tolsa’s
\alpha
numbers

We first define Tolsa’s
\alpha
numbers. They first appeared in the area in
[
Tol09
]
in connection to singular integral operators, and have been heavily used since then. The
\alpha
quantify the closeness of a Radon measure
\mu
to a multiple of
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on some plane.
Let
\mu
and
\nu
be Radon measures. For an open ball
B
define

F_{B}(\sigma,\nu):=\sup\Bigl\{\Bigl|{\textstyle\int\phi\,d\sigma-\int\phi\,d\nu}\Bigr|:\,\phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}(B)\Bigr\},

where

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}(B)=\{\phi:{\rm Lip}(\phi)\leq 1,\,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}f\subseteq B\}

and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}(\phi)
stands for the Lipschitz constant of
\phi
. See
[
Mat95
, Chapter 14]
for the properties of this distance. Next, set

(6.2)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B,P):=\frac{1}{r_{B}\,\sigma(2B)}\,\inf_{c\geq 0}\,F_{2B}(\sigma,\,c\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P}),

(6.3)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)=\inf_{P\in\mathcal{A}(d,n)}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B,P).

Note that the right hand side of (
6.3
) is computed over
2B
(rather than
B
). This is simply for notational convenience.

Remark 6.3
.

We denote by
c_{B}
and
P_{B}
a constant and a plane that infimise
\alpha_{\sigma}(B)
. That is, we let
c_{B}>0
and
P_{B}\in\mathcal{A}(n,d)
be such that, if we set

(6.4)

\mathcal{L}_{B}:=c_{B}\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{B}},

then

(6.5)

\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)=\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B,\mathcal{L}_{B})=\frac{1}{r_{B}^{d+1}}\,F_{2B}(\sigma,\,\mathcal{L}_{B})

We will need the following properties of these coefficients.

Lemma 6.4
.

[
Tol09
, Lemma 3.1]

For any ball
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

(a)

\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)\lesssim 1
,

(b)

If
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
and
r_{B}\approx_{c}r_{B^{\prime}}
, then
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)\lesssim_{c}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B^{\prime})
.

(c)

c_{B}\approx 1
.

We recall a characterisation of uniform rectifiable sets by Tolsa.

Theorem 6.5
.

[
Tol09
, Theorem 1.2]

An Ahlfors
d
-regular set
E\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is UR if and only if for all
R\in\mathcal{D}(E)
we have

\sum_{Q\subseteq R}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(Q)^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\ell(R)^{d}.

6.2.2.
Jones’
\beta
numbers

The second quantity we introduce are the well-known Jones’
\beta
numbers. For a ball
B
centered on
E
, a
d
-plane
P\in\mathcal{A}(n,d)
, and
p>0
, put

\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B,P)=\left(\frac{1}{r_{B}^{d}}\int_{B}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,L)}{r_{B}}\right)^{p}d\sigma(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

The Jones’
\beta
-number of
E
in the ball
B
is defined as the infimum over all
d
-affine planes
P\in\mathcal{A}(n,d)
:

\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B)=\inf_{P\in\mathcal{A}(d,n)}\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B,P).

Remark 6.6

(Infimising planes I)
.

In some situations, we will be dealing with planes
P_{B}
which infimise certain coefficients (e.g.
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)
) in a ball
B
. Then we call
P_{B}
one such plane and denote by
\pi_{B}
the orthogonal projection onto
P_{B}
.

Remark 6.7

(Infimising planes II)
.

We adopt the following convention. Below, it will often happen that, while working with with
p
in the range
[1,\infty]
, we will use
\alpha_{\sigma}
for
p=1
, and
\beta_{E}^{d,p}
for
p>1
. In this situation, we will abuse notation and also let
P_{B}
be the
d
-plane that infimises
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B,P)
. Then, given
P_{B}
, whether this infimises
\beta_{E}^{d,p}
or
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}
will be clear from context, that is, in a theorem that is stated for
p\geq 1
, in the proof we will assume any
P_{B}
that appears is defined for
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}
if
p=1
and for
\beta_{E}^{d,p}
if
p>1
.

In the lemma below we gather some basic properties of
\beta
numbers which will be used throughout the paper.

Lemma 6.8
.

(1)

(Well known).
Suppose that
B,B^{\prime}
are two balls centered on
E
such that
B\subset B^{\prime}
and
r(B)\approx_{c}r(B^{\prime})
. Then, if
P\in\mathcal{A}(n,d)
,

(6.6)

\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B,P)\lesssim_{c}\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B^{\prime},P).

In particular,

(6.7)

\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B)\lesssim_{c}\beta_{\sigma}^{d,p}(B^{\prime}).

(2)

(
Equation 5.4,
[
DS91
]
)
.
For any ball
B
centered on
E
,

(6.8)

\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}B,P_{B}\right)^{d+1}\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B,P_{B})

The constant in
\lesssim
is dimensional.

(3)

(Well known). For any ball
B
, we have

(6.9)

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B)\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\,\,\mbox{ for any }\,\,q\geq 1.

Proposition 6.9

(
[
DS91
, Condition C]
)
.

A
d
-Ahlfors regular set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is UR if and only if

\sum_{Q\subset R}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(Q)^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\ell(R)^{d}.

Here
1\leq q\leq\infty
when
d=1
and
1\leq q<2^{*}
when
d\geq 2
.

6.2.3.
Relation between
\beta
,
\alpha
and angle between planes

We can relate
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\beta_{E}^{1}
via the following lemma.

Lemma 6.10
.

[
Tol09
, Lemma 3.2]

For any ball
B
centered on
E

(
and recalling how we defined
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)
)
,

(6.10)

\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B,P_{B})\lesssim\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B,\mathcal{L}_{B})=\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B).

The following lemma is originally stated in more generality than this, but it is implied by the original. It says that the
\beta
number control the angles between best approxiamting planes at different scales. Recall that
\pi_{B}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
P_{B}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

[
AS18
, Lemma 2.16]
Suppose
E
is Ahlfors
d
-regular and
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
are centered on
E
with
r_{B}\approx r_{B^{\prime}}
. Then

(6.11)

d(P_{B}\cap B^{\prime},P_{B^{\prime}}\cap B^{\prime})\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}

where
d(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes Hausdorff distance. In particular, if
P_{B}^{\prime}
and
P_{B^{\prime}}^{\prime}
denote the planes passing through the origin parallel to
P_{B}
and
P_{B^{\prime}}
respectively, then

(6.12)

\angle(P_{B},P_{B^{\prime}}):=\angle(P_{B}^{\prime},P_{B^{\prime}}^{\prime}):=||\pi_{{B}}-\pi_{{B^{\prime}}}||\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}.

6.3.
The coefficients
\Omega
and
\gamma

In this subsection we introduce the quantities relevant to Dorronsoro’s estimates.
Let
q\geq 1
and consider a function
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
so that
f\in L^{q}(E)
. For a each ball
B
centered on
E
, and an affine map
A:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, let

(6.13)

\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A)=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f-A|}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 11.74988pt\Omega_{f}^{q}(B)=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A).

In the following lemma we gather some basic properties of the
\Omega_{f}
numbers.

Lemma 6.12
.

(1)

(Monotonicity). If
B\subseteq B^{\prime}\subset E
, and
r_{B^{\prime}}\approx_{c}r_{B}
, for
q\geq 1
,

(6.14)

\Omega_{f}^{1}(B)\leq\Omega_{f}^{q}(B)\lesssim_{c}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

(2)

(Affine invariance). Let
f\in L^{1}(\sigma)
, and suppose that
A_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is affine. Then

(6.15)

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}_{f-A_{0}}(B)=\Omega^{1}_{f}(B).

Proof.

(1)

The proof of this is an easy exercise with Jensen’s inequality and we leave the details to the reader.

(2)

It suffices to show that if
A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
infimises
\Omega^{q}_{f}(B)
, then
A_{1}:=A-A_{0}
infimises
\Omega_{f-A_{0}}^{q}(B)
. Suppose there existed
A_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
with
\Omega_{f-A_{0}}^{q}(B,A_{2})<\Omega_{f-A_{0}}^{q}(B,A_{2})
. But then
\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{0}+A_{2})<\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A)
, which is a contradiction.

∎

We now come to the definition of the quantity
\gamma_{f}
. Let
f
be a real valued function defined on
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Definition 6.13
.

•

For
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
f\in L^{q}(E)
and
A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
, set

(6.16)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A)=\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A)+|\nabla A|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B).

Then set

(6.17)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A).

•

If
f\in L^{1}(E)
and
A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
let

(6.18)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B,A)=\Omega_{f}^{1}(B,A)+|\nabla A|\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B),

and then

(6.19)

\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B)=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)}\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B,A).

It is immediate from the definitions that for
q\geq 1
,
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\geq\Omega_{f}^{q}(B)
. Recalling (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) we also have that, for any
q\geq 1
,

(6.20)

\displaystyle\gamma^{1}_{f}(B)\lesssim\gamma^{q}_{f}(B);

(6.21)

\displaystyle\gamma^{1}_{f}(B)\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B).

6.4.
Less basic facts about the
\gamma
coefficients

Recall that
\pi_{B}
is the projection onto
P_{B}
. Lemma
6.14
below essentially says that we can always use a specific map
A_{B}
as a minimiser of
\gamma_{f}^{p}
. Note that we do not require that
E
is UR nor that it supports a Poincaré inequality.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Ahlfors
d
-regular subset,
B
a ball centered on
E
and
q\geq 1
. Let
f\in L^{q}(E)
. There is an affine map in
\mathcal{M}(n,1)
, denoted by
A_{B}
, so that

(6.22)

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B),

(6.23)

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B)\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B,A_{B})\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B),

and

(6.24)

A_{B}\circ\pi_{B}=A_{B}.

Proof.

Fix a
q\geq 1
and
f\in L^{q}(E)
. Let
A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
such that it attains the infimum in the definition of
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)
. Define

(6.25)

A_{B}(x):=A\circ\pi_{B}(x).

Here
\pi_{B}
is the orthogonal projection onto
P_{B}
, the infimising plane for
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)
in (
6.22
), or for
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)
in (
6.23
).
Since
\pi_{B}
is linear,
A_{B}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
. Note that, by Taylor’s theorem,
A(x)=\nabla A\cdot x+b
,
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
A_{B}(x)=\nabla A\cdot\pi_{B}(x)+b
. So we have that

\displaystyle|A(x)-A_{B}(x)|

\displaystyle=|\nabla A\cdot x-\nabla A\cdot\pi_{B}(x)|

(6.26)

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla A||x-\pi_{B}(x)|\leq|\nabla A|\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B}).

Thus using Minkowski’s inequality, for
q\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{f-A_{B}}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A)+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{A-A_{B}}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form1}}}{{\leq}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A)+|\nabla A|\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B})}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\Omega_{f}^{p}(B,A)+|\nabla A|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)=\gamma_{f}^{q}(B).

The inequality for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
follows by using (
6.10
) in the last line.
This proves the second inequality in (
6.22
) and (
6.23
). The first inequalities are immediate, since
|\nabla A_{B}|\leq|\nabla A||\nabla\pi_{B}|\leq|\nabla A|
by definition and the fact that
\pi_{B}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
∎

The quantity
\gamma_{f}^{p}
also enjoys some quasi-monotonicity properties.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
q\geq 1
and
f\in L^{q}(E)
. If
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
are two balls centered on
E
such that
r_{B^{\prime}}\approx_{c}r_{B}
, then

(6.27)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\lesssim_{c}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let
A_{B},A_{B^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
as in Lemma
6.14
, (
6.24
). If
q\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B^{\prime}})=\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B^{\prime}})+|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\beta_{\sigma}^{d,q}(B)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:beta-monotone}\atop\eqref{e:omega-monotone}}}{{\lesssim_{c}}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})+|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\beta_{\sigma}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})=\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}}}{{\lesssim_{c}}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

Similarly, to estimate
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
, we use Lemma
6.4
(b) to compute

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B,A_{B^{\prime}})=\Omega_{f}^{1}(B,A_{B^{\prime}})+|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{c}\Omega_{f}^{1}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})+|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B^{\prime})=\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})\lesssim_{c}\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B^{\prime}).

∎

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
q\geq 1
and
f\in L^{q}(E)
. If
B
is a ball centered on
E
, and
c>0
, then

\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B)=c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B),

and similarly for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}

Proof.

We first show that
\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B)\leq c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B).
Let
\varepsilon>0
. Suppose
A
is such that
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A)<\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)+\varepsilon
. Then by definition,

\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B)\leq\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B,cA)=c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A)<c(\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)+\varepsilon)

and letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
gives
\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B)\leq c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B).
To obtain the converse inequality, we claim that if
A\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
attains the infimum in
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)
, then
cA
attains the infimum in
\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B)
. If that were the case, then

\displaystyle c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)=c\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A)=\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B,cA)=\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B).

To prove the claim, assume the contrary. Then there exists
A_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
so that
\gamma_{cf}(B,A_{1})<(1-c_{1})\gamma_{cf}(B,cA)
, for some small
c_{1}>0
. But then

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A_{1}/c)=c^{-1}\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B,A_{1})<\tfrac{1-c_{1}}{c}\gamma_{cf}^{q}(B,cA)=(1-c_{1})\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A).

This contradicts that
A
infimises
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)
. The same proof works for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
.
∎

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
. Assume that
q
is in the Dorronsoro range
7
7

7

Which, recall, is given by

•

If
d=1
, then
1\leq q\leq\infty
.

•

If
d\geq 2
, then
1\leq q<p^{*}
whenever
1<p<2
; and
1\leq q<2^{*}
whenever
2\leq p<\infty
.

.
Then there exist constants
C,\,\Lambda\geq 1
so that

(6.28)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{p}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

whenever
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
and
B
is a ball centered on
E
.
The constants
C,\,\Lambda
depend on
d
,
n
, the Ahlfors regularity constant of
E
,
p
and
q
. The same holds for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
.

Proof.

Let
A_{0}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
be so that
A_{0}\equiv f_{B}
(so, in particular,
|\nabla A_{0}|=0
). For
q\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\leq\gamma_{f}^{q}(B,A_{0})=\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{0})+0\cdot\beta_{\sigma}^{d,q}(B)=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f-f_{B}|}{r_{B}}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

The same estimate holds for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
by replacing
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)
with
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)
. By Proposition
4.2
, if
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
, the pair
(f,g)
satisfy a
(p^{\prime},p^{\prime})
-Poincaré inequality, for all
1<p^{\prime}\leq p
. By Jensen’s inequality, the same pair satisfies a
(1,p^{\prime})
-Poincaré inequality for the same range of
p^{\prime}
.

We want to apply Theorem
4.7
with the metric measure space given by
E
, with the Euclidean distance and measure
\mu=\sigma=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{E}
. Note that since
E
is Ahlfors
d
-regular, then (
4.6
) is satisfied with
s=d
.
We consider two distinct cases.

•

If
d=1
, then
p^{\prime}>d
always, and thus we can apply (
4.10
) for all
1\leq q\leq\infty
; this gived (
6.28
) for all
1\leq q\leq\infty
on the left hand side, and all
1<p^{\prime}\leq p
on the right hand side.

•

d\geq 2
. In this case, we distinguish two subcases.

–

1<p<2
. In this case, we have to consider the range of
q
’s
1\leq q<p^{*}
, as given in the hypotheses of the Lemma. Since
d\geq 2
, then
d>p
, and thus Theorem
4.7
(1) is applicable. Theorem
4.7
(1) applies with the same range of
q
’s, i.e.
1\leq q<p^{*}
. So, we obtain (
6.28
) in this case.

–

2\leq p<\infty
. The hypotheses of the Lemma tells us that
1\leq q<2^{*}
. If
p<d
(which forces
d>2
) then
2^{*}\leq p^{*}
, and thus we apply again Theorem
4.7
(1), and obtain (
6.28
). For
p\geq d
, (
6.28
) is immediate using Theorem
4.7
(2) and (3) and Remark
4.8
.

The estimate for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
follows from the above, since in this case
q=1
.

∎

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
and
B,B^{\prime}
two balls centered on
E
so that
B\subset B^{\prime}
and
r_{B}\approx r_{B^{\prime}}
. Then, whenever
q
is in the Dorronsoro range,

(6.29)

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime});

(6.30)

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\lesssim_{c}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B^{\prime}}g^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\mbox{ for }\,g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f);

(6.31)

\displaystyle|A_{B}(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(x)|\lesssim_{c}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,B^{\prime})+r_{B^{\prime}}),

where
A_{B}
and
A_{B^{\prime}}
are the maps from Lemma
6.14
.

Proof.

First we show (
6.31
) assuming (
6.29
). Let
x_{0}
be a point in
B^{\prime}\cap E
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We compute

\displaystyle|A_{B}(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla A_{B}(x-x_{0})-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}(x-x_{0})|+|A_{B}(x_{0})-A_{B^{\prime}}(x_{0})|

(6.32)

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}||x-x_{0}|+|A_{B}(x_{0})-f(x_{B})|+|f(x_{B})-A_{B^{\prime}}(x_{0})|

Using (
6.29
), we can bound the first term in (
6.32
) by
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})|x-x_{0}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,B^{\prime})
. Using Chebyshev’s inequality, we can choose
x_{0}\in B\cap E
such that the last two terms in (
6.32
) are bounded by
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)r_{B}
and
\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}
, respectively. We then obtain (
6.31
) using the quasi-monotonicty of
\gamma_{f}^{q}
, as per (
6.27
).

We now focus on proving (
6.29
) and (
6.30
). Let
\varepsilon>0
to be chosen later. We look at two cases.

Case 1.
First, we assume that
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon
. Let
A_{B}
,
A_{B^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{M}(n,1)
as in Lemma
6.14
. Using the bound
|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)
(and the equivalent for
B^{\prime}
, see (
6.22
)) and the quasi-monotonicity of
\gamma_{f}^{q}
, see (
6.27
), we compute

|\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\leq|\nabla A_{B}|+|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\\
\leq\varepsilon^{-1}|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)+\varepsilon^{-1}|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}\atop\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon^{-1}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

Moreover, we clearly have

|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon^{-1}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

This proves (
6.29
) and (
6.30
) in this case. Note that there are no constraints on
q
other than
q\geq 1
.

Before looking into Case 2, we need the following auxiliary claim, which we will prove below.

Claim 6.19
.

Keep the notation and assumptions form Lemma
6.18
. Let
\{B_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}
be the
d+1
balls found by applying Lemma
6.1
to the ball
B
(as in the statement of Lemma
6.18
). We can find
d+1
points
x_{i}\in B_{i}\cap E
,
i=0,...,d
, so that if
y_{i}=\pi_{B}(x_{i})
and
y_{i}^{\prime}=\pi_{B^{\prime}}(x_{i})
,

(6.33)

|x_{i}-y_{i}|+|x_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|\lesssim_{c}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)r_{B}+\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}},

and

(6.34)

|f(x_{i})-A_{B}(y_{i})|+|f(x_{i})-A_{B^{\prime}}(y_{i}^{\prime})|\lesssim_{c}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})+\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}}).

Case 2.
Now suppose

\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\lesssim_{c}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})<\varepsilon.

Let
x_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq d
be the points found in Claim
6.19
, and recall the notation
\{y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}\subset P_{B}
and
\{y^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}\subset P_{B^{\prime}}
. Since
P_{B}\in\mathcal{A}(n,d)
and
y_{0}\in P_{B}
, then
P_{B}-y_{0}\in\mathcal{G}(n,d)
and
P_{B}^{\perp}-y_{0}
is its orthogonal complement. Any
v\in{\mathbb{B}}
can be written as
\alpha_{1}v_{1}+\alpha_{2}v_{2}
, where
v_{1}\in P_{B}-y_{0}
,
v_{2}\in P_{B}^{\perp}-y_{0}
,
|v_{i}|=1
, and
\alpha_{i}\leq 1
. Thus

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle=\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}}\left|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{i}\leq 1,\,i=1,2\\
|v_{i}|=1,i=1,2\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})\alpha_{i}v_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap P_{B}-y_{0}}\left|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v\right|

(6.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap P_{B}^{\perp}-y_{0}}\left|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v\right|.

We will bound the last two terms in (
6.35
) separately.

If we choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small with respect to the constant
c
in (
6.1
), Lemma
6.1
, then

(6.36)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{i+1},{\rm span}\{y_{0},...,y_{i}\})\geq c^{\prime}r_{B}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{i+1}^{\prime},{\rm span}\{y_{0}^{\prime},...,y_{i}^{\prime}\})\geq c^{\prime}r_{B},

where
c^{\prime}\approx c
. We immediately relabel
c^{\prime}
as
c
to keep a manageable notation.
In particular, the vectors
\{u_{i}=y_{i}-y_{0}:i=1,...,d\}
form a basis for
P_{B}-y_{0}
, and also
|u_{i}|\approx r_{B}
. Hence there exists a
1\leq j\leq d
so that

(6.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in P_{B}-y_{0}\atop|v|=r_{B}}|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v|\lesssim_{d}|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})(y_{j}-y_{0})|.

We continue computing:

(
6.37
)

\displaystyle\approx_{d}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|(A_{B}(y_{j})-A_{B}(y_{0}))-(A_{B^{\prime}}(y_{j})-A_{B^{\prime}}(y_{0}))|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\lesssim_{d}\left|\left(A_{B}(y_{j})-A_{B}(y_{0})\right)+\left(A_{B^{\prime}}(y_{0}^{\prime})-A_{B^{\prime}}(y_{j}^{\prime}\right))\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\left|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}\right|\left(|y_{j}-y_{j}^{\prime}|+|y_{0}-y_{0}^{\prime}|\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt=:T_{1}+T_{2}.

Now,

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left(A_{B}(y_{j})-A_{B}(y_{0})\right)-\left(f(x_{j})-f(x_{0})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\left|(f(x_{j})-f(x_{0}))-\left(A_{B^{\prime}}(y^{\prime}_{j})-A_{B^{\prime}}(y^{\prime}_{0})\right)\right|.

Recall now that we chose the points
\{x_{i}\}
appropriately, and so, by Claim
6.19
, they satisfy (
6.34
). This gives the bound

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})r_{B}+\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})r_{B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}}}{{\lesssim_{d,c}}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})r_{B^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}}}{{\lesssim_{d,c}}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}.

On the other hand, again by the choice of
\{x_{i}\}
(
6.33
),

\displaystyle T_{2}\lesssim|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}}}{{\lesssim}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}.

This shows that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in P_{B}-y_{0}\\
|v|=1\end{subarray}}\left|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v\right|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

We now bound the last term in (
6.35
). If
v\in P_{B}^{\perp}-y_{0}
, then

|\pi_{B^{\prime}}(v)|=|\pi_{B^{\prime}}(v)-\pi_{B}(v)|\leq\|\pi_{B^{\prime}}-\pi_{B}\|_{{\rm op}}\cdot|v|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:beta-angle}\atop{{\rm Lemma}\,\ref{l:beta-properties}}}}{{\lesssim}}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})|v|,

and so if
|v|=r_{B}
,

|(\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}})v|=|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}v|\leq|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\cdot\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})r_{B}.

Combining the above estimates gives (
6.29
) also in Case 2 (small
\beta
’s).

What we have left, is to prove (
6.30
) for Case 2 (small
\beta
’s).
By Chebychev, we can find points
z_{i}\in B_{i}\cap E
so that

\frac{|A_{B^{\prime}}(z_{i})-A_{B^{\prime}}(z_{0})|}{|z_{i}-z_{0}|}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|A_{B^{\prime}}(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(y)|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y).

Just as before with the
y_{i}
, (
15.28
) implies that

|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\lesssim\max_{i=1,...,d}\frac{|A_{B^{\prime}}(z_{i})-A_{B^{\prime}}(z_{0})|}{|z_{i}-z_{0}|}.

Combining these inequalities gives

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{i}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|A_{B^{\prime}}(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(y)|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Omega_{f}^{1}(B,A_{B^{\prime}})+\max_{i}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y).

Now, using (
6.14
) and (
6.22
), we obtain

(6.38)

\Omega_{f}^{1}(B^{\prime},A_{B}^{\prime})\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B}^{\prime})\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime}).

To deal with the other term, we compute, for each
0\leq i\leq d
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|f(x)-f_{B^{\prime}}|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{0}}\frac{|f_{B^{\prime}}-f(y)|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B^{\prime}}\frac{|f(x)-f_{B^{\prime}}|}{r_{B^{\prime}}}\,d\sigma(x)\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B^{\prime}}g^{p}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where we used the fact that
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
and Theorem
4.2
together with Jensen’s inequality.
This proves (
6.30
) in this case as well, and finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Claim
6.19
.

It is easy to see that we can assume that the quantities in the upper bounds of (
6.33
) and (
6.34
) are positive, for otherwise the claim follows trivially. Let
\{B_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}
be the family of balls as in the statement of the Claim. Let
C>0
and set

\displaystyle E_{i}=\big\{

\displaystyle x\in B_{i}:\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B})\geq C\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)r_{B},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B^{\prime}})\geq C\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}},

\displaystyle|f(x)-A_{B}(x)|>C\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})r_{B},\;\;\mbox{ or }

\displaystyle|f(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(x)|>C\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})r_{B^{\prime}}\big\}.

If
E_{i}=B_{i}
, then by Chebychev’s inequality,

\displaystyle 1
