\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B})}{C\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)r_{B}}d\sigma(x)+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{B^{\prime}})}{C\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\frac{|f(x)-A_{B}(x)|}{C\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,A_{B})r_{B}}d\sigma(x)+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{i}}\frac{|f(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(x)|}{C\Omega_{f}^{q}(B^{\prime},A_{B^{\prime}})r_{B^{\prime}}}d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{c}C^{-1}

which is a contradiction for
C>0
large enough (depending on
c
), thus we can find
x_{i}\in B_{i}\cap E\backslash E_{i}
which satisfies the above properties (and recall that
A_{B}(x_{i})=A_{B}\circ\pi_{B}(x_{i})=A_{B}(y_{i})
). This proves the claim.
∎

Part I

Proof of Theorem
A
.

The Hajłasz upper gradients control the square function.

7.
Proof of Theorem
A
via a good
\lambda
-inequality

In the following two sections, we prove Theorem
7.1
below, of which Theorem
A
is an immediate consequence. To state it, let us introduce some notation.
For
q\geq 1
, and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(E)
, set

(7.1)

\mathcal{G}^{q}_{Q_{0}}f(x)=\left(\sum_{Q_{0}\supseteq Q\ni x}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{Q_{0}}f(x)=\left(\sum_{Q_{0}\supseteq Q\ni x}\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}(B_{Q})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Theorem 7.1
.

Fix
2\leq d\leq n-1
and
1<p<\infty
. Suppose that
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a uniformly
d
-rectifiable set and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(E)
. Let
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
. Then, if
C_{1}\geq 1
is chosen sufficiently large and if
1\leq q<\min\{2^{*},p^{*}\}
,

(7.2)

\|\mathcal{G}^{q}_{Q_{0}}f\|_{L^{p}(Q_{0})}\lesssim\left(\int_{2C_{1}B_{Q_{0}}}g^{p}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

whenever
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
. The same statement holds if we replace
\mathcal{G}^{q}_{Q_{0}}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{Q_{0}}
.

Before getting started, we need the following corollary of Proposition
1.2
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be uniformly
d
-rectifiable and
f:E\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be
L
-Lipschitz with
L>0
. If
1\leq q<\frac{2d}{d-2}

(
or
1\leq q\leq\infty
if
d=1
)
. Then
\Omega_{f}^{q,2L}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dxdr}{r}
is a
CL^{2}
-Carleson measure.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
1.2
to the function
\frac{f}{L}
, which is now
1
-Lipschitz, and with
N=2
. Then any ball
B
centered on
E
with
r_{B}<\diam E
,

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\Omega_{f/L}^{q,2}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dr}{r}d\sigma(x)\lesssim r_{B}^{d}

and so for

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\Omega_{f}^{q,2L}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dr}{r}d\sigma(x)=L^{2}\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r_{B}}\Omega_{f/L}^{q,2}(x,r)^{2}\frac{dr}{r}d\sigma(x)\lesssim L^{2}r_{B}^{d}.

∎

Remark 7.3
.

We set some notation. Fix
n,d,p,E,Q_{0}
and
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
as in Theorem
7.1
. Fix also
C_{1}>2\Lambda
. These will remain fixed throughout the current section. Let
|\nabla_{H}f|\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
be the minimal Hajłasz upper gradient. For
1\leq s<p
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x):=\mathcal{G}_{Q_{0}}^{q}f(x)\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 11.74988pt\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x):=\left(M_{C_{1}\ell(Q_{0})}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},

where for a function
u

M_{R}u(x)=\sup_{0<r<R}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}|u|\,d\sigma.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
q
be in the Dorronsoro’s range and

(7.3)

\frac{dq}{d+q}<s<\min\{q,p,d\}

if
q>1
or
s=1
if
q=1
. For each
\alpha>1
there exists an
\varepsilon>0
so that for each
\lambda>0
,

(7.4)

\big|\big\{x\in Q_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda,\;\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\big\}\big|<\frac{15}{16}\big|\big\{x\in Q_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\lambda\big\}\big|.

Remark 7.5
.

Before going any further we check that the choice of
s
in the hypotheses of Lemma
7.4
is in fact possible. It suffices to show that, with
1<p<\infty
and
q
is in the Dorronsoro range, then
dq/(d+q)<\min\{p,q,d\}
. Recall that the Dorronsoro range is given by

•

If
d=1
, then
1\leq q\leq\infty
.

•

If
d\geq 2
, then
1\leq q<p^{*}
whenever
1<p<2
; and
1\leq q<2^{*}
whenever
2\leq p<\infty
.

That
dq/(d+q)<\min\{d,q\}
, is true in all cases.
On the other hand, we see that

•

If
d=1
, then
q/(1+q)<1<p
, so that (
7.3
) is satisfied in this case.

•

If
d\geq 2
, we have the usual two cases: if
1<p<2
, then
q<p^{*}
. But note that
dp/(d+q)<p
if and only if
q<p^{*}
. On the other hand, if
2\leq p<\infty
, then
q<2^{*}
, and
2^{*}\leq p^{*}
since
p\geq 2
; thus (
7.3
) is satisfied in this case, too.

Now let us show how Lemma
7.4
proves Theorem
7.1
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Let
\alpha>1
and let
\varepsilon>0
be as in Lemma
7.4
. We compute

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{0}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{q}f\right)^{p}\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\{x\in Q_{0}:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\lambda\}\right|\lambda^{p-1}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=\alpha^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\{x\in Q_{0}:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda\}\right|\lambda^{p-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left\{x\in Q_{0}:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\right\}\right|\lambda^{p-1}d\,\lambda

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\alpha^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left(\{x\in Q_{0}:\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)>\varepsilon\lambda\}\right)\right|\lambda^{p-1}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

Because Lemma
7.4
holds for each
\alpha>1
, we apply it with
\alpha
satisfying
\alpha^{p}\tfrac{15}{16}<\tfrac{16}{17}
. Then

(7.5)

\displaystyle I_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:G>a,M<vea}}}{{<}}\alpha^{p}\frac{15}{16}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left(\{x\in Q_{0}:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\lambda\}\right)\right|\lambda^{p-1}\,d\lambda\leq\frac{16}{17}\int_{Q_{0}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{q}f\right)^{p}\,d\sigma.

On the other hand we trivially have

(7.6)

\displaystyle I_{2}\leq a^{p}\varepsilon^{-p}\int_{Q_{0}}\left(\mathcal{M}^{s}f\right)^{p}\,d\sigma.

Putting (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) together (and ’hiding’ the upper bound for
I_{1}
on the left hand side),

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{q}f)^{p}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle<\frac{34}{\varepsilon^{p}}\int_{Q_{0}}\left(\mathcal{M}^{s}f\right)^{p}\,d\sigma\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\int(M_{C_{1}\ell(Q_{0})}(\mathds{1}_{2C_{1}B_{Q_{0}}}\,g^{s}))^{\frac{p}{s}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p/s}\int_{2C_{1}B_{Q_{0}}}(|\nabla_{H}f|^{s})^{\frac{p}{s}}d\sigma=\int_{2C_{1}B_{Q_{0}}}|\nabla_{H}f|^{p}d\sigma

where, in the last inequality, we used that the Hardy-Littlewood maximal function is bounded from
L^{p/s}(\sigma)
to
L^{p/s}(\sigma)
, which holds since
s<p
by assumption.
∎

8.
The proof of the good-
\lambda
inequality

In this section we prove the good-
\lambda
inequality in Lemma
7.4
.
We start with some set up. Given
\lambda>0
, let
{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
be the set of maximal cubes in

\{x\in Q_{0}\,:\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\lambda\}.

To prove Lemma
7.4
, it suffices to show that it holds for each
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
. That is, we aim to prove that for each
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
, the following is true: for each
\alpha>1
there is
\varepsilon>0
so that

(8.1)

|\{x\in R\,:\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda,\;\;\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}|<\frac{15}{16}|R|.

Let now
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
,
\alpha>0
, and
\varepsilon>0
be a constant to be determined later and will only depend on
\alpha
. Let

(8.2)

\displaystyle E_{R}=\{x\in R\,:\,\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}.

If
|E_{R}|<\frac{1}{2}|R|
, then

|\{x\in R\,:\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda,\;\;\mathcal{M}^{s}g(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}|\leq|E_{R}|\leq\frac{15}{16}|R|

and we’re done. We then assume that
|E_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|R|
. Recall that the defining property of
|\nabla_{H}f|\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
is that for
\sigma
-a.e.
x,y\in E
we have that

\displaystyle|f(x)-f(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|x-y|(|\nabla_{H}f(x)|+|\nabla_{H}f(y)|.

Let
\delta>0
be arbitrary, and pick
\delta_{1}
so small so that
|x-y|\delta_{1}<\delta
. Since
|\nabla_{H}f|\in L^{p}
, we know that, if
\phi_{t}
is an approximate identity,
\lim_{t\to 0}\phi_{t}*|\nabla_{H}f|(x)=|\nabla_{H}f|(x)
for
\sigma
-a.e.
x
(see
[
DZ01
]
, Corollary 2.9). Then we may pick
t
so small, so that the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle|f(x)-f(y)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+|x-y|(\phi_{t}*|\nabla_{H}f|(x)+\phi_{t}*|\nabla_{H}f|(y))

\displaystyle\leq 2+\delta\left[\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(y,t)}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+|x-y|\left[\left(\sup_{r\leq\Lambda|x-y|}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}+\left(\sup_{r\leq\Lambda|x-y|}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(y,r)}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\right]

If
x,y\in E_{R}
, then
|x-y|\leq\ell(Q_{0})
, and so, since
C_{1}\geq 2\Lambda
,
M_{\Lambda|x-y|}g^{s}\leq M_{C_{1}\ell(Q_{0})}|\nabla_{H}f|^{s}
. Hence
f
is
C\varepsilon\lambda
-Lipschitz on
E_{R}
. By Kirszbraun’s extension theorem, we can extend
f
to a
C\varepsilon\lambda
-Lipschitz function
F
defined on all of
E
. Recall that
q<\tfrac{2d}{d-2}
. Thus we can apply Lemma
7.2
and with Chebychev’s inequality we find
E^{1}_{R}\subseteq E_{R}
with
|E^{1}_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{4}|R|
so that,

(8.3)

\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{F}^{q,2C\varepsilon\lambda}(B_{Q})^{2}\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\;\;\mbox{ for }x\in E^{1}_{R}.

Let
A_{Q}^{F}
be the affine map minimizing
\Omega_{F}^{q,2C\varepsilon\lambda}(B_{Q})
(not to be confused with the affine map from Lemma
6.14
). So, in particular,

(8.4)

|\nabla A_{Q}^{F}|\leq 2C\varepsilon\lambda\;\;\mbox{ if $Q\subseteq R$ intersects $E_{R}^{1}$}.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume the notation of Remark
7.3
. Suppose that
1\leq q<\min\{2^{*},p^{*}\}
and
1\leq s<\min\{q,p,d\}
if
q>1
or
s=1
if
q=1
. Then

(8.5)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{E^{1}_{R}}\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})^{2}\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}.

Let’s finish the proof of the good-
\lambda
inequality assuming Lemma
8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

Lemma
8.1
together with Chebychev’s inequality implies that there is
E^{2}_{R}\subseteq E^{1}_{R}
with
|E^{2}_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|E^{1}_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{8}|R|
so that, for all
x\in E^{2}_{R}
,

(8.6)

\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})^{2}\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}.

Since
E
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable we can use Proposition
6.9
: there is
E^{3}_{R}\subseteq E^{2}_{R}
with
|E^{3}_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{16}|R|
so that for all
x\in E^{3}_{R}

(8.7)

\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B_{Q})^{2}\lesssim 1,

where the constant behind the symbol
\lesssim
depends on the uniform rectifiability constants of
E
.
Denote by
\widehat{R}
the unique parent cube of
R
. Then, for
x\in E^{2}_{R}
and
0<r\leq\ell(\widehat{R})
, if
x\in Q\subseteq\widehat{R}
and
q\geq 1
,

(8.8)

\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})\leq\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})=\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})+|\nabla A_{Q}^{F}|\cdot\beta_{\sigma}^{d,q}(B_{Q})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:AQF<2Ce}}}{{\lesssim}}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})+\varepsilon\lambda\cdot\beta_{\sigma}^{d,q}(B_{Q}),

which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form10}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{R\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A^{F}_{Q})^{2}+(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\sum_{R\subseteq Q\ni x}\beta_{E}^{q}(B_{Q})^{2}

(8.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:sumomegaQAQF<elambda},\eqref{e:sumbetaQ<1}}}{{\lesssim}}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\;\;\mbox{ for all}\;\;x\in E^{3}_{R}.

For
\widetilde{\gamma}_{f}
we obtain the very same estimate, since (
8.7
) also holds for
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}
.
Because
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
is a maximal cube of
\{x\in Q_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{g}f(x)>\lambda\}
, then

(8.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{\widehat{R}\subseteq Q\subseteq Q_{0}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)^{2}=\sum_{x\in Q\subseteq Q_{0}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B_{Q})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form12}\atop\eqref{e:form11}}}{{\leq}}\lambda^{2}+(C\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}.

Note that the constant
C
in the last display depends only on
d,n
and on
\Lambda
, which in turn depends only on
d
, the Ahlfors regularity constant,
p
and
q
. Thus we can pick
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small so that
C\varepsilon+1<\alpha
, to obtain

(8.11)

\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)^{2}\leq(\alpha\lambda)^{2}\,\,\mbox{ for each }x\in E^{3}_{R},\,R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda},

which implies that

|\{x\in R:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)\leq\alpha\lambda\}|\geq|E^{3}_{R}|\geq\frac{1}{16}|R|,

and so

\displaystyle|\{x\in R:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda,\;\;\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\leq|\{x\in R:\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)>\alpha\lambda\}|\leq\frac{15}{16}|R|.

∎

This proves (
8.1
) and finishes the proof. The next section is dedicated to proving the claim in (
8.5
).

9.
Proof of the square function estimate (
8.5
)

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

By Jensen’s inequality, it suffices to prove Lemma
8.1
assuming
q\geq 2
. Remark that this is consistent with the upper bound
q<\min\{2^{*},p^{*}\}
, since, if the smaller number is
2^{*}
, we can find
2\leq q<2^{*}
; if
p^{*}
is the smaller number, it holds nonetheless that
p^{*}>2
, since
p>1
. Recall also that we picked
s
so that
dq/(d+q)<s<\min\{d,p,q\}
.
Now let

\displaystyle h=f-F,

where recall that
F
is a
C\varepsilon\lambda
-Lipschitz function on
E
which coincide with
f
on
E_{R}
. Minkowski’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{|f-A_{Q}^{F}|}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{|F-A_{Q}^{F}|}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{h}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

and so we get

\displaystyle\int_{E^{1}_{R}}\sum_{\widehat{R}\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})^{2}d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{E^{1}_{R}}\sum_{\widehat{R}\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{F}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})^{2}d\sigma(x)+\int_{E_{R}^{1}}\sum_{\widehat{R}\supseteq Q\ni x}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{|h|}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:sum-over-E'}}}{{\lesssim}}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}|E_{R}^{1}|+\sum_{Q\subseteq R\atop Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{|h|}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}|Q|

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}|R|+I.

Now we estimate
I
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{R}:=\{Q_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}(E)
be a Whitney decomposition of
E\backslash E_{R}^{1}
, that is, a family of maximal cubes
Q_{j}
for which
10B_{Q_{j}}\cap E^{1}_{R}=\emptyset
. For each
Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}
, let
x_{j}\in E_{R}^{1}
be so that

\displaystyle|x_{Q_{j}}-x_{j}|\approx\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{j},E_{R}^{1}).

(Recall that
x_{Q}
denotes the center of the cube
Q
). This in particular implies that we can find a ball
B_{j}
centered on
x_{j}
such that
r(B_{j})\approx\ell(Q_{j})
and so that
Q_{j}\subset B_{j}
. We now compute

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{j}}|f(y)-F(y)|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B_{j}}|f(y)-f_{B_{j}}|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\int_{B_{j}}|f_{B_{j}}-F(y)|^{q}\,d\sigma(y):=I_{1}(Q_{j})+I_{2}(Q_{j}).

We first estimate
I_{2}(Q_{j})
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{j}}\left|f_{B_{j}}-F(y)\right|^{q}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{j}}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}f(z)-F(y)\,d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B_{j}}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}f(z)-F(z)d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)+\int_{B_{j}}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}F(z)-F(y)d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle=:I_{2,1}(Q_{j})+I_{2,2}(Q_{j}).

Since
F
is
C\varepsilon\lambda
-Lipschitz and
|B_{j}|\approx|Q_{j}|\approx\ell(Q_{j})^{d}
, we have

(9.1)

I_{2,2}(Q_{j})\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{q}\ell(Q_{j})^{q+d}.

On the other hand, since
F(x_{j})=f(x_{j})
as
x_{j}\in E_{R}
, we have

\displaystyle I_{2,1}(Q_{j})

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q_{j})^{d}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}f(z)-f(x_{j})\,d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}+\ell(Q_{j})^{d}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}F(x_{j})-F(z)\,d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}

\displaystyle:=I_{2,1,1}(Q_{j})+I_{2,1,2}(Q_{j}).

Again using that
F
is
C\varepsilon\lambda
Lipschitz, we bound

(9.2)

I_{2,1,2}(Q_{j})\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{q}\ell(Q_{j})^{q+d}.

On the other hand, using that
g
is an Hajłasz upper gradient of
f
, we have

\displaystyle\ell(Q_{j})^{d}\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}f(z)-f(x_{j})\,d\sigma(z)\right|^{q}\lesssim\ell(Q_{j})^{q+d}\left[\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}g(z)\,d\sigma\right)^{q}+g(x_{j})^{q}\right].

By Lebesgue differentiation Theorem and Jensen’s inequality, it is easy to see that
g(x_{j})\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{j})
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}g(z)d\sigma(z)\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}g(z)^{s}\,d\sigma(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{j}).

Since
x_{j}\in E_{R}^{1}
,
\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{j})\lesssim\varepsilon\lambda
. All in all we obtain

(9.3)

I_{2,1,1}(Q_{j})\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{q}\ell(Q_{j})^{q+d}.

We estimate
I_{1}(Q_{j})
. Note that since we chose
s<p
and
f\in M^{1,p}(E)
, then
(f,g)
satisfy a
(1,s)
-Poincaré inequality. This follows from Proposition
4.2
. Furthermore, since also
s<d
, we can apply Theorem
4.7
. By this theorem, since
q<s^{*}
, which hold because
qd/(q+d)<s
, we get that

\displaystyle I_{1}(Q_{j})

\displaystyle\approx\ell(Q_{j})^{d}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{j}}|f(y)-f_{B_{j}}|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)

(9.4)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\lesssim\ell(Q_{j})^{d+q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B_{j}}g(z)^{s}\,d\sigma(z)\right)^{\frac{q}{s}}\lesssim\ell(Q_{j})^{d+q}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{q},

as
x_{j}\in E_{R}^{1}
and
C_{1}\geq 2\Lambda
. Gathering together the bounds (
9.3
), (
9.2
), (
9.1
) and (
9
), we obtian that

(9.5)

\int_{Q_{j}}|h|^{2}\,d\sigma\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{q}\ell(Q_{j})^{d+q}.

Recall that, since
q\geq 2
, then for
a_{j}\geq 0
,

(9.6)

\left(\sum_{j}a_{j}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\leq\sum_{j}a_{j}^{\frac{2}{q}}.

Thus we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{Q\subset R\atop Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\left(\ell(Q)^{-d}\int_{B_{Q}}\left(\frac{|h(x)|}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{q}\,d\sigma(x)\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}|Q|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\subseteq R\atop Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\left(\sum_{Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}\atop Q_{j}\cap B_{Q}\neq\emptyset}\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d+q}}\int_{Q_{j}}|h(x)|^{q}d\sigma(x)\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}|Q|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:estimate-h}}}{{\lesssim}}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\sum_{Q\subseteq R\atop Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\left(\sum_{Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}\atop Q_{j}\cap B_{Q}\neq\emptyset}\left(\frac{\ell(Q_{j})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{d+q}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}|Q|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form20}}}{{\lesssim}}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\sum_{Q\subseteq R\atop Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\sum_{Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}\atop Q_{j}\cap B_{Q}\neq\emptyset}\left(\frac{\ell(Q_{j})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}(d+q)}|Q|

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\sum_{Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}\atop Q_{j}\cap B_{R}\neq\emptyset}\sum_{Q\subseteq R\atop B_{Q}\cap Q_{j}\neq\emptyset,Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset}\frac{\ell(Q_{j})^{2\left(\frac{d}{q}+1\right)}}{\ell(Q)^{2\left(\frac{d}{q}+1\right)-d}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\sum_{Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}\atop Q_{j}\cap B_{R}\neq\emptyset}|Q_{j}|\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}|R|.

The last inequality follows from the fact that
q<\frac{2d}{d-2}
(and recall
d>1
) which implies

2\left(\frac{d}{q}+1\right)-d>2\left(\frac{d(d+2)}{2d}+1\right)-d=2\left(\frac{d}{2}-1+1\right)-d=0

and that, if
B_{Q}\cap Q_{j}\neq\emptyset
and
Q\cap E_{R}\neq\emptyset
, then
\ell(Q)\gtrsim\ell(Q_{j})
, and there can only be boundedly many such cubes
Q
of the same size, so the interior sum on the penultimate line is essentially a geometric series.

Combining these estimates, we get

\int_{E^{1}_{R}}\sum_{\widehat{R}\supseteq Q\ni x}\Omega_{f}^{q}(B_{Q},A_{Q}^{F})^{2}\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}|R|\lesssim(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}|E^{1}_{R}|.

This finishes the proof of Lemma
8.1
.
∎

Part II

Proof of Theorem
B
.

The square function controls the tangential gradient.

10.
Detour on tangential gradients and other preliminaries

We introduce tangential differentiability and the tangential gradient.
Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
d
-rectifiable set. If
x\in E
, recall that the approximate tangent space
T_{x}E
is the
d
-dimensional linear subspace parallel to the approximate tangent to
E
at
x
. The tangent space
T_{x}E
then exists for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
-almost all points in
E
.

Definition 10.1

(Tangential differentiability and tangential gradient)
.

•

We say that
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
tangentially differentiable
with respect to
E
at
x
if the restriction of
f
to
x+T_{x}E
is differentiable at
x
. That is to say, there exists a continuous linear map
d_{x}f:T_{x}E\to{\mathbb{R}}
so that

(10.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to x}\frac{f(x)-f(y)-d_{x}f(y-x)}{|y-x|}=0.

Then
d_{x}f
is uniquely defined by the formula

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(x+tv)-f(x)}{t}=d_{x}f(v)\,\mbox{ if }\,v\in T_{x}E.

•

If
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is
d
-rectifiable,
x\in E
,
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is tangentially differentiable with respect to
E
at
x
, its gradient at
x
is the vector
\nabla_{t}f(x)
characterised by the condition that

(10.2)

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),v\rangle=d_{x}f(v)\mbox{ for each }v\in T_{x}E.

Remark 10.2
.

If
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
is Lipschitz, then, taking a Lipschitz extension
F
of
f
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we define

(10.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}f:=\nabla_{t}F.

This definition is independent of the extension. This follows from
[
Mag12
, Lemma 11.5]
.

The following theorem below is the fact that Rademacher’s theorem holds for Lipschitz functions defined on a
d
-rectifiable subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
d
-rectifiable and
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz. Then
d_{x}f
, and so
\nabla_{t}f
, exists for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
-almost every point in
E
.

A proof can be found in
[
Fed14
, 3.2.19]
,
[
AFP00
, Theorem 2.90]
or
[
Mag12
, Theorem 11.4]
. When taking limits, we will need the following two facts.

Lemma 10.4

(
[
Vil20
, Theorem 1.4(T2)]
)
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
d
-rectifiable and lower
d
-regular with also
\mathcal{H}^{d}(E)<+\infty
. Let
x\in E
be so that
E
has a tangent at
x
, denoted by
V_{x}
. Then

(10.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{B(x,r)\cap E}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,V_{x})}{r}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)=0.

Lemma 10.5

(
[
MT21
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
d
-uniformly
d
-rectifiable, and let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
L
-Lipschitz. Then

(10.5)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{t}f(x)|=\limsup_{y\to x\atop y\in E}\frac{f(y)-f(x)}{|x-y|}\leq L.

10.1.
Some convergence lemmas

Throughout this subsection, assume that
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is Ahlfors
d
-regular. For
x\in E
, set

(10.6)

\displaystyle L_{x}f(y):=f(x)+\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-x\rangle.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
d
-rectifiable and Ahlfors
d
-regular. Suppose that
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
is Lipschitz. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{|f(y)-L_{x}f(y)|}{r}\,d\sigma(y)=0.

Proof.

Denote by
\pi_{x}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to x+T_{x}E
the orthogonal projection onto
x+T_{x}E
. Then we have

\displaystyle|f(x)-L_{x}f(y)|

\displaystyle=|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-x\rangle|

(10.7)

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-\pi_{x}(y)\rangle|+|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),\pi_{x}(y)-x\rangle|.

Since
f
is Lipschitz, say with constant
L
, and
E
is Ahlfors regular, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-\pi_{x}(y)\rangle|}{r}\,d\sigma(y)\lesssim_{L}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,x+T_{x}E)}{r}\,d\sigma(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:beta-tan-0}}}{{\to}}0\,\,\mbox{ as }\,r\to 0.

On the other hand, since
\pi_{x}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we see that for any
\varepsilon>0
, if
r>0
is sufficiently small, then

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),\pi_{x}(y)-x\rangle|}{r}\,d\sigma(y)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}\frac{|f(x)-L_{x}f(\pi_{x}(y))|}{r}\,d\sigma(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:differentiability}}}{{<}}\varepsilon.

∎

Now, recall from Lemma
6.14
that, given a ball
B
centered on
E
, we can find an affine map
A_{B}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
so that

(10.8)

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)

and so that
A_{B}\circ\pi_{B}=A_{B}
, where
\pi_{B}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the plane
P_{B}
infimising
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be Ahlfors
d
-regular and
d
-rectifiable. Let
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
L
-Lipschitz. Then for each ball
B
centered on
E
, we have

|\nabla A_{B}|\lesssim L.

Proof.

Note that, since
A\equiv f_{B}
is affine and has
\nabla f_{B}=0
, then, for
1\leq q<\infty
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\leq\Omega_{f}^{q}(B,f_{B})\leq L.

Fix some numerical constant
c<1
. If
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)>c
, then

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{B}|\leq c^{-1}|\nabla A_{B}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradAB-beta}}}{{\lesssim}}_{c}\gamma_{f}^{q}(B)\lesssim L.

For the case where
\beta_{E}^{d,q}(B)<c
, the proof is as in Lemma
6.18
, and specifically for Case 2 of (
6.30
). Note that there, the Poincaré inequality is only used to bound a term of the form
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}\frac{|f(x)-f_{B}|}{r_{B}}\,d\sigma(x)
, which now can be bounded simply by
L
, since
f
is
L
-Lipschitz. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
d
-rectifiable and lower
d
-regular. Let
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
L
-Lipschitz and
x\in E
so that
T_{x}E
exists. Suppose that
\{Q_{j}\}\subset\mathcal{D}_{E}
is a sequence of cubes so that
x\in Q_{j}
for each
j
and
\ell(Q_{j})\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. Then if
M\geq 1
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
,

(10.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\gamma_{f}^{q}(MB_{Q_{j}})=0.

Proof.

Note that

\gamma_{f}^{q}(MB_{Q_{j}})\leq\gamma_{f}^{q}(MB_{Q_{j}},L_{x}f)
.
Using Lemma
10.6
, it follows that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a
j
sufficiently large so that

\displaystyle\Omega_{f}^{q}(MB_{Q_{j}},L_{x}f)=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{MB_{Q_{j}}}\left(\frac{|f(y)-L_{x}f(y)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)^{q}\,d\sigma(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}<\varepsilon.

This is clear if
q=1
. If
q>1
, note that
|f(y)-L_{x}f(y))|\lesssim_{L}\ell(Q_{j})
. Thus

\Omega^{q}_{f}(MB_{Q_{j}},L_{x}f)^{q}\lesssim\Omega^{1}_{f}(MB_{Q_{j}},L_{x}f)^{q}\lesssim_{L}\varepsilon^{q}.

On the other hand, note that
|\nabla L_{x}f(y)|\lesssim|\nabla_{t}f(x)|\leq L
. To finish the proof of the lemma is then enough to show that

(10.10)

\beta_{E}^{d,q}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 11.74988pt\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0

as
j\to\infty
. To see the first, by
[
Vil20
, Theorem 1.4 (T2))]
(which can be applied because we assume lower regularity), we see that
\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}(MB_{Q_{j}})\leq\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}(MB_{Q_{j}},T_{x}E)\to 0
. Since clearly
\beta_{E}^{d,q}\leq\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}
for any
q\geq 1
, we are done with the decay of the
\beta
-coefficients. On the other hand, that
x+T_{x}E
is the approximate tangent at
x
, implies that
T_{x,r}[\sigma]r^{-d}\rightharpoonup c\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{x+T_{x}E}
(see for example
[
DL08
, Theorem 4.8]
). But that a sequence of Radon measures
\mu_{j}\rightharpoonup\mu
implies that
F_{B}(\mu_{j},\mu)\to 0
for all balls
B
(see
[
Mat95
, Lemma 14.13]
). Hence the weak convergence of
T_{x,r}[\sigma]r^{-d}
to
c\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{x+T_{x}E}
in fact implies that also
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0
.
∎

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be uniformly
d
-rectifiable. Let
M>1
be sufficiently large,
f:E\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
an
L
-Lipschitz and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(E)
. The following holds for
\sigma
-a.e.
x\in Q_{0}
: if
\{Q_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{D}(E)
is a sequence of cubes so that
x\in Q_{j}
for each
j
, and
\ell(Q_{j}^{x})\downarrow 0
, then

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left\|\frac{A_{MB_{Q_{j}^{x}}}-L_{x}f}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(MB_{Q_{j}^{x}})}=0.

In particular,
\nabla A_{MB_{Q_{j}}}\rightarrow\nabla_{t}f(x)
for
\sigma
-a.e.
x\in Q_{0}
.

Recall that
A_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
is the affine map given in Lemma
6.14
. To prove this proposition, we will first need the two lemmas below.

Lemma 10.10
.

Same hypotheses and notation as in Proposition
10.9
. Let
A_{j}^{x}:=A_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
. For
\sigma
-almost all
x\in E
, we have

(10.11)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{z\in B(x,3\ell(Q_{j}))\cap E}\frac{|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}=0.

Proof.

Since
f
is Lipschitz by Lemma
10.7
we have

|\nabla A_{j}^{x}|\lesssim L.

The following estimate is similar to the proof of
[
DS93
, Eq. III (4.4)]
. Set
B_{j}^{x}:=B(x,3\ell(Q_{j}))
. Let
z\in 3B_{j}^{x}\cap E
.
For each
0<\lambda<1
and
y\in B(z,3\lambda\ell(Q_{j}))\cap E=:3\lambda B^{z}_{j}\cap E
, we write

\displaystyle|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|f(z)-f(y)|+|A_{j}^{x}(z)-A_{j}^{x}(y)|+|A_{j}^{x}(y)-f(y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim L\lambda\ell(Q_{j})+|A_{j}^{x}(y)-f(y)|.

Averaging over
3\lambda B_{j}^{z}
, we get

\displaystyle|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|

\displaystyle=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{3\lambda B_{j}^{z}}|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim L\lambda\ell(Q_{j})+\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{d}\ell(Q_{j})^{d}}\int_{3\lambda B_{j}^{z}}|A_{j}^{x}(y)-f(y)|^{q}\,d\sigma(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim L\lambda\ell(Q_{j})+\lambda^{-d/q}\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},A_{j}^{x})\ell(Q_{j}).

If
\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},\ell(Q_{j}))\leq 1
, then we pick
\lambda=\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},\ell(Q_{j}))^{q/(d+q)}
and so we obtain

|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|/\ell(Q_{j})\lesssim\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},A_{j}^{x})^{\frac{q}{d+q}}.

If, on the other hand,
\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},A_{j}^{x})>1
, then

\sup_{z\in 3B_{j}^{x}\cap E}|f(x)-A_{j}^{x}(x)|/\ell(Q_{j})\lesssim 1\lesssim\Omega_{f}^{q}(3B_{j}^{x},A_{j}^{x})^{\frac{q}{d+q}}.

If
M
is sufficiently large, we obtain

(10.12)

\left(|A_{j}^{x}(z)-f(z)|/\ell(Q_{j})\right)^{\frac{d+q}{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ABbeta<gammaAB<gamma}}}{{\lesssim}}\gamma_{f}^{q}(MB_{Q_{j}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-to-0}}}{{\to}}0.

∎

Lemma 10.11
.

Same hypotheses and notation as in Proposition
10.9
. For
x\in Q_{0}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
, set
A_{j}^{x}:=A_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
. For
\sigma
-almost all
x\in E
we have

(10.13)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left|\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x)\right|=0.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{j}:=\mathcal{L}_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
, recalling the notation set in (
6.4
): here
\mathcal{L}_{MB_{Q_{j}}}=c\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{j}}}
, where
c,P_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
minimise
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{j}})
. Set also
P_{j}:=P_{MB_{Q_{j}}}
, and define
P_{j}^{x}
to be the affine
d
-plane parallel to
P_{j}
containing
x
, and
P_{j}^{0}=P_{j}^{x}-x
. Because
E
is uniformly rectifiable,
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, as was established in the proof of Lemma
10.8
. This in particular implies that

(10.14)

\angle(P_{j},x+T_{x}E)=\angle(P_{j}^{0},T_{x}E)\to 0\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ as }\hskip 11.74988ptj\to\infty

Note also that

(10.15)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(P_{j},P_{j}^{x})/\ell(Q_{j})\leq\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0

when
j\to\infty
. This was also established in the proof of Lemma
10.8
. For each
j
, we compute

\displaystyle|(\nabla A_{j}-\nabla_{t}f(x))|

\displaystyle=\sup_{|y|=1}|\langle\nabla A_{j}-\nabla_{t}f(x),y\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{y\in P_{j}^{0}\atop|y|=1}|\langle\nabla A_{j}-\nabla_{t}f(x)),y\rangle|+\sup_{y\in(P_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\atop|y|=1}|\langle\nabla A_{j}-\nabla_{t}f(x)),y\rangle|\right)

(10.16)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}(j)+I_{2}(j).

We will compute the two limits
\lim_{j\to\infty}I_{i}(j),i=1,2
separately.

We start with
I_{1}(j)
.
Let
\phi
be a
C^{\infty}
-bump function that is identically
1
on
{\mathbb{B}}
and
0
outside
2{\mathbb{B}}
and let

\phi_{j}(y)=\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\phi_{j}\left(\frac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right).

Note that
y\mapsto\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x),y\rangle
is Lipschitz with constant depending only on
L
- this can be seen using Lemma
10.7
; in particular the Lipschitz constant is uniform in
j
. Hence

\displaystyle I_{1}(j)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{B}}}|\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x),y\rangle|\phi(y)d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{0}}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x)),(y-x)/\ell(Q_{j})\rangle\phi_{j}(y)d\,\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{x}}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{cL}{\ell(Q_{j})^{d+1}}\int\Phi_{j}^{x}(y)\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{x}}(y),

where

\Phi_{j}^{x}(y):=\frac{1}{cL}\left\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x),y-x\right\rangle\phi\left(\frac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)

and
c\approx 1
is a numerical constant appropriately chosen so that
\Phi_{j}^{x}
is
1
-Lipschitz. That this can be done is easily checked. Moreover, for sufficiently large
M
, it is supported on
MB_{Q_{j}}
. With this in mind, we compute

\displaystyle I_{1}(j)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{L}\ell(Q_{j})^{-d-1}\int\Phi_{j}^{x}(y)\left(d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{x}}-d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}}\right)(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\ell(Q_{j})^{-d-1}\int\Phi_{j}^{x}(y)\left(d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{x}}-d\sigma\right)(y)+\ell(Q_{j})^{-d-1}\int\Phi_{j}^{x}(y)\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle:=I_{1,1}(j)+I_{1,2}(j)+I_{1,3}(j).

We see that

(10.17)

I_{1,1}(j)\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(Q_{j})^{d+1}}F_{MB_{Q_{j}}}(\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}^{x}},\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{j}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form605}}}{{\to}}0\mbox{ as }j\to\infty.

Similarly

(10.18)

I_{1,2}(j)\leq\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{j}})\to 0,

as seen in the proof of Lemma
10.8
. We are left to estimate
I_{1,3}(j)
. We see that

\displaystyle I_{1,3}(j)

\displaystyle=\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\int\ell(Q_{j})^{-1}\left|\left\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x},\,y-x\right\rangle-\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-x\rangle\right|\phi\left(\tfrac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\leq\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\int\ell(Q_{j})^{-1}\left|\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x},y-x\rangle+f(x)-f(y)\right|\phi\left(\tfrac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\int\ell(Q_{j})^{-1}\left|f(y)-L_{x}f(y)\right|\phi\left(\tfrac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)\,d\sigma(y)=:I_{1,3,1}(j)+I_{1,3,2}(j).

By Lemma
10.6
, we immediately see that

(10.19)

\lim_{j\to\infty}I_{1,3,2}(j)=0.

As for
I_{1,3,1}(j)
, we see that
\langle\nabla_{j}^{x},y-x\rangle=A_{j}^{x}(y)-A_{j}^{x}(x)
, and so

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to 0}I_{1,3,1}(j)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\int|f(x)-A_{j}^{x}(x)|/\ell(Q_{j})\,\phi\left(\tfrac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)\,d\sigma(y)

(10.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\lim_{j\to\infty}\ell(Q_{j})^{-d}\int|f(x)-A_{j}^{x}(x)|/\ell(Q_{j})\,\phi\left(\tfrac{y-x}{\ell(Q_{j})}\right)\,d\sigma(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Aj-f-lim}}}{{=}}0

Putting together (
10.17
), (
10.18
), (
10.19
) and (
10.1
), we see that
I_{1}(j)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

Now we focus on
I_{2}(j)
, as defined in (
10.1
). Recall from Lemma
6.14
that
A_{j}\circ\pi_{P_{j}}=A_{j}
, and so
\nabla A_{j}\in P_{j}^{0}
. This and the fact that
\nabla_{t}f\in T_{x}E
imply, for
y\in(P_{j}^{0})^{\perp}
,

\displaystyle|\langle A_{j}(x)-\nabla_{t}f(x),y\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y\rangle|=|\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),\pi_{x}(y)\rangle|\leq|\nabla_{t}f(x)||\pi_{x}(y)|

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{t}f(x)||(\pi_{x}-\pi_{P_{j}^{0}})(y)|,

where here
\pi_{x}
is the orthogonal projection onto
T_{x}E
and
\pi_{P_{j}^{0}}
the orthogonal projection onto
P_{j}^{0}
. Then we see that

\displaystyle I_{2}(j)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{y\in(P_{j}^{0})^{\perp}\atop|y|=1}|\nabla_{t}f(x)|\cdot|(\pi_{x}-\pi_{P_{j}^{0}})(y)|

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\leq|\nabla f(x)|\left(\angle(T_{x}E,P_{j}^{0})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradtf-leq-L}}}{{\leq}}L\left(\angle(T_{x}E,P_{j}^{0})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:angle-to-0}}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

Thus we see that
\lim_{j\to\infty}I_{i}(j)=0
,
i=1,2,
. This proves (
10.13
) and finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Propostion
10.9
.

By (
10.12
), for any
\varepsilon>0
, we can find a
j
so that
\tfrac{|A_{j}^{x}(x)-f(x)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}<\varepsilon
. For this
j
and an arbitrary
y\in MB_{Q_{j}}
, and using the Taylor expansion
A_{j}^{x}(y)=A_{j}^{x}(x)+\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}(x),y-x\rangle
, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{|A_{j}^{x}(y)-L_{x}f(y)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}(x)-\nabla_{t}f(x),x-y\rangle\right|}{\ell(Q_{j})}+\frac{|A_{j}^{x}(x)-f(x)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Ajx-fx}}}{{<}}\varepsilon+\frac{|\langle\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x),x-y\rangle|}{\ell(Q_{j})}

Since
x\in Q_{j}
,
|x-y|\lesssim_{M}\ell(Q_{j}^{x})
. Thus

\displaystyle\frac{|A_{j}(y)-L_{x}f(y)|}{\ell(Q_{j})}\lesssim_{M}\varepsilon+|\nabla A_{j}^{x}-\nabla_{t}f(x)|.

Since this holds for any
y\in MB_{Q_{j}}\cap E
uniformly, we can take the supremum over such
y
’s. Then letting
j\to\infty
, and applying Lemma
10.11
, we conclude the proof of Proposition
10.9
.
∎

11.
The proof of Theorem
11.1
via a good-
\lambda
inequality

Recall that
\mathcal{G}^{1}f\lesssim\mathcal{G}^{q}f
for any
q\geq 1
, and also
\mathcal{G}^{1}f\lesssim\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}f
. Hence, to verify Theorem
B
, it suffices to prove the theorem below.

Theorem 11.1
.

For
1<p<\infty
, let
E\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an uniformly
d
-rectifiable and
f:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Lipschitz. Then,

(11.1)

\|\nabla_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}\lesssim\|\mathcal{G}^{1}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}.

11.1.
First reductions and the proof of Thereom
11.1

Let
M
be a large constant; we will adjust its value as we go along. We denote by
L
the Lipschitz constant of
f
, and we will be careful that our estimates do not depend on it. For
Q\in\mathcal{D}(E)
, let
A_{Q}=A_{MB_{Q}}
, where
A_{MB_{Q}}
is the affine map from Lemma
6.14
. For
\lambda>0
,

(11.2)

\mbox{let }{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}={\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}(f)\mbox{ be a family of maximal cubes }R\in\mathcal{D}\mbox{ such that }|\nabla A_{R}|>\lambda.

Recall from Lemma
10.7
, that
|\nabla A_{R}|\lesssim L
for any
R\in\mathcal{D}(E)
. We conclude that
{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
is well defined. Now set
8
8

8

We will usually write just
E_{\lambda}
because the function
f
will be fixed. We write
E_{\lambda}(f)
whenever we are dealing with two distinct functions.

(11.3)

E_{\lambda}:=E_{\lambda}(f):=\bigcup_{R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}}R.

Let
x\in E
.

•

If there is a cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}(E)
so that
Q\ni x
and
|\nabla A_{Q}|>\lambda
, then
x\in E_{\lambda}
, simply because then either
Q
or an ancestor of
Q
belongs to
{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
.

•

By Lemma
10.11
, it holds for
\sigma
-almost all
x\in E
that, if we have a sequence of cubes
Q_{j}
all containing
x
and such that
\ell(Q_{j})\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, then
\lim_{j\to\infty}|\nabla A_{Q_{j}}(x)-\nabla_{t}f(x)|=0
(whenever
f
is Lipschitz).

•

The previous two bullet points imply that
for
\sigma
-almost every
x\in E
, if
0<\lambda<|\nabla_{t}f(x)|
, then
x\in E_{\lambda}
. In particular

(11.4)

|\nabla_{t}f(x)|\leq\sup\{\lambda>0:x\in E_{\lambda}\}.

Note that
E_{\lambda_{2}}\subseteq E_{\lambda_{1}}
whenever
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}
. Thus, if
t>0
,

(11.5)

\{x:\sup\{\lambda>0:x\in E_{\lambda}\}>t\}\subseteq E_{t}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\int|\nabla_{t}f|^{p}d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}|\{x:|\nabla_{t}f|>t\}t^{p-1}dt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form22}}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\{x:\sup\{\lambda>0:x\in E_{\lambda}\}>t\}t^{p-1}dt

(11.6)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form23}}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|E_{t}|t^{p-1}dt.

So, to prove Theorem
11.1
, it suffices to bound the right hand side in (
11.6
).

Proposition 11.2

(Good-
\lambda
inequality)
.

Hypotheses as in Theorem
11.1
. There is
\theta>0
so that for each
\kappa>1
sufficiently close to 1, there is
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\kappa)>0
small enough
(
depending on
\kappa
)
, such that the following holds. For any
\lambda>0
, set

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}:=G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}(f):=\left\{x\in E\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\right\}.

Then

(11.7)

|E_{\kappa\lambda}\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}|<\theta|E_{\lambda}|.

Proof of Theorem
11.1
.

Let us explain how to finish the proof assuming this lemma. For
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\kappa^{-p}\int_{0}^{\infty}|E_{\lambda}|\lambda^{p-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}|E_{\kappa\lambda}|\lambda^{p-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}(|E_{\kappa\lambda}\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}|+|(G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q})^{c}|)\lambda^{p-1}d\lambda

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Ecap G<E}}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta|E_{\lambda}|\lambda^{p-1}\,d\lambda+\varepsilon^{-p}\|\mathcal{G}_{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}

and so for
\kappa>1
such that, say,
\kappa^{p}-\theta>\tfrac{1}{2}
,

\|\nabla_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form21}}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|E_{\lambda}|\lambda^{p-1}d\lambda\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-p}}{\kappa^{p}-\theta}\|\mathcal{G}_{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}\leq 2\varepsilon^{-p}\|\mathcal{G}_{q}f\|_{L^{p}(E)}^{p}.

This proves the theorem, so now we focus on proving Proposition
11.2
.
∎

12.
The stopping time procedure and the proof of the good-
\lambda
inequality

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
11.2
with Main Lemma
12.3
(see below). We first prove an easy reduction.

Lemma 12.1
.

To prove Proposition
11.2
, we just need to verify (
11.7
) for one value of
\lambda>0
.

Proof.

Suppose we have shown (
11.7
) for some value
\lambda>0
. Now let
\lambda^{\prime}
be any positive number.
Since (
11.7
) holds with
any
Lipschitz function
f
, it also holds for
f
replaced with
g=\frac{f\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}
. Let

A_{Q}^{g}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}A_{Q}
.
Then all our previous results regarding
f
and the affine maps
A_{Q}
also hold for the function
g
and associated affine maps
A_{Q}^{g}
. Thus, if
E_{\lambda}(g)
is constructed the same way as
E_{\lambda}(f)
with
g
in place of
f
and
\lambda^{\prime}
in place of
\lambda
, we have that
E_{\lambda}(g)=E_{\lambda^{\prime}}(f)
. Let

G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}(g)=\{x\in E\,|\,\mathcal{G}^{q}g(x)\leq\varepsilon\lambda\}.

Since
\mathcal{G}^{q}g=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}^{q}f
by Lemma
6.16
, we get that

G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}(g)=G_{\varepsilon\lambda^{\prime}}^{q}(f)

and so

|E_{\kappa\lambda^{\prime}}(f)\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda^{\prime}}^{q}(f)|=|E_{\kappa\lambda}(g)\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}(g)|<\theta|E_{\lambda}(g)|=\theta|E_{\lambda^{\prime}}(f)|.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 12.2
.

We now assume
\lambda>0
is fixed, but we will indicate what value we need it to be later in the proof.

Take
\lambda
fixed as in Remark
12.2
, let
{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
the relative maximal family, as in (
11.2
), and
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
. A further immediate reduction is that it suffices to find a
\theta>0
(not depending on
R
) so that for any
\kappa>0
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that

(12.1)

|E_{\kappa\lambda}\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}\cap R|<\theta|R|.

This inequality will be our goal for the remainder of the section.

12.1.
Definition of the stopping cubes

The proof of (
12.1
) will be done via a stopping time argument. Before defining the stopping criteria, let us spell out some simple facts that will be extensively used later.

•

First, note that

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}}}{{\lesssim}}_{M}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{q}f(x)\leq C(M)\varepsilon\lambda\;\;

(12.2)

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }\;\;Q\subseteq R,\;\;x\in Q\cap G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}\cap R\;\;\mbox{ and }\;R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}.

•

Let
\rho>0
, to be fixed later. Let us say immediately that the
\theta
we are looking for will satisfy
\theta\in(1-\rho,1)
. If
|G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}\cap R|<(1-\rho)|R|
, then (
12.1
) follows immediately, so assume

(12.3)

|G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}\cap R|\geq(1-\rho)|R|.

This is the second fact, which in particular implies that

G_{\varepsilon\lambda}^{q}\neq\emptyset
.

•

Now, since
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
, if
\widehat{R}
is the parent of
R
, we know that
|\nabla A_{\widehat{R}}|\leq\lambda
. Thus, for some
C>0
,

(12.4)

\lambda<|\nabla A_{R}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-gradA}}}{{\leq}}|\nabla A_{\widehat{R}}|+C\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{\widehat{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma<ve}}}{{<}}|\nabla A_{\widehat{R}}|+C\varepsilon\lambda\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda.

And this is the third fact.

We now define the stopping time criteria. Fix
\delta_{0},C_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and set

(12.5)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}:=2\kappa-1>1

and
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
be those maximal cubes
Q
which contain a child
Q^{\prime}
such that either

•

\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subseteq T\subseteq R}\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{T})^{2}\geq C_{0}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}
, call these cubes
\mathsf{BSF}(R)
(‘big square function’), or

•

\angle(P_{Q^{\prime}},P_{R})\geq\delta_{0}
, and call these cubes
\mathsf{BA}(R)
(‘big angle’), or

•

|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|<\alpha_{0}^{-1}|\nabla A_{R}|
or
|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|>\alpha_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|
, call these cubes
\mathsf{BG}(R)
(‘bad gradient’), or

•

\ell(Q^{\prime})<\varepsilon_{0}\ell(R)
, call these cubes
\mathsf{SSL}(R)
(‘small side length’).

Let
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
be those cubes in
R
that are not properly contained in a cube from
\mathsf{Stop}(Q)
. Then
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
is a
stopping-time region
, meaning that is a collection of cubes satisfying the following:

(1)

It has a maximal cube
Q_{\mathsf{Tree}(R)}
containing all cubes from
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. In our case, the maximal cube is just
R
.

(2)

For all
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
and
Q\subseteq T\subseteq Q_{\mathsf{Tree}(R)}
we have
T\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.

(3)

If
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then all siblings of
Q
are in
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.

A few remarks. First, note that every
x\in R
is contained in a cube from
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
, since at the least every
x\in R
is contained in a cube
Q
with
\ell(Q)<\varepsilon_{0}\ell(R)
.
Second, if
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then

(12.6)

\Omega_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{Q})^{2}\leq\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{Q})^{2}\leq\sum_{Q\subseteq T\subseteq R}\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{T})^{2}<C_{0}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2},

(12.7)

\angle(P_{Q},P_{R})<\delta_{0},

and

(12.8)

\alpha_{0}^{-1}\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}}}{{<}}\alpha_{0}^{-1}|\nabla A_{R}|\leq|\nabla A_{Q}|\leq\alpha_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-simLambda}}}{{\leq}}\alpha_{0}(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda\leq 2\lambda.

From (
•
‣
12.1
) and (
12.4
), note that the constant
C
in (
12.8
) depends on
M
. Hence we can choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (depending on
M
and
\alpha_{0}
), so that
\alpha_{0}(1+C\varepsilon)\leq 2
.

12.2.
Packing estimates for the stopping cubes, statement of Main Lemma
12.3

In this subsection, we prove that, with the criteria as above, our algorithm doesn’t stop too often. Most packing estimates will be fairly easy, except the one right below, whose proof will require more work.

Main Lemma 12.3
.

With notation as in Subsection
12.1
and assumptions as in Proposition
11.2
, we have the packing estimate

(12.9)

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)}|Q|\leq\frac{1}{4}|R|.

We now finish the proof of Proposition
11.2
via two further easy lemmas, which will be proven immediately. The last sections of Part II will be devoted to the proof of Main Lemma
12.3
.

Lemma 12.4
.

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}|Q|\leq\frac{1}{4}|R|.

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)
. Then by definition, for each such a
Q
, there is a cube
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Child}(Q)
so that

\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subseteq T\subseteq R}\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{T})^{2}\geq C_{0}(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}.

If we pick
C_{0}
sufficiently large with respect to
M
, this gives us

(\varepsilon\lambda)^{2}\leq C_{0}^{-1}\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subseteq T\subseteq R}\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{T})^{2}<\sum_{T\ni x\atop T\subseteq R}\gamma_{f}^{1}(B_{T})^{2}=\mathcal{G}_{q}f(x)^{2}.

This says that
Q^{\prime}\subseteq R\backslash G_{\varepsilon\lambda}
, and hence, since
E
is Ahlfors regular and the cubes in
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
are disjoint,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}|Q|\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}|Q^{\prime}|\lesssim\left|\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}Q^{\prime}\right|\lesssim|R\backslash G_{\varepsilon\lambda}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:GcapR>1/2R}}}{{\lesssim}}\rho|R|

so that, for
\rho
smaller than some absolute constant,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}|Q|<\frac{1}{4}|R|.

∎

Lemma 12.5
.

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BA}(R)}|Q|\leq\frac{1}{4}|R|.

Proof.

For
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, and assuming
\alpha\leq 2
,

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}}}{{\leq}}\frac{|\nabla A_{R}|}{\lambda}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})

(12.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:AQ<alphaAR}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\alpha|\nabla A_{Q}|}{\lambda}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\gamma(MB_{Q}),

and so for any
T\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
,

(12.11)

\sum_{T\subseteq Q\subseteq R}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}\sum_{T\subseteq Q\subseteq R}\gamma(MB_{Q})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-small}\atop\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}}}{{\lesssim_{C_{0},M}}}\varepsilon^{2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}|Q|

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\int_{R}\sum_{T\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)\atop T\subseteq Q}\mathds{1}_{T}(x)\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\sum_{T\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\atop Q\supseteq T}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\mathds{1}_{T}(x)\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jones-small}}}{{\lesssim_{C_{0},M}}}\varepsilon^{2}\int_{R}\left(\sum_{T\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\mathds{1}_{T}(x)\right)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{0},M}\varepsilon^{2}|R|.

By the results in
[
DS91
]
(specifically the claim at the beginning of Section 14 and equation (14.1)) and the above inequality, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough depending on
\delta_{0}>0,C_{0}
and
M
, we can guarantee that

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BA}(R)}|Q|<\frac{1}{4}|R|.

∎

12.3.
Proof of the good-
\lambda
inequality
11.2

We can now end the proof of Proposition
11.2

assuming Main Lemma
12.3
.

Proof of Proposition
11.2
.

Recall that it suffices to prove (
12.1
). First, gathering Main Lemma
12.3
, Lemma
12.4
and Lemma
12.5
We have that

(12.12)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{BSF}(R)}Q\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{BA}(R)}Q\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)}Q\right)\right|\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)\setminus\mathsf{SSL}(R)}|Q|\leq\frac{3}{4}|R|.

Since our stopping-time region
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, and hence our minimal cubes
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
, depend on the parameter
\varepsilon_{0}
. To highlight this, we denote
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
as
\mathsf{Tree}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)
and also we adopt the notation
\mathsf{BSF}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R),\mathsf{BA}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R),

\mathsf{BG}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)
and
\mathsf{Stop}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)
.

Let
\kappa>1
be sufficiently close to
1
, as in the statement of Proposition
11.2
. We claim the following: suppose that
T\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\kappa\lambda}={\mathscr{C}}_{\kappa\lambda}(f)
(as defined in (
11.2
)), that
T\subseteq R
for some
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
and
\ell(T)>\varepsilon_{0}\ell(R)
. Then
T\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)
. Let us prove this. By definition, if
T\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\kappa\lambda}
then
|\nabla A_{T}|>\kappa\lambda
. But recall from (
12.4
) that
\lambda<|\nabla A_{R}|\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda
. Hence we can estimate
|\nabla A_{T}|
from below as follows:

|\nabla A_{T}|>\kappa\lambda=\kappa\lambda\,\frac{1+C\varepsilon}{1+C\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\kappa}{1+C\varepsilon}|\nabla A_{R}|.

Now, choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small,
\kappa>1
sufficiently close to
1
, and recalling that
\alpha_{0}=2\kappa-1
, we can insure that
\kappa/(1+C\varepsilon)>\alpha_{0}
, and then we obtain

|\nabla A_{T}|>\alpha_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|.

Since
\ell(T)>\varepsilon_{0}\ell(R)
, this implies that
T\subseteq Q
for some
Q\in\mathsf{Stop}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)\setminus\mathsf{SSL}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)
. Moreover, notice that the collections
\mathsf{BSF}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R),\mathsf{BA}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R),
and
\mathsf{BG}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)
increase as
\varepsilon_{0}\rightarrow 0
. Thus,

|E_{\kappa\lambda}\cap R|\leq\left|\bigcup_{\varepsilon_{0}>0}\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)\setminus\mathsf{SSL}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)}Q\right|=\lim_{\varepsilon_{0}\rightarrow 0}\left|\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)\setminus\mathsf{SSL}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(R)}Q\right|\leq\frac{3}{4}|R|

We now pick
\max\{3/4,1-\rho\}<\theta<1
and (
12.1
) is proven. This concludes the proof of Lemma
11.7
assuming Lemma
12.3
, which will be the focus of the next section.

∎

For the rest of the proof, we assume
\varepsilon_{0}
to be fixed and suppress it from the notation for
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
and
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
.

13.
Proof of Main Lemma
12.3
: construction of the approximating Lipschitz graph

We will finish the proof of Theorem
11.1
once we prove Main Lemma
12.3
, that is, once we prove the estimate

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)}|Q|\leq\frac{1}{4}|R|.

In this section we set up the standard scheme that is typically used in stopping time arguments to construct a Lipschitz graph (with small constant) which approximates our set
E
well from the scale of the root cube of a stopping time region (in our case
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
,
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
), down to its minimal cubes (
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
for us).

To the authors’ knowledge, this scheme first appeared in the work of David and Semmes
[
DS91
]
and of Legér
[
Lég99
]
. For a neat presentation in the plane, we refer the reader to
[
Tol14
]
. Our context is closest to that of
[
DS91
]
; for several proofs below we redirect the reader there, in particular to Chapter 8.

Remark 13.1
.

Sice
E
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable, it admits
corona decomposition
in terms of Lipschitz graphs (by
[
DS91
, C4]
). Then constructing a Lipschitz graph seems rather redundant. We will see below, however, that we will need to work
with
this Lipschitz graph, and so using the aforementioned result as a black box would not be possible.

13.1.
Smoothing procedure and Whitney cubes

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, let

d_{R}(x):=d_{\mathsf{Tree}(R)}(x):=\inf_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}(\ell(Q)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q)).

Without loss of generality we might (and will) assume that
P_{R}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
\pi=\pi_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the family of dyadic cubes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Elements of
\mathcal{I}
will generally be denoted using
I,J
. Define

D_{R}(x):=\inf_{y\in\pi^{-1}(x)}d_{R}(y)\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,D_{R}(I):=\inf_{y\in I}D_{S}(y)\mbox{ for }I\in\mathcal{I}.

A fact that will play a role later is that both
d_{R}
and
D_{R}
are
1
-Lipschitz. Furthermore, by
[
DS91
, Lemma 8.21]
,

(13.1)

d_{R}(x)\approx_{M}D_{S}\circ\pi(x)\;\;\mbox{ for all }x\in MB_{R}\cap E.

Remark 13.2
.

Since we will cite
[
DS91
]
quite a bit, we provide a dictionary between their and our own notation. A stopping time region there is denoted by
S
. This corresponds to our
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. Their
Q(S)
(the root, or maximal, cube) is our
R
. They write
L=\diam(Q(S))
(first paragraph, page 43). So, for us,
L\approx\ell(R)
. Their
k_{0}
(
[
DS91
, Proposition 8.2]
) plays the role of our
M
. They define
U_{0}=P\cap B(\pi(x_{0}),2k_{0}L)
(see
[
DS91
, pg. 45, line 7]
), where
P=P_{Q(S)}
. For us
x_{0}
will be
x_{R}
(the center of
R
), which we may assume to be
x_{R}=0
.

Remark 13.3
.

In
[
DS91
, Chapter 8]
they also define
Z
to be the set of points that are contained in infinitely many cubes from
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
(see pg 43, last paragraph). However, we have defined our stopping-time region
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
in such a way that every
x\in R
is contained in a minimal cube, and so
Z=\emptyset
in our situation.

We will also need the following.

Lemma 13.4

(
[
DS91
, Lemma 8.4]
)
.

Let
\pi^{\perp}
denote the projection into
{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
. Then for
x,y\in 2MB_{R}
with
|x-y|\geq 10^{-3}\min\{d_{R}(x),d_{R}(y)\}
,

(13.2)

|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|<2\delta_{0}|\pi(x-y)|.

To prove this lemma, we will need the following easy fact.

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. Then

(13.3)

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}(M^{2}B_{Q},P_{Q})\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}},

where
C
depends only on
C_{0}
,
M
and
d
.

Proof.

Since
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then
\gamma_{f}^{1}(M^{2}B_{Q})<C_{0}\varepsilon\lambda
. This, by definition, implies that

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{Q}|\beta_{E}^{d,q}(M^{2}B_{Q})<C_{0}\varepsilon\lambda.

Now, since
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then
|\nabla A_{Q}|\geq\alpha^{-1}|\nabla A_{R}|
, and since
R\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\lambda}
,
|\nabla A_{R}|>\lambda
. Recalling also that
\alpha\leq 2
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(M^{2}B_{Q})<2C_{0}\varepsilon.

Now, by (
6.10
) and (
6.8
)

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,\infty}(MB_{Q})

\displaystyle\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\lesssim\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,d}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(M^{2}B_{Q})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\lesssim_{M,d,C_{0}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}.

∎

Sketch of the proof of Lemma
13.4
.

In
[
DS91
]
’s proof of this lemma they use the constant
K
in place of
2M
, but it is easily adapted to our case: the proof relies on
[
DS91
, Equation (6.1)]
, but Lemma
13.5
takes care of this. However, for the proof in
[
DS91
]
to work, we need to choose
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\delta_{0}
(the angle parameter for
\mathsf{BA}
) (in fact, we need
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ll\delta_{0}
). In David and Semmes’ proof
\delta_{0}
is in fact
\delta
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{W}_{R}\subset\mathcal{I}
be the maximal dyadic cubes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
so that

\diam(I)\leq\frac{1}{20}D_{R}(I).

We index them with an index set
{\mathscr{I}}={\mathscr{I}}(R)
, so that
\mathcal{W}_{R}=\{I_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}}
.

Lemma 13.6

(
[
DS91
, Lemma 8.7]
)
.

Let
I,J\in\mathcal{W}_{R}
. If
10I\cap 10J\neq\emptyset
, then

(13.4)

C^{-1}\diam(I)\leq\diam(J)\leq C\diam(I),

for some constant
C
independent of
I,J
.

Sketch of the proof.

The lemma follows from the fact that if
10J\cap 10I\neq\emptyset
, then

(13.5)

10\diam(I)\leq D_{R}(y)\leq 60\diam(I)\,\mbox{ for all }y\in 10I.

That (
13.5
) holds is proven in the paragaph below Equation (8.8) in
[
DS91
]
, page 44.
∎

A corollary of Lemma
13.6
is that if
10J\cap 10I\neq\emptyset
, then

(13.6)

\displaystyle\ell(I)\approx\ell(J)\approx D_{R}(y)\,\mbox{ for all }y\in 10I.

We will write

(13.7)

I\sim J\;\;\mbox{ when }\;\;10I\cap 10J\neq\emptyset,\,\mbox{ for }I,J\in\mathcal{W}_{R}.

In
[
DS91
, Chapter 8]
a subset of indices
{\mathscr{I}}_{0}
is introduced (see pg. 45, below Equation (8.9)). We alter its definition slightly to make some estimates more convenient later, but the results stay the same. Note that David and Semmes’
{\mathscr{I}}
corresponds to our
{\mathscr{I}}
and their
{\mathscr{I}}_{0}
to our
{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
. For
K\geq 1
, set

(13.8)

U_{K}:=\pi(2KB_{R})

and define

(13.9)

{\mathscr{I}}_{K}:=\{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\,|\,\mbox{ there is a }\,j\in{\mathscr{I}}\,\mbox{ s.t. }\,I_{i}\sim I_{j}\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,I_{j}\cap U_{K}\neq\emptyset\}.

We have the following lemma.

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
K\geq 1
some constant large enough. Then

(13.10)

\bigcup_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}I_{i}\subseteq V_{K}:=\pi(K^{2}B_{R})\subset B(\pi(x_{R}),K^{2}\ell(R)).

Proof.

We only need to prove the first containment. Let
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
. If
j\in{\mathscr{I}}
is so that
I_{j}\sim I_{i}
and
I_{j}\cap U_{K}
, then

\ell(I_{j})\approx D_{S}(I_{j})\lesssim K\ell(R)

and so

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{i},\pi(R))

\displaystyle\leq\diam 10I_{i}+\diam 3I_{j}+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{j},\pi(R))

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(I_{j})+D_{S}(I_{j})\lesssim K\ell(R)

Now (
13.10
) follows for
K
large enough with respect to the implicit constant, which is dimensional.
∎

Lemma 13.8
.

If
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, there is a cube in
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, denoted by
Q_{j}
, so that

(13.11)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi(Q_{j}),I_{j})\lesssim\ell(I_{j});

(13.12)

\displaystyle\ell(I_{j})\approx\ell(Q_{j}).

The proof of this lemma can be found in
[
DS91
, Equation 8.10]
.
If
j\in{\mathscr{I}}\backslash{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, we will let
Q_{j}=R
.

13.2.
Construction of the approximating Lipschtiz graph and approximating Lipschitz function

13.2.1.
Construction of the Lipschitz graph

Let
\{\phi_{i}\}
be a partition of unity with respect to the
\mathcal{W}_{R}=\{I_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}
. Each
\phi_{i}
will satisfy

(13.13)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{I_{i}}\leq\phi_{i}\leq\mathds{1}_{3I_{i}},

(13.14)

\displaystyle\|{\partial}^{\alpha}\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\ell(I_{i})^{-\alpha},\,\mbox{ for }|\alpha|\leq 2;

(13.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\phi_{i}\equiv 1.

We are ready to define the approximating Lipschitz graph promised at the beginning of this section. For
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
, we let
B_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
be the affine map whose graph is
P_{Q_{i}}
. Now set

(13.16)

H(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}B_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x).

We will denote the graph of
H
by
\Gamma_{R}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
b_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be the affine map
x\mapsto x+B_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{M}(d,n)
. Then set

(13.17)

h(x):=x+H(x)=x+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}B_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x).

where we view
{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
as a subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so
x+H(x)
and
x+B_{i}(x)
make sense
9
9

9

In
[
DS91
]
, our
H
is denoted by
A
, see Equation (8.14).
.
To summarise,

(13.18)

h({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=\{(x,H(x))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}\,|\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\}=\Gamma_{R}.

13.2.2.
H is indeed Lipschitz

We want to show that
H
is in fact Lipschitz, and with small constant. To this aim, we need the following.

Lemma 13.9

(
[
DS91
, Lemma 8.17]
)
.

For
i,j\in{\mathscr{I}}
, if
I_{i}\sim I_{j}
, then

(13.19)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{i},Q_{j})\leq C\diam(I_{i})\,\mbox{ and }

(13.20)

\displaystyle|b_{i}(x)-b_{j}(x)|=|B_{i}(x)-B_{j}(x)|\leq C\varepsilon\ell(I_{i})\,\mbox{ for all }x\in 100I_{i}.

The proof of this lemma is just like in David and Semmes’ monograph, except that we must also treat the case when either
i
or
j
are not in
{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
; even the, the proof is very similar and we omit the details.
Lemma
13.9
is used to prove the following one, which is stated and proved in
[
DS91
]
, between Equations (8.15) and (8.19), page 46.

Lemma 13.10
.

The restriction of
H
to
2I_{j}
,
j\in{\mathscr{I}}
, is
3\delta_{0}
-Lipschitz.

Remark 13.11
.

By the penultimate paragraph of
[
DS91
, p. 47]
, there is
C>0
so that
H
is
C\delta_{0}
-Lipschitz on
U_{0}
. This is where we diverge a bit from their construction: David and Semmes then do a Whitney extension of
H|_{U_{K}}
to get a globally defined function
H
that is still
C\delta_{0}
-Lipschitz. However, we have already defined
H
on
all
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and it can be shown that our extension is globally
C\delta_{0}
-Lipschitz as well. Indeed, in Lemma
13.8
we chose
Q_{i}=Q_{R}
when
i\in{\mathscr{I}}\setminus{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, and that
P_{R}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Hence
B_{i}=0
for
i\in{\mathscr{I}}\setminus{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
. Thus by the definition of
H
and (
13.10
), this means that

(13.21)

H(x)=0\;\;\mbox{ for all }x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash V_{K},

(13.22)

h(x)=x\;\;\mbox{ for all }x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash V_{K}.

The set
V_{K}
was defined in (
13.10
).
Using Pythagoras’ theorem, one can show that
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is
(1+C\delta_{0}^{2})
-bi-Lipschitz and
b_{i}
is
(1+4\delta^{2}_{0})
-bi-Lipschitz. Also, by our construction, we have

Lemma 13.12
.

Notation as above. Then

(13.23)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,\Gamma_{R})\lesssim\varepsilon d_{R}(x)\mbox{ for all }x\in R.

13.2.3.
Construction of the approximating Lipschitz function

Now, define a new function
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
by setting

(13.24)

F(x)=\sum_{i}A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x).

Here
A_{Q_{i}}
are the affine maps
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as in Lemma
6.14
.
Again, because
A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}=A_{R}\circ\pi_{P_{R}}=A_{R}
for
i\in{\mathscr{I}}\backslash{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, we have

(13.25)

F(x)=A_{R}(x)\;\;\mbox{ for all }x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash V_{K}.

13.3.
Some properties of the Whitney cubes

Lemma 13.13
.

For
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(Q)
, let
x_{Q}
be its center and let
I_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{R}
be the cube containing
\pi(x_{Q})
. Then

(13.26)

MB_{Q_{j}}\subseteq M^{2}B_{Q},

where
Q_{j}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
is the Christ-David cube from Lemma
13.8
.

Proof.

Note that necessarily we have
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, since
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(Q)
implies that
Q\subset B_{R}
, and then
\pi(x_{Q})\in U_{0}
, as defined in Lemma
13.7
. First we claim that

(13.27)

d_{R}(x_{Q})\approx\ell(Q).

The upper bound is immediate:

d_{R}(x_{Q})=\inf_{Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},Q^{\prime})+\ell(Q^{\prime})\right)\leq\ell(Q).

We prove the lower bound. Let
T\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
: if
T\supseteq Q
, then
\ell(T)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},T)=\ell(T)\geq\ell(Q)
; otherwise,
T\cap Q=\emptyset
, so in particular,
0.1B_{Q}\cap 0.1B_{T}=\emptyset
. This implies that
\ell(T)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},T)\geq 0.1\ell(Q)
. Infimizing over all such
T
’s gives
d_{R}(x_{Q})\geq 0.1\ell(Q)
, which proves (
13.27
). Recalling that, by hypothesis,
\pi(x_{Q})\in I_{j}
for some
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{0}
, we use this to conclude that

(13.28)

\ell(Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dRapproxellQ}}}{{\approx}}d_{R}(x_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dsimD}}}{{\approx}}D_{R}(\pi(x_{Q}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ellIapproxDRy}}}{{\approx}}\ell(I_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QiIi}}}{{\approx}}\ell(Q_{j}).

To conclude the proof of the lemma we need to estimate
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,Q_{j})
. Note that for
x\in E\cap 2MB_{R}
, if
x^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{R}
is a closest point to
x
, then, recalling that
h
is
(1+C\delta_{0}^{2})
-Lipschitz (and hence
2
-Lipschitz for
\delta_{0}
small enough),

\displaystyle|x-h\circ\pi(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|x-x^{\prime}|+|h\circ\pi(x^{\prime})-h\circ\pi(x)|

(13.29)

\displaystyle\leq 3|x-x^{\prime}|\approx\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,\Gamma_{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:distxGamma}}}{{\lesssim}}\delta_{0}d_{R}(x).

(By definition, if
x^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{R}
, then
h\circ\pi(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime}
). In particular, if
x\in Q
for some
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then

(13.30)

\displaystyle|x-h\circ\pi(x)|\lesssim\delta_{0}\ell(Q).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,Q_{j})

\displaystyle\leq|x_{Q}-x_{Q_{j}}|\leq|h\circ\pi(x_{Q})-a\circ\pi(x_{Q_{j}})|+C\delta_{0}\ell(Q)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form31}}}{{\leq}}|\pi(x_{Q})-\pi(x_{Q_{j}})|+\delta_{0}\ell(Q)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi(x_{Q})\in I_{j}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(I_{j})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{j},\pi(x_{Q_{j}}))+\delta_{0}\ell(Q)

\displaystyle\leq\ell(I_{j})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{j},\pi(Q_{j}))+\ell(Q_{j})+\delta_{0}\ell(Q)

(13.31)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QiIi}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(I_{j})+\ell(Q_{j})+\delta_{0}\ell(Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QsimdsimDsimI_j}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(Q).

So for
M
large enough, (
13.26
) follows.
∎

Lemma 13.14
.

Let

(13.32)

\alpha_{1}=(1-\alpha_{0}^{-1})/2.

For
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)
,

(13.33)

|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{R}|\geq\alpha_{1}\lambda

Proof.

If
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)
, the by definition it has a child
Q^{\prime}
for which either

(a)

|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|>\alpha_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|
, or

(b)

|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|<\alpha_{0}^{-1}|\nabla A_{R}|
.

Consider first case (a). First, from Lemma
6.18
and (
6.27
) it follows that
|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|\lesssim\gamma(MB_{Q})
. Then recall that for if
Q\subset R
,
\gamma(M^{2}B_{Q})\lesssim\varepsilon\lambda
(this is (
•
‣
12.1
)). Thus we get

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{R}|

\displaystyle\geq|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}-\nabla A_{R}|-|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}\atop\eqref{e:gamma<ve}}}{{\geq}}|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|-|\nabla A_{R}|-C\varepsilon\lambda.

Recall also that
\lambda<|\nabla A_{R}|\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda
(by (
12.4
)). Thus, if
\varepsilon>0
is chosen sufficiently small with respect to
\alpha_{0}-1
(and
M
, since
C
here depends on it), we get

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{R}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-simLambda}}}{{\geq}}(\alpha_{0}-1-C\varepsilon)\lambda\geq\frac{(\alpha_{0}-1)\lambda}{2}.

Given that
1-\alpha^{-1}=(\alpha_{0}-1)/\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{0}-1
, we see (
13.33
) holds in this case. In the latter case (b), for
\varepsilon
small enough

\displaystyle|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{R}|

\displaystyle\geq|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}-\nabla A_{R}|-|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-monotone}\atop\eqref{e:gamma<ve}}}{{\geq}}|\nabla A_{R}|-|\nabla A_{Q^{\prime}}|-C\varepsilon\lambda

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-simLambda}}}{{\geq}}(1-\alpha_{0}^{-1}-C\varepsilon)\lambda\geq\frac{(1-\alpha_{0}^{-1})\lambda}{2}

and so have (
13.33
) holds in this case as well.

∎

14.
Proof of Main Lemma
12.3
via a square function estimate

In this section, we prove Lemma
12.3
using the square function estimate below.

Lemma 14.1

(Square function estimate)
.

Let
E,R
be as in Main Lemma
12.3
and
F
be defined as in (
13.24
). Then

(14.1)

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}.

Here
3B_{I}=B(x_{I},3\ell(I))
, and
x_{I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is the center of
I
.

Let us first prove a technical claim.

Lemma 14.2
.

Let
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
and
x\in I_{j}
. For an appropriate choice of
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon

(
depending only on
\alpha_{1}
, as defined (
13.32
)
)
, we have

(14.2)

|\nabla F(x)-\nabla A_{R}(x))|\geq\frac{\alpha_{1}}{4}\lambda.

Proof.

Once again, recall that
F(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)
, where
b_{i}(x)=x+B_{i}
,
B_{i}
is an affine map
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
whose graph is
P_{Q_{i}}
, and that
A_{Q_{i}}
is the map defined in Lemma
6.14
, that is, essentially the affine map that best approximates
f
at
Q_{i}
.

Let
x\in I_{i}
,
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
. If we show that

(14.3)

|\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})(x)-\nabla A_{R}(x)|\geq\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}\lambda,

then we are done, since

\displaystyle|\nabla F(x)-\nabla A_{R}(x))|

\displaystyle\geq|\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})(x)-\nabla A_{R}(x)|-|\nabla F(x)-\nabla A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradF-gradAQi}\atop\eqref{e:gradAQi-gradAR}}}{{\geq}}\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}\lambda-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{4}\lambda=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{4}\lambda.

We focus on proving (
14.3
). We compute

\displaystyle|\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})(x)-\nabla A_{R}(x)|=|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}\cdot\nabla b_{i}-\nabla A_{R}|

\displaystyle=|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{R})\cdot\nabla b_{i}+\nabla A_{R}\cdot(\nabla b_{i}-{\rm Id})|

\displaystyle\geq|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{R}|-|\nabla A_{R}|\cdot|\nabla B_{i}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-gradA}\atop\eqref{e:MBQ_j<M^2BQ}}}{{\geq}}(|\nabla A_{Q}-\nabla A_{R}|-\gamma(M^{2}B_{Q}))-2\delta_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:a<gradAQ-gradAR}\atop\eqref{e:gradA-simLambda},\eqref{e:gamma<ve}}}{{\geq}}\alpha_{1}\lambda-C\varepsilon\lambda-2\delta_{0}(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda\geq\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}\lambda.

∎

Proof of Main Lemma
12.3
.

Note that (
13.28
) and (
13.31
) (and the fact that disjoint cubes
Q
and
T
satisfy
c_{0}B_{Q}\cap c_{0}B_{T}=\emptyset
by the definition of the Christ-David cubes) imply that there are only boundedly many
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)
for which
\pi(x_{Q})\in I_{j}
for any given
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
; that is, if we set
