\mathsf{BG}_{j}(R):=\{Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R):\pi(x_{Q})\in I_{j}\},

then

(14.4)

\#\mathsf{BG}_{j}(R)\lesssim 1\;\;\;\mbox{ for all }j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}.

Conversely, if
Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)
, then
B_{Q}\subset 2KB_{R}
, and therefore
\pi(x_{Q})\in I_{j}
for some
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
.
Note also that
F-A_{R}
is compactly supported, since, by (
13.25
),
F(x)=A_{R}(x)
whenever
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus V_{K}
. Then, using Dorronsoro’s Theorem
1.1
, the affine invariance of
\Omega
(
6.15
) and Lemma
14.1
, we obtain

(14.5)

\|\nabla F-\nabla A_{R})\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Omega_{F-A_{R}}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Omega_{F}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:OmegaF-sim}}}{{\lesssim}}(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}(R)}|Q|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}_{j}(R)}|Q|

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{BG}_{j}(R)}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QsimdsimDsimI_j}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{0}}\#\mathsf{BG}_{j}(R)\ell(I_{j})^{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QtoI_h}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}\int_{I_{j}}dx

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:F-A_R>lambda}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda^{-2}\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}\int_{I_{j}}|\nabla(F(x)-A_{R}(x))|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{-2}\|\nabla F-\nabla A_{R}\|_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form700}}}{{\lesssim}}\delta_{0}^{2}|R|.

For
\delta_{0}>0
sufficiently small, this proves (
12.9
), and finishes the proof of Main Lemma
12.3
, and thus the proof of Theorem
11.1
.
∎

15.
Proof of the square function estimate Lemma
14.1
.

This last section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
14.1
. For reader’s sake, we report here its statement.

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
E,R
be as in Main Lemma
12.3
and
F
be defined as in (
13.24
). Then

(15.1)

\sum_{I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}.

We split the family of dyadic cubes
I
in the sum of (
15.1
) intro three subfamilies; we will prove the estimate above for each one of them.

•

Let
\Delta_{K}(R)=\Delta_{K}
be those dyadic cubes
I
for which
3I\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
.

•

Let
\Delta_{1}\subseteq\Delta_{K}
be those cubes for which
I\subseteq I_{j}
for some
j
.

•

Let
\Delta_{2}\subseteq\Delta_{K}\backslash\Delta_{1}
be those cubes
I
with
\ell(I)\leq\ell(R)
.

•

Let
\Delta_{3}\subseteq\Delta_{K}\backslash(\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2})
be those cubes
I
with
\ell(I)>\ell(R)
.

Over the next few subsections, we will prove

Lemma 15.2
.

(15.2)

\sum_{I\in\Delta_{i}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|R|\;\;\mbox{ for }\;\;i=1,2,3.

Proof of Lemma
14.1
assuming (
15.2
).

Lemma
14.1
follows almost immediately. Indeed, notice that if
I
is a dyadic cube so that
3I\cap 3I_{j}=\emptyset
for all
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, then
F|_{3I}=A_{R}
and so
\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})=0
. Hence, we only need to compute the sum in Lemma
14.1
over cubes
I\in\Delta_{K}
, in which case

\sum_{I}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|=\sum_{I\in\Delta_{K}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{I\in\Delta_{i}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Deltai-bound}}}{{\lesssim}}(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|R|.

∎

15.1.
A technical lemma

In this subsection, we prove the following lemma, which will be useful to estimates the sums in (
15.2
).

Lemma 15.3
.

Let
j\in{\mathscr{I}}
and
x\in 3I_{j}
. We have

(15.3)

|\nabla\left(F-A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}\right)(x)|\lesssim(\delta_{0}+\varepsilon)\lambda.

In particular, if we choose
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon
sufficiently small with respect to
\alpha_{1}=\frac{1-\alpha^{-1}_{0}}{2}
, we obtain

(15.4)

|\nabla\left(F-A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}\right)(x)|\lesssim\frac{\alpha_{1}}{4}\lambda.

Proof.

Since
\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\phi_{i}\equiv 1
, then we can compute

\displaystyle\left|\nabla F(x)-\nabla(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j})\right|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left[\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})\right]\phi_{i}(x)+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)\nabla\phi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt-\nabla\left(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}(x)\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\phi_{i}(x)\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}|\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})-\nabla(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j})|

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\left|\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}(x)\right)\nabla\phi_{i}(x)\right|=:T_{1}+T_{2}.

Note that
\phi_{i}(x)\neq 0
only whenever
3I_{j}\cap 3I_{i}\neq\emptyset
and that, given a fixed
j\in{\mathscr{I}}
, there exists boundedly many other
i
such that
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Hence, to estimate
T_{1}
, it suffices to estimate
|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}-\nabla A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}|
for some
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
with this property. To this aim, we first claim that

(15.5)

\displaystyle\ell(Q_{i})\approx\ell(Q_{j})\,\mbox{ and }

(15.6)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{j},Q_{i})\lesssim\ell(Q_{i}).

The first one is immediate: we know that, since
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
, then
\ell(I_{i})\approx\ell(I_{j})
by (
13.6
) and that
\ell(Q_{i})\approx\ell(I_{i})
whenever
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
by Lemma
13.8
. We have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{i},Q_{i})\leq|x_{Q_{j}}-x_{Q_{i}}|

\displaystyle\leq|x_{Q_{i}}-h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{i}})|+|h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{i}})-h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{j}})|+|h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{j}})-x_{Q_{j}}|

As in (
13.30
), we have
|x_{Q_{i}}-h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{i}})|\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})
(for any
i
). Also, since
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi(Q_{i}),I_{i})\lesssim\ell(I_{i})
for any
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle|h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{i}})-h\circ\pi(x_{Q_{j}})|\lesssim\ell(I_{j})\approx\ell(Q_{j})\approx\ell(Q_{i}).

All in all we see that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{i},Q_{j})\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})
. This implies that, if
Q^{*}\in\mathsf{BG}(R)
is the cube given by Lemma
13.13
(either for
i
or
j
, it doesn’t matter), then
MB_{Q_{i}}\subset 3M^{2}B_{Q^{*}}
and
MB_{Q_{j}}\subset 3M^{2}B_{Q^{*}}
, whenever
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Using (
6.29
), (
6.27
), and (
•
‣
12.1
), we get that

(15.7)

|\nabla A_{Q_{j}}-\nabla A_{Q_{i}}|\leq C\varepsilon\lambda,

whenever
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}
and
C
depends on
M
. Recall also that
|\nabla b_{i}|\leq(1+C\delta_{0}^{2})\lambda
. Finally, it is easy to see from the fact that
Q_{i},Q_{j}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, that

\displaystyle|\nabla b_{i}(x)-\nabla b_{j}(x)|=|\nabla B_{i}(x)-\nabla B_{j}(x)|\lesssim\delta_{0}.

Thus we get (recall
x\in 3I_{j}
):

\displaystyle T_{1}=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\nabla(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i})-\nabla(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j})|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\lesssim\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}\cdot(\nabla b_{i}(x)-\nabla b_{j}(x))|+|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{j}})\cdot\nabla b_{j}(x)|

(15.8)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\leq C\alpha_{0}\lambda\delta_{0}+C\varepsilon\lambda(1+C\delta_{0}^{2}).

Let us now estimate
T_{2}
. We compute

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset}\frac{|A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{j}(x)|}{\ell(I_{j})}

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset}\frac{|A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{j}(x)-A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{j}(x)|}{\ell(I_{j})}=:T_{2,1}+T_{2,2}.

Note that for
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
A_{Q_{i}}x-A_{Q_{i}}y=\nabla A_{Q_{i}}
, so

\displaystyle T_{2,1}\leq\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset}|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}|\frac{|b_{i}(x)-b_{j}(x)|}{\ell(I_{j})}.

Now,

\displaystyle|b_{i}(x)-b_{j}(x)|=|B_{i}(x)-B_{j}(x)|\approx\angle(P_{Q_{i}},P_{Q_{j}})\ell(I_{j})\lesssim\delta_{0}\ell(I_{j}),

since
Q_{i},Q_{j}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. Moreover,
|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}|\leq\alpha_{0}|\nabla A_{R}|\leq\alpha_{0}(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda
from (
12.4
) and again using that
Q_{i}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. We then see that

(15.9)

\displaystyle T_{2,1}\lesssim\delta_{0}\alpha_{0}(1+C\varepsilon)\lambda

We estimate
T_{2,2}
. From (
6.31
) and the fact that
MB_{Q_{i}}\subset M^{2}B_{Q_{j}}
(which can be deduced as in the paragraph above (
15.7
)) and viceversa, we deduce that

\displaystyle|A_{Q_{i}}(x)-A_{Q_{j}}(x)|\lesssim\gamma(M^{2}B_{Q_{j}})\left(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,M^{2}B_{Q_{j}})+\ell(Q_{j})\right)

Now, (
13.12
) tells us that
\ell(Q_{j})\approx\ell(I_{j})
; also recall that since
Q_{j}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
,
\gamma(M^{2}B_{Q_{j}})\lesssim_{M}\varepsilon\lambda
. We finally conclude that

(15.10)

\displaystyle T_{2,2}\lesssim\varepsilon\lambda,

since
|b_{i}|\lesssim 1
for any
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
.
Collecting (
15.1
), (
15.9
) and (
15.10
), we finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

15.2.
Estimates for
\Delta_{1}

Recall that
\Delta_{1}
is the family of those dyadic cubes
I\in\mathcal{I}
so that
3I\cap 3I_{j}
, for some
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
and
I\subset I_{j}
.
Our aim in this subsection is to prove

(15.11)

\sum_{I\in\Delta_{1}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|R|.

Proof of (
15.11
).

We claim that it suffices to prove that for all
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
,

(15.12)

\sum_{I\subseteq I_{i}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|I_{j}|.

Let us see why this claim is valid.
Let
{\mathscr{I}}_{1}
be the set of indexes
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
for which there exists at least one cube
I\in\Delta_{1}
with
I\subset I_{i}
. By definition, if
I\in\Delta_{1}
, then
3I\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
. This implies that if
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{1}
, then there exists a
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
so that
3I_{i}\cap 3I_{j}\neq\emptyset
. By (
13.6
) we have that
\ell(I_{j})\approx\ell(I_{i})
. Then, for
K\geq 1
sufficiently large,
I_{j}\subset V_{K}=\pi(K^{2}B_{R})
(this is (
13.10
)). We conclude that

(15.13)

I_{i}\subseteq\pi(CK^{2}B_{R})\mbox{ whenever }i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{1},

where
C
is come sufficiently large universal constant. We conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\Delta_{1}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{1}}\sum_{I\subseteq I_{i}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:I<I_jsum}}}{{\lesssim}}(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{1}}|I_{i}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form34}}}{{\lesssim}}(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|R|.

This proves (
15.11
), assuming (
15.12
). We now verify this latter inequality.

First, note that for any
i\in{\mathscr{I}}

(15.14)

\|\nabla F-\nabla(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{Q_{i}})\|_{L^{\infty}(3I_{i})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradF-gradAQi<lambdave}}}{{\lesssim}}\delta_{0}\lambda,

since
\varepsilon\ll\delta_{0}
. Further, from Theorem
1.1
it follows easily that

(15.15)

\sum_{I\subset I_{i}}\Omega_{F-A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{Q_{i}}}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim_{d}\|\nabla(F-A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{Q_{i}})\|_{L^{\infty}(3I_{i})}^{2}|I_{i}|.

Then
10
10

10

Recall that
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, that
A_{Q_{i}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
b_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and that both
A_{Q_{i}}
and
b_{i}
are affine, so that their composition is also affine.
, for each
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{I\subseteq I_{i}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:omega-affine-invariant}}}{{=}}\sum_{I\subseteq I_{i}}\Omega_{F-A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{Q_{j}}}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form607}}}{{\lesssim}}|I_{i}|\|\nabla F-\nabla(A_{Q_{j}}\circ b_{Q_{i}})\|_{L^{\infty}(3B_{I_{i}})}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:F-AQjcircBQ_jLinfty}}}{{\lesssim}}(\lambda\delta_{0})^{2}|I_{i}|,

which proves the lemma.
∎

15.3.
Estimates for
\Delta_{2}

The goal of this section is to prove

(15.16)

\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda\delta)^{2}|R|.

15.3.1.
Preliminaries

We establish first some preliminary facts.
Recall that
\Delta_{2}\subset\Delta_{R}
is the family of dyadic cubes
I\in\mathcal{I}
so that
3I\cap 3I_{j}
,
j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
,
\ell(I)\leq\ell(R)
and also
I
is not contained in any
I_{j}
,
j\in{\mathscr{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{W}_{R}
covers
V_{K}
(see (
13.10
)), this implies that given
I\in\Delta_{2}
, there is a
j_{0}\in{\mathscr{I}}
so that
I_{j_{0}}\subset I
. Now let
Q_{I}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
be a cube so that

\ell(Q_{I}^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\pi(Q_{I}^{\prime}))\leq 2D_{R}(I).

That is,
Q_{I}^{\prime}
‘almost-minimises’
D_{R}(I)
. By definition,
\mathcal{W}=\{I_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}
is the maximal family of dyadic cubes in
\mathcal{I}
so that
\diam(I_{i})<\frac{1}{20}D_{R}(I_{i})
. Then, since
I_{j_{0}}\subsetneq I
,
I\in\Delta_{2}
,
\diam(I)\geq\frac{1}{20}D_{R}(I)
.
This implies that

(15.17)

\ell(Q_{I}^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\pi(Q_{I}^{\prime}))\leq 2D_{R}(I)\leq 40\ell(I).

Now we choose
Q_{I}\supseteq Q_{I}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
to be maximal so that
\ell(Q_{I})\leq 40\ell(I)
. Since
\ell(I)\leq\ell(R)
,

(15.18)

\ell(Q_{I})\approx\ell(I).

Our task is to estimate

\displaystyle\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{M}(d,1)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{3B_{I}}\frac{|F(x)-A(x)|}{\ell(I)}\,dx.

To this end, let
A_{I}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be the affine map that minimizes
\Omega_{h}^{1}(C_{1}I)
, where
C_{1}
is a large constant we will pick later. Recall that
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
was defined in (
13.17
), and that it is given by

\displaystyle h(x)=x+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}B_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x),

where
B_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
is the map whose graph is
P_{Q_{i}}
,
Q_{i}
is the cube corresponding to
I_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
,
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
, as found in Lemma
13.8
. Note also that if
i\in{\mathscr{I}}\setminus{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, then
B_{i}\equiv 0
and so
b_{i}(P_{R})=P_{R}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Now let
x\in 3B_{I}
. Then

\displaystyle F(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}}\left(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x))\phi_{i}(x)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{Q_{I}}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x)

(15.19)

\displaystyle=:T_{1}(x)+T_{2}(x)+T_{3}(x).

We will bound the integrals of each of these terms separately to estimate
\Omega_{F}^{1}=\Omega_{F-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}}^{1}
.

15.3.2.
Bounds on
T_{1}

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following estimate on
T_{1}
:

Lemma 15.4
.

For
I\in\Delta_{2}
,

(15.20)

\int_{3B_{I}}|T_{1}(x)|\,dx\lesssim\delta_{0}\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Recall that
3B_{I}=B(x_{I},3\ell(I))
, where
x_{I}
is the center of
I
. For
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
, set
11
11

11

Recall that
b_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the affine map whose image is
P_{Q_{i}}
. Then
b_{i}(x_{I_{i}})
lies on
P_{Q_{i}}
. Recall also that
x_{I_{i}}
is the center of
I_{i}
.

\displaystyle B(i):=B(b_{i}(x_{I_{i}}),10\diam(I_{i}))

and let
\psi_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth cut-off function such that

\displaystyle{\rm spt}(\psi_{i})\subset 2B(i),\hskip 11.74988pt\psi_{i}\equiv 1\mbox{ on }B(i)\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{and }|\nabla\psi_{i}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(I_{i})}.

Set also

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}:=\psi_{i}\left(\phi_{i}\circ\pi\right),

which is easily seen to be
C/\ell(I_{i})
-Lipschitz.

Lemma 15.5
.

Let
I\in\Delta_{2}
. If
C_{2}\geq 3
and
3I_{i}\cap C_{2}I\neq\emptyset
, then for
M
large enough depending on
C_{2}
,

(15.21)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\Psi_{i}\subseteq M^{\frac{1}{2}}B_{Q_{i}}\subseteq MB_{Q_{I}}.

Proof.

For
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, the computation in (
12.2
) together with (
•
‣
12.1
) give
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q})\lesssim\varepsilon
; then

\beta_{E}^{1}(MB_{Q_{i}},P_{Q_{i}})\lesssim\varepsilon
, since
Q_{i}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. By Chebyhev’s inequality there is
\xi\in Q_{i}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\xi,P_{Q_{i}})\lesssim M\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})
. Hence, there is
\zeta\in P_{Q_{i}}
with
|\zeta-\xi|\lesssim M\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})
. So we compute

\displaystyle|b_{i}(x_{I_{i}})-x_{Q_{i}}|

\displaystyle\leq|b(x_{I_{i}})-\xi|+|\xi-x_{Q_{i}}|

(15.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim|b_{i}(x_{I_{i}})-\xi|+\ell(Q_{i})\lesssim|b_{i}(x_{I_{i}})-\zeta|+\ell(Q_{i}).

Now recall that
P_{R}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and that
\angle({\mathbb{R}}^{d},P_{Q_{i}})<\delta_{0}
since
Q_{i}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. This implies that
|b_{i}(x_{I_{i}})-\zeta|\approx|\pi(b_{i}(x_{I_{i}}))-\pi(\zeta)|\approx|x_{I_{i}}-\pi(\zeta)|
. Moreover, since
|\zeta-\xi|\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})
and
\xi\in Q_{i}
, then
|\pi(\zeta)-\pi(x_{Q_{i}})|\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})
. Thus

\displaystyle\eqref{e:form40}\lesssim|x_{I_{i}}-\pi(x_{Q_{i}})|+\ell(Q_{i})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{i},\pi(Q_{i}))+\ell(Q_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:distQjIj}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(Q_{i}),

Recall also that
\diam(I_{i})\approx\ell(I_{i})\approx\ell(Q_{i})
whenever
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
by (
13.12
). Thus
2B(i)\subset M^{\frac{1}{2}}B_{Q_{i}}
whenever
M
is chosen sufficiently large; this proves the first containment in (
15.21
).

Let us prove the second one. Note that since
3I_{i}\cap C_{2}I\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{W}_{R}
covers
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we can find
I_{j}\subseteq C_{2}I
with
I_{i}\sim I_{j}
(recall this notation in (
13.7
)). Thus,

\ell(Q_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QiIi}}}{{\approx}}\ell(I_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I_{j}\sim I_{i}}}{{\approx}}\ell(I_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I_{j}\subset C_{2}I}}{{\lesssim}}C_{2}\ell(I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Q_IsimI}}}{{\approx}}C_{2}\ell(Q_{I}).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi(Q_{i}),\pi(Q_{I}))

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi(Q_{i}),I_{i})+\ell(I_{i})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I_{i},I)+\ell(I)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\pi(Q_{I}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:distQjIj}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(I)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\pi(Q_{I}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QI'distIQI'}}}{{\lesssim}}\ell(I).

The last two inequalities imply

\displaystyle|\pi(x_{Q_{i}})-\pi(x_{Q_{I}})|

\displaystyle\leq\ell(Q_{i})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi(Q_{i}),\pi(Q_{I}))+\ell(Q_{I})

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(I)\approx\ell(Q_{I})

By (
13.2
), we can finally conclude that

\displaystyle|x_{Q_{i}}-x_{Q_{I}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|\pi(x_{Q_{i}})-\pi(x_{Q_{I}})|^{2}+|\pi^{\perp}(x_{Q_{i}})-\pi^{\perp}(x_{Q_{I}})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\pi(x_{Q_{i}})-\pi(x_{Q_{I}})|\lesssim\ell(Q_{I}),

which implies (
15.21
) for
M
large.
∎

Lemma 15.6
.

(15.23)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}\left((A_{Q_{I}}-A_{Q_{i}})\Psi_{i}\right)\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}\frac{|A_{Q_{I}}-f|}{\ell(Q_{i})}d\sigma+\lambda\delta_{0}=:L_{i},

(15.24)

|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}-\nabla A_{Q_{i}}|\lesssim L_{i},

and for all
x
,

(15.25)

|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|\lesssim L_{i}(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q_{i})+\ell(Q_{i})).

Remark 15.7
.

It will come in handy later that, since

(15.26)

G_{i}(x):=\frac{(A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x))\Psi_{i}(x)}{CL_{i}}

is
1
-Lipschitz with support in
2B(i)
, then, if
P_{Q_{i}},c_{Q_{i}}
are a plane and a constant infimising
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{i}})
,

(15.27)

\displaystyle\left|\int G_{i}(x)\,c_{Q_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}(x)-\int G_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)\right|\lesssim\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}.

Proof.

We first prove (
15.24
). To begin with, note that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|A_{Q_{I}}-A_{Q_{i}}|d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|A_{Q_{I}}-f|d\sigma+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|f-A_{Q_{i}}|d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|A_{Q_{I}}-f|d\sigma+\Omega_{f}^{1}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-small}}}{{\lesssim}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|A_{Q_{I}}-f|d\sigma+\delta_{0}\lambda\ell(Q_{i})=L_{i}\ell(Q_{i}).

Once again, we will need the following fact:
since
E
is Ahlfors
d
-regular, there is a constant
c
(depending only on the Ahlfors regularity data of
E
) so that for any ball
B
centered on
E
we can find balls
12
12

12

Not to be confused with the affine maps
B_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n-d}
. The meaning will be clear from the context.

B_{0},...,B_{d}
centered on
E
of radii
c\ell(Q_{i})
so that
2B_{k}\subseteq c_{0}B_{Q_{i}}
and

(15.28)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{B_{k+1}},{\rm span}\{x_{0},...,x_{k}\})\geq 4c\ell(Q_{i}),

where
x_{k}=x_{B_{k}}
is the center of
B_{k}
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality, there are
x_{k}\in B_{k}\cap E
so that if
y_{k}=\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}}(x_{k})
,

(15.29)

|x_{k}-y_{k}|\lesssim_{c}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{i}})M\ell(Q_{i})\lesssim_{M}\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})

and

(15.30)

|A_{Q_{i}}(x_{k})-A_{Q_{I}}(x_{k})|\lesssim L_{i}\ell(Q_{i}).

One may find them as in the proof of Claim
6.19
.
If
\varepsilon>0
is small enough with respect to
c
(so depending only on the Ahlfors regularity data of
E
), we still have that

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{k+1},{\rm span}\{y_{0},...,y_{k}\})\geq c\ell(Q_{i})\,\mbox{ for }\,k=0,...,d-1.

This can be shown using (
13.3
).
In particular, the vectors
\{u_{k}=y_{k}-y_{0}:k=1,...,d\}
form a basis for
P_{Q_{i}}-y_{0}
. Hence, if
v\in B(0,r_{MB_{Q_{I}}})
, we can write
v=\alpha_{1}v_{1}+\alpha_{2}v_{2}
, where
|\alpha_{i}|\lesssim 1
and
v_{1}\in P_{Q_{i}}-y_{0}
and
v_{2}\in P_{Q_{i}}^{\perp}-y_{0}
, and
|v_{i}|\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})
for
i=1,2
. We now compute

\displaystyle\|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}}\|_{{\rm op}}=\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}}|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})v|

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\leq\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap P_{B}-y_{0}}|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})v|+\sup_{v\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap P_{B}^{\perp}-y_{0}}|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})v|.

So, we first estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in P_{Q_{i}}-y_{0}\atop|y|\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})}

\displaystyle|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})v|\lesssim\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})(y_{k}-y_{0})|

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|(A_{Q_{i}}(y_{k})-A_{Q_{i}}(y_{0}))-(A_{Q_{I}}(y_{k})-A_{Q_{I}}(y_{0}))|

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|(A_{Q_{i}}(x_{k})-A_{Q_{i}}(x_{0}))-(A_{Q_{I}}(x_{k})-A_{Q_{I}}(x_{0}))|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt+(|\nabla A_{Q_{i}}|+|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}|)(|x_{k}-y_{k}|+|x_{0}-y_{0}|)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form50}\atop\eqref{e:form51}}}{{\lesssim}}L_{i}\ell(Q_{i})+\lambda\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})\lesssim L_{i}\ell(Q_{i}),

where we used the fact that
|\nabla A_{Q}|\lesssim\lambda
for any
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. On the other hand, if
v\in P_{Q_{i}}^{\perp}-y_{0}
, then
\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}^{\perp}-y_{0}}(v)=0
. Recall also that
\nabla A_{Q_{i}}=\nabla A\cdot\nabla\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}}
. The kernel of
\nabla\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}}
is
P_{Q_{i}}^{\perp}-y_{0}
, and so
\nabla A_{Q_{i}}\equiv 0
on
P_{Q_{i}}^{\perp}-y_{0}
. Hence

\displaystyle|\pi_{P_{Q_{I}}-y_{0}}(v)|

\displaystyle=|\pi_{P_{Q_{I}}-y_{0}}(v)-\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}-y_{0}}(v)|\leq\|\pi_{P_{Q_{I}}-y_{0}}-\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}-y_{0}}\|_{{\rm op}}\cdot|v|

\displaystyle\approx\angle(P_{Q_{I}},P_{Q_{i}})|v|\lesssim\left[\angle(P_{Q_{I}},{\mathbb{R}}^{d})+\angle({\mathbb{R}}^{d},P_{Q_{i}})\right]|v|\lesssim\delta_{0}|v|,

since
Q_{i}
,
Q_{I}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. If moreover
|v|\lesssim\ell(Q_{i})
, we obtain

|(\nabla A_{Q_{i}}-\nabla A_{Q_{I}})v|=|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}v|=|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi_{P_{Q_{I}}-y_{0}}(v)|\lesssim|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}|\delta_{0}\ell(Q_{i}).

Combining the above estimates gives (
15.24
). The proof of (
15.25
) is just like the proof of (
6.31
); we write it for convenience.
We have

Now we prove (
15.23
). Note that

\displaystyle\|(A_{Q_{I}}-A_{Q_{i}})\nabla\Psi_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sup_{x\in 2B(i)}\frac{|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|}{\ell(Q_{i})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:A-A<L}}}{{\lesssim}}L_{i}.

All in all we get

\displaystyle\|\nabla

\displaystyle\left[(A_{Q_{I}}-A_{Q_{i}})\Psi_{i}\right]\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\nabla A_{Q_{I}}-\nabla A_{Q_{i}})\Psi_{i}\|_{\infty}+\|(A_{Q_{I}}-A_{Q_{i}})\nabla\Psi_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim L_{i}

This and (
4.5
) imply (
15.23
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
15.4
.

Recall that we are aiming for a bound on
\int_{3I}|T_{1}(x)|dx
, where
I\in\Delta_{2}\subset\Delta_{R}
and that

T_{1}(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I}\left(A_{Q_{i}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x),

since that
x\in 3I
implies that
\phi_{i}(x)\neq 0
only when
3I_{i}\cap 3I
. Moreover
\angle({\mathbb{R}}^{d},P_{Q_{i}})\leq\delta_{0}
. So we have

\displaystyle\int_{3I}|T_{1}(x)|dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{b_{i}(3I)}|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|(\phi_{i}\circ\pi)(x)\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{b_{i}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}

By (
15.27
), we see that

\displaystyle\int_{3I}|T_{1}(x)|\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}L_{i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma(x):=T_{1,1}+T_{1,2}.

By Lemma
6.4
,
\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(B)\lesssim 1
for any ball. We compute

\displaystyle T_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{i}})L_{i}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}\lesssim\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}L_{i}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:def-Li}}}{{\lesssim}}\delta_{0}\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{Q_{i}}|A_{Q_{I}}-f|d\sigma

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:MBQiMBQI}}}{{\leq}}\delta_{0}\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\Omega_{f}^{1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Thus, since
\Omega_{f}^{1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\leq\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})
, we obtain the required estimate in Lemma
15.4
for the term
T_{1,1}
.
We bound
T_{1,2}
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{1,2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{3I_{i}\cap I\neq\emptyset}\int\left(|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-f|+|f-A_{Q_{i}}(x)|\right)\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:MBQiMBQI}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\left(\int_{MB_{Q_{I}}}|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-f|\phi_{i}\circ\pi(x)d\sigma(x)+\gamma(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{MB_{Q_{I}}}|A_{Q_{I}}(x)-f|\phi_{i}\circ\pi(x)d\sigma(x)+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\gamma(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\gamma(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gamma-small}}}{{\lesssim}}\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}+\varepsilon\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}

This gives (
15.20
) for the term
T_{1,2}
as well, and we are done.
∎

15.3.3.
Bounds on
T_{2}

In this subsection we prove the following estimate.

Lemma 15.8
.

Let
I\in\Delta_{2}
. Then

(15.31)

\int_{3B_{I}}\left|T_{2}(x)\right|\,dx\lesssim\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\lambda\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Recall that

\displaystyle T_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}b_{Q_{I}}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}\atop 3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{i}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}b_{Q_{I}}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x).

We compute

\displaystyle|T_{2}(x)|\leq|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}|\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}|b_{i}(x)-b_{Q_{I}}(x)|\phi_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gradA-simLambda}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}|b_{i}(x)-b_{Q_{I}}(x)|\phi_{i}(x).

Then, setting
b(x):=(b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x))\Psi_{i}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{3B_{I}}|T_{2}(x)|\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{3B_{I}}|b_{i}(x)-b_{Q_{I}}|\phi_{i}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{b_{i}(3B_{I})}|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\phi\circ\pi(x)\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{P_{Q_{i}}}|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int|b(x)|\big(c_{Q_{i}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}-d\sigma\big)(x)+\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int|b(x)|\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{T}_{2,1}+\mathcal{T}_{2,2}.

In order to bound
\mathcal{T}_{2,1}
, we will need the following lemma, similar to Lemma
15.6
.

Lemma 15.9
.

(15.32)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}((b_{i}\circ\pi-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi)\Psi_{i})\lesssim M_{i}:=\varepsilon+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}\frac{|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|}{\ell(Q_{i})}\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x),

(15.33)

|\nabla(b_{i}\circ\pi)-\nabla(b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi)|\lesssim M_{i}

and for all
x
,

(15.34)

|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\lesssim M_{i}(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q_{i})+\ell(Q_{i})).

In particular, for
C
an appropriately chosen dimensional constant,

(15.35)

{\rm Lip}\left(\frac{(b_{i}\circ\pi-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi)\Psi_{i}}{CM_{i}}\right)\leq 1.

Proof.

We start off by showing (
15.33
). If
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
b_{i}\circ\pi(x)\in P_{Q_{i}}
. Also, since
\angle(P_{Q_{i}},{\mathbb{R}}^{d})<\delta_{0}
, then it can be easily seen that

(15.36)

|x-b_{i}\circ\pi(x)|\lesssim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{Q_{i}})\;\;\mbox{ for }\;\;x\in E.

Hence, since
{\rm spt}(\Psi_{i})\subset MB_{Q_{i}}
by (
15.21
),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|x-b_{i}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:MBQiMBQI}}}{{\lesssim}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{MB_{Q_{i}}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{Q_{i}})d\sigma(x)

(15.37)

\displaystyle\lesssim\beta_{\sigma}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jones-small}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})

Thus,

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)\\
\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}\left(|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-x|+|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\right)\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bpi-x<eell}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{Q_{i}}|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)=M_{i}\ell(Q_{i}).

Since
E
is Ahlfors
d
-regular, there is a constant
c
(depending only of the Ahlfors regularity constants of
E)
for which we can find balls
B_{0},...,B_{d}
centered on
E
and of radii
c\ell(Q_{k})
so that
2B_{k}\subseteq c_{0}B_{Q_{i}}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{k+1},{\rm span}\{x_{0},...,x_{k}\})\geq 4c\ell(Q_{i}).

By Chebyshev’s inequality, there are
x_{k}\in B_{k}\cap E
,
k=0,...,d
, so that

(15.38)

|b_{i}\circ\pi(x_{k})-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x_{k})|\lesssim M_{i}\ell(Q_{i}).

Since
x_{k}\in B_{k}
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{k+1}-x_{0},\,{\rm span}\{x_{1}-x_{0},...,x_{k}-x_{0}\})\geq 2c\ell(Q_{i}).

Let
y_{k}^{\prime}=\pi_{P_{Q_{i}}}(x_{k})
and
y_{k}=\pi(x_{k})
. By (
13.3
),
|x_{k}-y_{k}^{\prime}|\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(Q_{i})
. Also, since
Q_{i}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
,
\angle({\mathbb{R}}^{d},P_{Q_{i}})<\delta_{0}
. Thus

(15.39)

\displaystyle|x_{k}-y_{k}|\leq|x_{k}-y_{k}^{\prime}|+|y_{k}^{\prime}-y_{k}|\leq(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}+\delta_{0})\ell(Q_{i})\ll c\ell(Q_{i})

for a sufficiently small choice of
\varepsilon
and
\delta_{0}
. This in particular implies that

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{k+1}-y_{0},\,{\rm span}\{y_{1}-y_{0},...,y_{k}-y_{0}\})\geq c\ell(Q_{i}).

And therefore the vectors
\{u_{k}=y_{k}-y_{0}:k=1,...,d\}
are linearly independent (with good constants), and form a basis for
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Then, with
x\in B(0,2r_{B(i)})
,

(15.40)

\displaystyle|\nabla(b_{i}\circ\pi)(x)-\nabla(b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi)(x)|\lesssim\max_{1\leq k\leq d}|(\nabla b_{i}-\nabla b_{Q_{I}})(y_{0}-y_{k})|.

Since
b_{i}
and
b_{Q_{I}}
are affine,
b_{i}(y_{0})-b_{i}(y_{k})=\nabla b_{i}(y_{0}-y_{k})
, and the same holds for
b_{Q_{I}}
. Thus we get

\displaystyle\eqref{e:form57}\lesssim\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\left(b_{i}(y_{0})-b_{i}(y_{k})\right)+\left(b_{Q_{I}}(y_{0})-b_{Q_{I}}(y_{k})\right)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:form56}}}{{\lesssim}}M_{i}\ell(Q_{i})

This implies that
|\nabla(b_{i}\circ\pi)-\nabla(b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi)|\lesssim M_{i}
and thus proves (
15.33
). The proof of (
15.34
) is just like the proof of (
6.31
).

Finally,

\|\nabla\left((b_{i}\circ\pi-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi_{i})\Psi_{i}\right)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\\
\leq\|\nabla(b_{i}\circ\pi-b_{Q_{I}}\pi_{i}))\Psi_{i}\|_{\infty}+\|(b_{i}\circ\pi-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi_{i})\nabla\Psi_{i}\|_{\infty}\\
\lesssim M_{i}+M_{i}\ell(Q_{i})\cdot\frac{1}{\ell(Q_{i})}\approx M_{i}

This imply (
15.32
).
∎

Estimate for
\mathcal{T}_{2,1}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int|b(x)|\frac{CM_{i}}{CM_{i}}(c_{Q_{i}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P_{Q_{i}}}-d\sigma)(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}M_{i}\alpha_{\sigma}^{d}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}.

By a similar argument as before with the
L_{i}
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}M_{i}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:def-Mi}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{Q_{i}}|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{Q_{i}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{Q_{I}})\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\beta_{\sigma}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}},P_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Since
Q_{I}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then
|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}|\approx\lambda
, by (
12.4
). In particular
\lambda\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\lesssim\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})
. Hence

(15.41)

\mathcal{T}_{2,1}\leq C\varepsilon\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\lambda\beta_{\sigma}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}},P_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}

Estimates for
\mathcal{T}_{2,2}
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{E}|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{E}|b_{i}\circ\pi(x)-x|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma(x)+\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{E}|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{T}_{2,2,1}+\mathcal{T}_{2,2,2}.

Since the supports of
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}
have bounded overlap, so do the supports of
\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}
. Thus we can estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,2,2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\int_{E}|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:MBQiMBQI}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda\int_{MB_{Q_{I}}}|x-b_{Q_{I}}\circ\pi(x)|\Psi_{i}(x)d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\beta_{\sigma}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}},P_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1},

where for the last inequality we can argue as in (
15.36
), recalling also that
Q_{I}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.
Similarly, arguing as in (
15.37
), we obtain

\mathcal{T}_{2,2,1}\lesssim\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\beta_{\sigma}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{i}})\ell(Q_{i})\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jones-small}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}.

This proves that

(15.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,2}\lesssim\lambda\varepsilon\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Finally, putting together the estimates (
15.41
) and (
15.42
), we obtain that

(15.43)

\int_{3B_{I}}|T_{2}(x)|\,dx\lesssim\mathcal{T}_{2,1}+\mathcal{T}_{2,2}\lesssim\varepsilon\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\lambda\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

15.3.4.
Bounds on
T_{3}

In this subsection we prove the following estimate.

Lemma 15.10
.

(15.44)

\int_{3I}\left|T_{3}(x)\right|\lesssim\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\lambda\varepsilon\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}+\Omega_{h}(C_{1}B_{I})\ell(I)^{d+1}.

The term
T_{3}(x)
is given by

\displaystyle T_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\left(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)\right)\phi_{i}(x),

since
{\rm spt}(\phi_{i})\subset 3I_{i}
.
Recall also from the proof of Lemma
15.5
that if
3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset
, then there is
I_{j}\subseteq I
with
I_{i}\sim I_{j}
, so in particular

\ell(I_{i})\approx\ell(I_{j})\leq\ell(I)

and so for
C_{1}
large enough,

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\phi_{i}\subseteq 3I_{i}\subseteq C_{1}B_{I}.

We compute

\displaystyle\int_{3B_{I}}|T_{3}(x)|

\displaystyle=\int_{3B_{I}}\left|\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}(A_{Q_{I}}\circ b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x))\phi_{i}(x)\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{3B_{I}}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}|\nabla A_{Q_{I}}|\cdot|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{I}(x)|\phi_{i}(x)dx

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:AQ<alphaAR}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-A_{I}(x)|dx

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-h(x)|+\lambda\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}|h(x)-A_{I}(x)|dx:=\mathcal{T}_{3,1}+\mathcal{T}_{3,2}.

We estimate
\mathcal{T}_{3,2}
first.
The affine map
A_{I}
was chosen to minimise
\Omega_{h}^{1}(C_{1}B_{I})
. Thus

(15.45)

\mathcal{T}_{3,2}\lesssim\lambda\Omega_{h}(C_{1}B_{I})\ell(I)^{d+1}.

We focus on
\mathcal{T}_{3,1}
. Compute

\displaystyle\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-h(x)|dx

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:def-h}}}{{=}}\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}\left|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}b_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(x)\right|dx

\displaystyle=\int_{C_{1}B_{I}}\left|\sum_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}(b_{Q_{I}}(x)-b_{i}(x))\phi_{i}(x)\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\int|b_{Q_{I}}(x)-b_{i}(x)|\phi_{i}(x)dx.

Note that this latter integral is equal to
\mathcal{T}_{2,2}
, and therefore it can be estimated in the same way. We thus conclude that

(15.46)

\mathcal{T}_{3,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dT22}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda\varepsilon\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}.

Note again that since
Q_{I}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then
|\nabla Q_{I}|\approx\lambda
, and therefore
\lambda\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q_{I}})\lesssim\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})
.
All in all, we obtain

(15.47)

\int_{3B_{I}}|T_{3}(x)|\,dx\lesssim\lambda\Omega_{h}(C_{1}B_{I})\ell(I)^{d+1}+\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}+\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\lambda\varepsilon\ell(Q_{j})^{d+1}

which implies (
15.44
).

15.3.5.
Putting together the estimates for
T_{1},T_{2}
and
T_{3}

We finally combine our estimates (and recall that
\varepsilon\ll\delta_{0}
) to get

\Omega_{F}(3B_{I})\ell(I)^{d+1}\leq\int_{3B_{I}}|F(x)-A_{Q_{I}}\circ A_{I}(x)|dx\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:def-T1T2T3}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int|T_{i}(x)|\,dx\\
\lesssim\lambda\Omega_{h}(C_{1}B_{I})\ell(I)^{d+1}+\delta_{0}\lambda\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\ell(Q_{i})^{d+1}+\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})\ell(Q_{I})^{d+1}

Recalling that
\ell(I)\approx\ell(Q_{I})
and
\ell(Q_{i})\approx\ell(I_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}\Omega_{F}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{I\in{\mathscr{C}}_{2}}\lambda\Omega_{h}(C_{1}I)^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt+\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}(\delta_{0}\lambda)^{2}\left(\sum_{3I_{i}\cap 3I\neq\emptyset}\frac{\ell(I_{i})^{d+1}}{\ell(I)^{d+1}}\right)^{2}|I|

\displaystyle\hskip 23.49976pt\hskip 23.49976pt+\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}\gamma(MB_{Q_{I}})^{2}|I|=:S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}.

First we estimate
S_{1}
. Recall that
h(x)=x+H(x)
and
h
is a
C\delta_{0}
-Lipschitz function supported in
V_{K}
. Since the identity map is affine,
\Omega_{h}^{1}=\Omega_{H}^{1}
, and so by Dorronsoro’s Theorem,

S_{1}=\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}\lambda\Omega_{H}(C_{1}B_{I})^{2}\ell(I)^{d}\lesssim\int|\nabla H|^{2}\,dx\lesssim\delta_{0}^{2}|V_{K}|\approx\delta_{0}^{2}|R|.

Next we estimate
S_{2}
: By Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\delta\lambda)^{2}\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\frac{\ell(I_{i})^{d+2}}{\ell(I)^{d+2}}\ell(I)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\delta_{0}\lambda)^{2}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\ell(I_{i})^{d+2}\sum_{I\in\Delta_{2}\atop 3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset}\ell(I)^{-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\delta_{0}\lambda)^{2}\sum_{3I_{i}\cap C\pi(B_{R})\neq\emptyset}\ell(I_{i})^{d}\lesssim(\delta_{0}\lambda)^{2}\ell(R)^{d}

Here we used the fact that
\ell(I_{i})\lesssim\ell(I)
whenever

(15.48)

3I_{i}\cap C_{1}I\neq\emptyset

and there are boundedly many dyadic cubes of any given side length satisfying this property and so the second sum in the second line is essentially a geometric series. We also used the fact that
I\in\Delta_{2}
implies
3I\cap 3I_{i}
for some
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
, but those were cubes that intersected
U_{K}
and they have side lengths
\lesssim\ell(R)
, and moreover
I\in\Delta_{2}
implies
\ell(I)\leq\ell(R)
, and so any
I_{i}
satisfying (
15.48
) must have
\ell(I_{i})\lesssim\ell(I)\lesssim\ell(R)
and must be contained in
C\pi(B_{R})
for some large enough constant
C
.

Lastly, we handle
S_{3}
. For this, we just observe that, given
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, there can only be boundedly many dyadic cubes
I
for which
Q_{I}=Q
, and so

S_{3}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}\gamma(MB_{Q})^{2}|Q|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|R|.

Combining these estimates together gives (
15.16
).

15.4.
Estimates for
\Delta_{3}

Finally, the goal of this section is to prove

(15.49)

\sum_{I\in\Delta_{3}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim(\lambda_{0}\delta)^{2}|R|.

Recall that
\Delta_{3}\subset\Delta_{K}
is the family of dyadic cubes so that each
I\in\Delta_{3}
has

•

3I\cap 3I_{i}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}
;

•

each
I\in\Delta_{3}
is not contained in any
I_{i}
, for
i\in{\mathscr{I}}
; and

•

\ell(I)>\ell(R)
.

For
I\in\Delta_{3}
, let
I_{i}\in\{I_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathscr{I}}_{K}}
be so that
3I\cap 3I_{i}\neq\emptyset
. We claim that
\ell(I_{i})\lesssim\ell(I)
. Indeed, note that if
I_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
and
I_{i}\cap V_{K}\neq\emptyset
, then

\ell(I_{i})<\frac{1}{20}D_{R}(I_{i})\leq\frac{1}{20}(\ell(R)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\pi(R))\lesssim\ell(R)\lesssim\ell(I).

Clearly then there are only boundedly many cubes
I\in\mathcal{I}
of some given sidelength satisfying this, that is

(15.50)

\displaystyle\#\{I\in\Delta_{3}\,|\,\ell(I)\approx 2^{j}\ell(R)\}\lesssim 1\,\,\mbox{ for all }\,\,j\geq 0.

Recall from (
13.25
), that
F(x)=A_{R}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus V_{K}=K^{2}\pi(B_{R})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{\ell(I)^{d}}\int_{V_{K}}\frac{|F(x)-A_{R}(x)|}{\ell(I)}\,dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\ell(R)^{2d+2}}{\ell(I)^{2d+2}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(M^{2}B_{R})^{2}.

Hence, keeping in mind also (
15.50
),

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\Delta_{3}}\Omega_{F}^{1}(3B_{I})^{2}|I|\lesssim\Omega_{F}^{1}(M^{2}B_{R})\,\ell(R)^{2d+2}\sum_{I\in\Delta_{3}}\ell(I)^{-d-1}\lesssim\Omega_{F}^{1}(M^{2}B_{R})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}.

Since by the way we constructed
F
we have that
\Omega_{F}^{1}(M^{2}B_{R})\lesssim\delta_{0}
, we obtain (
15.49
).

Part III

The proof of Theorem
D
.

Extenstion of boundary Sobolev functions

16.
Preliminaries

This Part is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
D
. We recall some notation and the statement of the theorem for the reader’s sake.
We had

\Gamma(x)=\left\{y\in\Omega:\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot|x-y|<d_{\Omega}(y)\right\}.

For a vector field
F:{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
,
1\leq m<+\infty
, the non-tangential maximal function of
F
is given by

\mathcal{N}(F)(x)=\sup_{y\in\Gamma(x)}|F(y)|.

Recall also the conical square function

\mathcal{S}(F)(x)=\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}|F(y)|^{2}\,\frac{dy}{\delta_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\right)^{1/2},

We defined the corresponding function spaces on
{\Omega}
as

(16.1)

\mathcal{V}_{p}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in L^{p}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

and

\mathcal{V}_{p}^{S}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in L^{p}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\,\mbox{ and }\,\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\nabla^{2}u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

Theorem 16.1
.

Let
d\geq 1
, let
1<p<\infty
and let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
be a corkscrew domain with uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary
{\partial\Omega}
. Then the following holds.

(1)

For any
f\in M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
we can construct an extension
u
of
f
so that
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}^{S}
with the bound

(16.2)

\|{\mathcal{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}+\|\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\,\nabla^{2}u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})},

for any Hajłasz
p
-upper gradient of
f
.

(2)

The trace
\mathcal{T}u
of any
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
is well defined
\sigma
-almost everywhere in the sense of Whitney averages.

(3)

If, on top of the current hypotheses, we assume that
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}({\partial\Omega})
, then

(16.3)

u\mbox{ is Lipschitz on }\overline{{\Omega}},

and

(16.4)

\nabla u\to\nabla_{t}f(x)\,\,\textup{non-tangentially}\,\,{\sigma}\textup{-a.e}.\,\,x\in{\partial\Omega},

where
\nabla_{t}f
stands for the tangential gradient of
f
.

If
\Omega
is a local John domain with constant
\theta>0
and uniformly
d
-rectifiable boundary then the following also holds.

(4)

The trace map
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{V}_{p}({\Omega})\to M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
is well defined with norm bound

(16.5)

\|\mathcal{T}u\|_{\dot{M}^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim_{p,\theta}\|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

(5)

The trace map
\mathcal{T}
is surjective, and given any
f\in M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
there exists a function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
so that
\mathcal{T}u=f
, so, in particular,
u
coincides with
f

\sigma
-a.e. on
\partial\Omega
in the sense of non-tangential convergence of Whitney averages.

16.1.
More on uniformly rectifiable sets

Below, we report well-known geometric facts about uniformly rectifiable sets. They will come in handy later on.

Lemma 16.2
.

Let
{\partial\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
be uniformly
d
-rectifiable. Let
0<\eta\ll\alpha\ll 1
be sufficiently small, and
A\geq 1
sufficiently large. The following holds: for
\sigma
-almost all
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
, there is a cube
R_{\xi}
and an
\alpha
-Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{\xi}
, so that

(1)

\xi\in\Gamma_{\xi}
.

(2)

For all
Q
containing
\xi
and with
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R_{\xi})
we have

(16.6)

b\beta_{{\partial\Omega},\infty}(AQ):=\inf_{P}d_{H}(AB_{Q}\cap{\partial\Omega},AB_{Q}\cap L)<\eta\ell(Q),

where the infimum is over all affine
d
-planes
P
.

(3)

Denoting by
P_{Q}
an infimising plane in (
16.6
), then
\angle(P_{R_{\xi}},P_{Q})<\alpha
for all
Q\ni\xi
,
Q\subset R_{\xi}
.

(4)

For all
Q
containing
\xi
and with
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R_{\xi})
we have

(16.7)

d_{H}(AB_{Q}\cap{\partial\Omega},AB_{Q}\cap\Gamma_{\xi})<\eta\ell(Q)

(5)

We have that

(16.8)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\xi}\cap AB_{R_{\xi}}\subset X(\xi,P_{R_{\xi}},2\alpha,A\ell(R_{\xi})).

(16.9)

\displaystyle{\partial\Omega}\cap AB_{R_{\xi}}\subset X(\xi,P_{R_{\xi}},2\alpha,A\ell(R_{\xi})).

Set

\mathsf{BWGL}(Q_{0}):=\left\{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}(Q_{0})\,|\,b\beta_{{\partial\Omega},\infty}(AQ):=\frac{\inf_{L}d_{H}(AB_{Q}\cap{\partial\Omega},AB_{Q}\cap L)}{\ell(Q)}>\eta\right\},

and

(16.10)

\mathcal{G}(Q_{0}):=\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}(Q_{0})\setminus\mathsf{BWGL}\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ and }\mathcal{G}_{j}(Q_{0}):=\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}(Q_{0})\setminus\mathsf{BWGL}(Q_{0}).

We will often simply write
\mathcal{G}_{j}
in stead of
\mathcal{G}_{j}(Q_{0})
.

16.2.
Whitney regions, well connected components, good corkscrews

Let
{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be an open set. We
consider the following Whitney decomposition of
\Omega
(assuming
\Omega\neq{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
): we have a family
{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
of dyadic cubes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with disjoint interiors such that

\bigcup_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)}I=\Omega,

and moreover there are
some constants
\Lambda>20
and
D_{0}\geq 1
such the following holds for every
I\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
:

(1)

10\cdot I\subset\Omega
;

(2)

\Lambda\cdot I\cap\partial\Omega\neq\varnothing
;

(3)

there are at most
D_{0}
cubes
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)

such that
10I\cap 10J\neq\varnothing
. Further, for such cubes
J
, we have
\frac{1}{2}\ell(J)\leq\ell(I)\leq 2\ell(J)
.

From the properties (1) and (2) it is clear that

(16.11)

10\ell(I)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(I,\partial\Omega)\leq\Lambda\ell(I).

The arguments to construct a Whitney decomposition satisfying the properties above are
standard.

Remark 16.3
.

In general, we will denote Whitney cubes by
I,J\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})
and Christ-David cubes by
Q,P,R\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
.

Let
I\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
. Let
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
be a closest point to
I
, that is, a point satisfying
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}({\partial\Omega},I)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\xi,I)\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}({\partial\Omega},I)
. Then
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\xi,I)\approx_{\Lambda}\ell(I)
. But also
\xi\in Q
for some cube with
\ell(I)=\ell(Q)
. This cube, which we denote by
Q_{I}
, will have the property that

(16.12)

\ell(Q_{I})=\ell(I)\mbox{ and }10\ell(I)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{I},P)\leq\Lambda\ell(I).

Conversely, for some
0<\tau<1
, and
given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\sigma}
, we let

(16.13)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\tau}(Q):=\{P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)\,|\,

\displaystyle\tau\cdot\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)\leq\ell(Q)\,

(16.14)

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\tau\cdot\ell(Q)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,P)\leq\Lambda\ell(Q)\},

and

(16.15)

\widetilde{W}_{\tau}(Q)=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{W}_{c}(Q)}P.

We will most often suppress the dependence on
\tau
in the notation and simply write
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}(Q)
or
\widetilde{W}(Q)
.

Definition 16.4

(
Well-connected components
)
.

Note that
\widetilde{W}(Q)
might consist of more than just one (quantitatively) connected component. More precisely, we decompose
\widetilde{W}(Q)
as follows: we say that a subset
v_{Q}\subset\widetilde{W}(Q)
is a
well-connected component
if any two points
x,y\in v_{Q}
can be joined by a
\theta
-cigar curve. Denote by
M_{Q}
the number of distinct well-connected components of
\widetilde{W}(Q)
. We will use the notation
\{v^{i}_{Q}\}_{i=1}^{M_{Q}}
. We might also abuse notation and denote by
\mathcal{W}(Q)=\{v_{Q}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{M_{Q}}
.

Remark 16.5
.

Note also that if
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\setminus\mathsf{BWGL}
, then there are at most two well-connected components
v_{Q}^{1},v_{Q}^{2}\subset\widetilde{W}(Q)
. Indeed, if
P_{Q}
is the plane minimising
b\beta_{{\partial\Omega},\infty}(AQ)
, then
\widetilde{W}(Q)\subset B_{Q}\setminus P_{Q}(\eta\ell(Q))
. Denoting by
D^{+}
and
D^{-}
the connected components of
B_{Q}\setminus P_{Q}(\eta\ell(Q))
, we see that if
v_{Q}^{1},v_{Q}^{2}\subset D^{+}
, say, are two well-connected components of
\widetilde{W}(Q)
, then we might join each pair
x_{1}\in v_{Q}^{1},x_{2}\in v_{Q}^{2}
with a
\theta
-cigar. Hence
v_{Q}^{1}=v_{Q}^{2}
. Thus, if
Q\notin\mathsf{BWGL}
, we might have at most two well-connected components.

Lemma 16.6
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
be a local John domain with constant
\theta
.

•

Choosing
0<\tau<1
in (
16.15
) appropriately depending on
\theta
, for each
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, we can ensure that
\widetilde{W}(Q)=\widetilde{W}_{\tau}(Q)\neq\varnothing
and that there is at least one cube
I\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}(Q)
so that
I\subset B^{Q}
.

•

Furthermore, to any corkscrew ball
B^{Q}\subset B_{Q}\cap{\Omega}
of radius at least
\theta\ell(Q)
, there is a well connected component
v_{Q}
such that
0.5B^{Q}\subset v_{Q}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from definitions: let
\tau=\theta/10
; if
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
then we know that there is a corkscrew ball
B^{Q}\subset B_{Q}\cap{\Omega}
with radius
\geq\theta\ell(Q)
. Then
x_{B^{Q}}
will be contained in a dyadic cube
I
with sidelength
\sim\theta/5
. Then
I
will satisfy both (
16.13
) and (
16.14
). The second part of the lemma is similarly immediate.
∎

Definition 16.7

(
Good corkscrews
)
.

Given
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, we say that a corkscrew ball
B^{Q}\subset B_{Q}\cap{\Omega}
with radius
\geq\theta\ell(Q)
is a
good corkscrew ball
if for each
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}\cap B_{Q}
, there is a
\theta
-carrot path connecting
x_{B^{Q}}
to
\xi
. If
{\Omega}
is a local John domain, we are always guaranteed the presence of at least one good corkscrew ball.

Definition 16.8

(
Good well-connected component
)
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
and let
B^{Q}
be a good corkscrew ball. We call the well connected component
v_{Q}\subset\widetilde{W}(Q)
which contains
0.5B^{Q}
a
good
well connected component. Of course, there might be multiple good well connected component for one
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Abusing notation, for each
Q
, we denote both the union and the family of good well connected components by
W(Q)
; the family of Whitney cubes constituting good well connected components will be denoted by
\mathcal{W}(Q)
.

16.3.
Compatible choices

What we would like to do now, is to set

(16.16)

u_{Q}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B^{Q}}u(x)\,dx,

and then define
f
as the limit of these averages when
\ell(Q)\to 0
. However, care must be taken when choosing
which good corkscrew ball
to use when taking the average. We need some compatibility in this choice. Lemma
16.9
below gives us this correct choice.

Lemma 16.9
.

For each
j\geq 1
, there exists a choice of good corkscrew balls
\{B^{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}}
such that, for each pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}
, if
R\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,i}
,
i\leq j
, is the minimal cube satisfying
\xi_{Q},\xi_{Q^{\prime}}\in 3B_{R}
, then we can find carrot paths joining
x_{B^{Q}},x_{B^{Q^{\prime}}}
to the center of a (common) good corkscrew ball in
3B_{R}
of radius
3\theta\ell(R)
.

Remark 16.10
.

We call a family of good corkscrew ball
\{B^{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{G}_{j}}
which satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
16.9
a
compatible choice
of corkscrew balls. Of course, for each
Q
there might be (uncountably) many corkscrew balls that form a compatible choice.

Proof of Lemma
16.9
.

Suppose the lemma is false. Then for some
j\geq 1
, we can find a pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}
, such that no choice of good corkscrew balls
B^{Q},B^{Q^{\prime}}
can be made so that both
B^{Q}
and
B^{Q^{\prime}}
are connected via carrot path to one good corkscrew ball in
3B_{R}
of radius at least
3\theta\ell(R)
. But consider
\xi_{R}
, the center of
R
. Then, by the local John condition, we can find a good corkscrew ball, denoted by
B^{3R}
, with
B^{3R}\subset B(\xi_{R},3\ell(R))\cap\Omega
, and with radius
r(B^{3R})=3\theta\ell(R)
. In particular, there is a
\theta
-carrot path connecting
x_{B^{3R}}
(the center of
B^{3R}
) to both
\xi_{Q}
and
\xi_{Q^{\prime}}
. Each of these carrot paths provide us with a corkscrew balls
c(Q)\subset B(\xi_{Q},\ell(Q))\cap\Omega
,
c(Q^{\prime})\subset B(\xi_{Q^{\prime}},\ell(Q))\cap{\Omega}
of radius
\theta\ell(Q)
. It is not necessarily true, however, that both
c(Q)
and
c(Q^{\prime})
are
good
corkscrew balls. If both of them are, then we are done. We denote by
D^{+},D^{-}
the two connected component of
AB_{Q}\setminus P_{Q}(\eta\ell(Q))
. Note that since
Q\in\mathsf{BWGL}(Q_{0})
,
D^{+}\cup D^{-}\cap{\partial\Omega}=\varnothing
. Assume without loss of generality that
c(Q)\subset D^{+}
and suppose
c(Q)
is not a good corkscrew ball. This means that there is a point
\zeta_{Q}\in B(\xi_{Q},\ell(Q))\cap{\partial\Omega}
which cannot be connected to
x_{C(Q)}
, the center of
C(Q)
, by a carrot path. Note that, then,
\zeta_{Q}
cannot be connected to
x_{B^{3R}}
either, at least via a carrot path that passes through
D^{+}
. Indeed, suppose that this could be done: we can find a carrot path joining
x_{B^{3R}}
to
\zeta_{Q}
. But then there is a corkscrew ball, denoted by
B_{\zeta_{Q}}
and of radius
\theta\ell(Q)
, which is contained in
2B_{Q}\cap{\Omega}\cap D^{+}
and whose center is connected with a carrot path to
\zeta_{Q}
. Hence both
B_{\zeta_{Q}}\subset D^{+}
ans
C(Q)\subset D^{+}
. But this implies that we can join
B_{\zeta_{Q}}
and
C(Q)
with a cigar curve, and thus construct a carrot path joining
x_{C(Q)}
to
\zeta_{Q}
, and this lead to a contradiction. Hence there cannot be a carrot path passing through
D^{+}
which joins
x_{B^{3R}}
to
\zeta_{Q}
. But since
B^{3R}
is a good corkscrew ball, we conclude that there is a carrot path joining
x_{B^{3R}}
to
\zeta_{R}
passing through
D^{-}
, as there is no other option. Note at this point that there must be a good corkscrew ball
C^{\prime}(Q)\subset D^{-}
, by the local John condition. This ball can be joined with a cigar curve to the corkscrew ball contained in the carrot path joining
x_{B^{3R}}
to
\zeta_{Q}
, by the fact that
b\beta_{{\partial\Omega},\infty}(AQ)<\eta
. We conclude that we can join the good corkscrew ball
C^{\prime}(Q)
to
B^{3R}
with a carrot path. We now repeat the same argument for
Q^{\prime}
and conclude that we can choose two good corkscrew balls
B^{Q}
and
B^{Q^{\prime}}
to a common good corkscrew ball
B^{3R}
via carrot path. This contradicts the assumption made at the beginning of the proof, and the lemma is proven.
∎

Remark 16.11
.

Given
Q\in\mathcal{G}_{j}
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(Q)
be the family of corkscrew balls that belong to at least a compatible choice (as in Remark
16.10
). For each good well connected component
v_{Q}
, we pick one good corkscrew ball
B^{Q}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(Q)
(where the relation between
v_{Q}
and
B^{Q}
is as in Lemma
16.6
). We call this subfamily
\mathcal{B}(Q)=\{B^{Q}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{Q}}
. Note that
N_{Q}:=\#\mathcal{B}(Q)\leq 2
by Remark
16.5
For each
Q\in\mathcal{G}
, we also define the family of good well connected components which contains (half of) a corkscrew ball from
\mathcal{B}(Q)
. That is

(16.17)

\mathsf{U}(Q):=\{v_{Q}\,|\,v_{Q}\in W(Q)\mbox{ and there is }B^{Q}\in\mathcal{B}(Q)\mbox{ s.t. }0.5B^{Q}\subset v_{Q}\}.

16.4.
Non-tangential cones and the spaces
\mathcal{V}_{p,\lambda}
; discrete non-tangential cones and the space
\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}
; truncated non-tangential cones

For technical reasons, we introduce some variants of the non-tangential cone and of the spaces defined in the introduction and recalled at the beginning og this Part III. For a parameter
\lambda>0
and
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
, set

\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi)=\left\{y\in\Omega\,|\,\lambda\cdot|\xi-y|<d_{\Omega}(y)\right\}.

Before, we had that
\Gamma(\xi)=\Gamma^{1/2}(\xi)
.
For a vector field
F:{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
,
1\leq m<+\infty
, define the corresponding non-tangential maximal function as

\mathcal{N}^{\lambda}(F)(\xi)=\sup_{y\in\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi)}|F(y)|.

Then set

\mathcal{V}_{p,\lambda}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in L^{p}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}^{\lambda}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\},

and

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(u)(x)=\left(\int_{\Gamma^{\lambda}(x)}|u(y)|^{2}\,\frac{dy}{d_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\right)^{1/2},

together with the corresponding space

\mathcal{V}_{p,\lambda}^{S}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in C^{2}({\Omega})\,|\,\mathcal{N}^{\lambda}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\,\mbox{ and }\,\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\nabla^{2}u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

Lemma 16.12
.

For any
\lambda>0
,
\mathcal{V}_{p,\lambda}=\mathcal{V}_{p,1/2}=\mathcal{V}_{p}
.

Proof.

That
\mathcal{V}_{p}=\mathcal{V}_{p,\frac{1}{2}}
is by definition (recall (
16.1
)). That
\mathcal{V}_{p,\alpha}=\mathcal{V}_{p,\tfrac{1}{2}}
follows from the fact that
\|N_{\alpha}(\nabla u)\|_{p}\approx_{\alpha,\beta}\|N_{\beta}(\nabla u)\|_{p}
for any
\alpha,\beta>0
. This follows using the Alhfors regularity of the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
and the classical proof for
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}_{+}
by Fefferman and Stein in
[
FS72
, Lemma 1]
(but see also
[
HT20
, Lemma 1.27]
).
∎

16.4.1.
Discrete versions

We will also need a "discretised" version of the non-tangential regions
\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi)
; the definition is as in (
16.20
), except that we take the union over all cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
containing
\xi
. More precisely, we set

(16.18)

\mathbb{Y}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}(\xi):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\atop Q\ni\xi}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\\
\ell(P)=\ell(Q)\\
\lambda P\ni\xi\end{subarray}}\widetilde{W}(Q).

Remark that this definition depends on
\lambda>0
, but also on
\tau>0
in the definition of
\widetilde{W}(Q)
in (
16.15
). Define the corresponding maximal function

N_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}(F)(\xi):=\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{Y}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}(y)}|F(y)|.

and the corresponding space

\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}:=\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}({\Omega}):=\left\{u\in L^{p}({\Omega})\,|\,N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}^{\lambda}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})\right\}.

Note in passing, that if
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}
, then
N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}^{\lambda_{1}}(F)(\xi)\leq N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}^{\lambda_{2}}(F)(\xi)
. This fact will be used below without explicit mention.

Lemma 16.13
.

For any
\lambda>0
,
\mathcal{V}_{p,\lambda}=\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}
.

The proof is as in
[
HT20
]
, Lemma 1.27. The lemma there is stated for continuous functions
u
, but it in fact holds more generally for measurable functions; see, for example,
[
MT21
]
, equations (1.7) and (1.8). Remark also that in
[
HT20
]
the authors use a different but equivalent definition of "discretised" non-tangential region; compare Definition 1.25 in
[
HT20
]
to our own (
16.18
).

Remark 16.14
.

Lemmas
16.12
and
16.13
together, tell us that to prove Theorem
16.1
(1) and (2), it suffices to prove it for
u\in\mathbb{V}^{\lambda}
, for some
\lambda>0
.

16.4.2.
Truncated versions

Given
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
16.2
and
R_{\xi}
as in its statement, and for
\lambda>0
, set

(16.19)

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}(\xi):=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}(R_{\xi})\,|\,\lambda Q\ni\xi\}.

Define also

(16.20)

\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}(\xi):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}(\xi)}\widetilde{W}(Q).

For simplicity, we will write
\mathcal{T}(\xi)=\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}(\xi)
and
\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}(\xi)=\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}(\xi)
.

Remark 16.15
.

Keep the hypotheses of Lemma
16.2
. For almost all
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
, we have the following construction. Denote by
H^{+}
and
H^{-}
the two connected components of
B(\xi,A\ell(R_{\xi}))\setminus X(\xi,P_{R_{\xi}},2\alpha,A\ell(R_{\xi}))
. Then clearly, either
H^{+}\subset{\Omega}
, or
H^{-}\subset{\Omega}
, or both.
If we choose
\alpha
sufficiently small with respect to
\theta
, we see that for each
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
, and for all
v_{Q}\in\widetilde{W}(Q)
,
v_{Q}\subset H^{+}\cup H^{-}
. We set

(16.21)

\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\pm}(\xi):=\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}(\xi)\cap H^{\pm}.

Note that for any pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
, if
B^{Q},B^{Q^{\prime}}
are two corkscrew balls of radius
\theta\ell(Q)
and
\theta\ell(Q^{\prime})
, respectively, and they are contained in the same connected component, say
H^{+}
, then we may connect
x_{B^{Q^{\prime}}}
to
x_{B^{Q}}
with a
\theta
-carrot path.

17.
Almost everywhere existence of the trace and pointwise Sobolev estimates

17.1.
Definition and convergence of trace

Fix
\lambda=3
.

Remark 17.1
.

Because we have fixed
\lambda
, and for notational convenience, in this section we hide the dependence of
\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}
,
N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}^{\lambda}(\nabla u)
,
\mathcal{T}^{\lambda}(\xi)
and
\mathbb{Y}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}^{\lambda}
on
\lambda
, hence simply writing
\mathbb{V}
,
N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)
,
\mathcal{T}(\xi)
and
\mathbb{Y}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{V}
.
For
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, set

\Phi(Q):=\frac{1}{N_{Q}}\sum_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}(Q)}u_{v_{Q}}

(We remind that the relevant notation was set in Remark
16.4
, Definition
16.8
and (
16.17
)).

Lemma 17.2
.

For all those
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
16.2
(in particular, for
\sigma
-almost all
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
),

(17.1)

\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)\,\mbox{ exists, }

perhaps up to a subsequence. If it exists up to a subsequence, then there are exactly two subsequences, both of which converge.

Remark 17.3
.

Lemma
17.2
corresponds to Theorem
16.1
(2).

Proof.

Let
\xi
be a point in
{\partial\Omega}
so that the conclusions of Lemma
16.2
are satisfied.
We distinguish between two distinct cases:

(1)

There is a
Q^{*}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
so that for all
Q\subset Q^{*}
in
\mathcal{T}(\xi)
,
N_{Q}=1
. Recalling Remark
16.15
, within this case we consider two subcases:

(a)

Either there is a
Q^{**}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
with
Q^{**}\subset Q^{*}
so that each
Q\subset Q^{**}
containing
\xi
is so that
0.5B^{Q}\subset H^{+}
, where
B^{Q}
is the the good corkscrew ball
B^{Q}\in\mathcal{B}(Q)
, as chosen in Remark
16.11
; or the same happens, with
H^{-}
instead of
H^{+}
.

(b)

No such
Q^{**}
exists.

(2)

There is no such
Q^{*}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
. In particular, there is a sequence of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
whose side length converges to
0
, such that
N_{Q}=2
.

Case 1(a).
We will show that in this case, the limit in (
17.1
) exists (without having to resort to subsequences). Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and let
P\subset Q^{**}
be so that
\ell(P)<C\varepsilon
, for some
C
to be determined below. Let
S,S^{\prime}
be two cubes in
\mathcal{T}(\xi)
contained in
P
and with
\ell(S^{\prime})<\ell(S)
. Note that since we are in the current case, we have that

\Phi(S)=u_{v_{S}},\,\mbox{ and }\,\Phi(S^{\prime})=u_{v_{S^{\prime}}}.

Moreover, we can connect
x_{B^{S}}
to
x_{B^{S^{\prime}}}
with a
\theta
-carrot path, which we denote by
\gamma(S,S^{\prime})
. Note also that by definition, any point in
v_{S}
can be connected to
x_{B^{S}}
by a rectifiable path of length
\lesssim\ell(S)
. The same can be said with
S^{\prime}
replacing
S
. Denoting
z=x_{B^{S^{\prime}}}
to ease notation, we write

|\Phi(S)-\Phi(S^{\prime})|=|u_{v_{S}}-u_{v_{S^{\prime}}}|\leq|u_{v_{S}}-u(z)|+|u(z)-u_{v_{S^{\prime}}}|=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

By the paragraph above the latest display, we see that there is a rectifiable path
\gamma_{y,z}
of length
\lesssim\ell(S)
connecting any point
y\in v_{S}
to
z=x_{B^{S^{\prime}}}
. Hence we may estimate

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{S}}|u(y)-u(z)|\,dy\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{S}}\int_{\gamma(y,z)}\nabla u(s)\,ds\,dy

(17.2)

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{S}}\int_{\gamma(y,z)}\sup_{x\in\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}(\xi)}\nabla u(x)\,ds\,dy\lesssim\ell(S)N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi).

Since
\ell(S)\leq\ell(P)\leq C\varepsilon
, choosing
C
appropriately (depending only on
\theta
), gives
I_{1}<\varepsilon/2
. The same estimate is obtained for
I_{2}
taking into account that
z\in v_{S^{\prime}}
,
v_{S^{\prime}}\subset\overline{\mathbb{Y}}_{{\text{\tiny{W}}}}(\xi)
and that
\ell(S^{\prime})<\ell(S)
. This shows that if we are in Case 1(a), the sequence
\{\Phi(Q)\}_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)}
is a Cauchy sequence, and therefore it converges.

Case 1(b).
In this case we will show that
\{\Phi(Q)\}_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)}
consists of two converging subsequences (with possibly different limits). Recall from Remark
16.15
, that if
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
, and
B^{Q}
is a corkscrew ball (good or not), then either
B^{Q}\subset H^{+}
or
B^{Q}\subset H^{-}
. Thus if no
Q^{**}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
as in Case 1(a) exists, we conclude that there are two infinite families
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}(\xi)\subset\mathcal{T}(\xi)
, so that if
Q\in\mathcal{T}^{\pm}(\xi)
and
B^{Q}\in\mathcal{B}(Q)
, then
B^{Q}\subset H^{\pm}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}^{+}(\xi)\cup\mathcal{T}^{-}(\xi)=\mathcal{T}(\xi)
.

Claim 17.4
.

With current hypotheses and notation, we have that

(17.3)

L^{+}(\xi):=\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}^{+}(\xi)\,:\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)\mbox{ exists.}

Proof.

To prove the claim, it suffice to following the considerations that lead to the bound (
17.2
), and recall that for any pair
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
, there is a
\theta
-carrot path joining
x_{B^{Q}}
to
x_{B^{Q^{\prime}}}
.
∎

We similarly claim that

Claim 17.5
.

L^{-}(\xi):=\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}^{-}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)\mbox{ exists.}

The proof of this is again the same as (
17.2
), and we leave the details to the reader. Remark that the two limits have not reason to be the same.

Case 2.
No cube
Q^{*}
as in Case 1 exists, and hence we have an infinite family of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
so that
N_{Q}=2
(recall that since
Q\in\mathsf{BWGL}
, it is always the case that
N_{Q}\leq 2
- this was clarified in Remark
16.5
). Now define

\mathsf{U}^{\pm}(\xi):=\{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}(Q)\,|\,Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\mbox{ and }v_{Q}\subset H^{\pm}\}.

Recall that
\mathsf{U}(Q)
is the family of good well connected components of the Whitney region of
Q
which contain a corkscrew ball from a compatible choice (as defined in (
16.17
)).

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
be so that there are
v_{Q},v_{Q^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\xi)
. Then it is easily seen from Remark
16.15
that we can connect any pair
x\in v_{Q}
and
v_{Q^{\prime}}
with a
\theta
-carrot path. Hence, as in the proof of the bound (
17.2
) in Case 1(a), we can show that the limits

\lim_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}^{\pm}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}u_{v_{Q}}\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ exist.}

It can be easily checked that

(17.4)

\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\lim_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}u_{v_{Q}}+\lim_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}^{-}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}u_{v_{Q}}\right).

∎

For all
\xi\in{\partial\Omega}
satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
16.2
, we define the trace
f(\xi)=Tu(\xi)
of
u
as follows:

(17.5)

f(\xi)=Tu(\xi)=\begin{cases}\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)&\mbox{ if Case 1(a) or Case 2 holds.}\\
\frac{L^{+}(\xi)+L^{-}(\xi)}{2}&\mbox{ if Case 1(b) holds.}\end{cases}

17.2.
The trace is in the Haiłasz-Sobolev space
M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})

Remark 17.6
.

We keep
\lambda=3
and the conventions of Remark
17.1
in force.

Lemma 17.7
.

For
u\in\mathbb{V}_{p,\lambda}
, let
f
be defined as in (
17.5
). For each pair
\xi,\zeta
satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
16.2
, we have the estimate,

(17.6)

|f(\xi)-f(\zeta)|\lesssim|x-y|(N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi)+N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\zeta)).

The implicit constant is independent of
\xi,\zeta
.

Remark 17.8
.

Lemma
17.7
(together with Lemma
17.10
below) corresponds to the first part of Theorem
16.1
(4).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Note that
\mathcal{T}(\xi),\mathcal{T}(\zeta)\neq\varnothing
. We consider various cases.

•

Both
\xi,\zeta
satisfy Case 1(a)
. By letting
j
sufficiently large, also depending on
\varepsilon>0
, we find a pair of cube
Q,P\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}
so that
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
,
P\in\mathcal{T}(\zeta)
and
|f(\xi)-\Phi(Q)|<\varepsilon
and
|f(\zeta)-\Phi(P)|
. We need to estimate
|\Phi(Q)-\Phi(S)|
. We have that
N_{Q}=N_{P}=1
. Hence
\Phi(Q)=u_{v_{Q}}
and
\Phi(P)=u_{v_{P}}
. Moreover,
v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}(Q)
and
v_{P}\in\mathsf{U}(P)
, that is,
v_{Q}
and
v_{P}
are
good
well connected components containing (half of) a good corkscrew ball coming from a compatible choice (recall the terminology in Remark
16.10
). So if
B^{Q}\in\mathcal{B}(Q)
and
B^{P}\in\mathcal{B}(P)
, then
0.5B^{Q}\subset v_{Q}
and
0.5B^{P}\subset v_{P}
. By Lemma
16.9
, we have that if
R
is the minimal cube such that
x_{B^{Q}},x_{B^{P}}\in 3B_{R}
, then there are
\theta
-carrot paths
\gamma(Q,R)
and
\gamma(P,R)
joining
x_{B^{Q}}
to
x_{B^{3R}}
and
x_{B^{P}}
to
x_{B^{3R}}
, respectively, where
x_{B^{3R}}
is the center of a good corkscrew ball of radius at least
3\theta\ell(R)
contained in
3B_{R}\cap{\Omega}
. We then compute

(17.7)

|u_{v_{Q}}-u_{v_{P}}|\leq|u_{v_{Q}}-u_{B^{3R}}|+|u_{B^{3R}}-u_{v_{P}}|=:A_{1}+A_{2}.

We concentrate on
A_{1}
, as the estimate for
A_{2}
follows the same path (no pun intended). Let
z\in 0.5B^{Q}
be a point so that
u(z)<+\infty
. Then write

A_{1}\leq|u_{v_{Q}}-u(z)|+|u(z)+u_{B^{3R}}|.

Note that since
z\in 0.5B^{Q}\subset v_{Q}
, we can connect any point
y\in v_{Q}
to
z
via a rectifiable path
\gamma(y,z)\subset v_{Q}
of length
\lesssim_{\theta}|y-z|
. Hence

\displaystyle|u_{v_{Q}}-u(z)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{Q}}|u(y)-u(z)|\,dy\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{Q}}\int_{\gamma(y,z)}\nabla u(s)\,ds\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{v_{Q}}\int_{\gamma(y,z)}\sup_{s\in\gamma(y,z)}\nabla u(s)\,ds\,dy\leq\ell(\gamma(y,z))\cdot\sup_{x\in v_{Q}}\nabla u(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q)N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi).

Note that since
|\gamma(Q,R)|\lesssim\ell(R)
, then we can connect any point
y\in B^{3R}
with a rectifiable curve
\gamma(y,z)
with
|\gamma(y,z)|\lesssim\ell(R)
. Note also that
0.5B^{3R}\subset\Gamma_{D}^{3}(\xi)\cap\Gamma_{D}^{3}(\zeta)
. Thus

\displaystyle|u(z)-u_{B^{3R}}|

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B^{3R}}|u(y)-u(z)|\,dy\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B^{3R}}\int_{\gamma(y,z)}|\nabla u|(s)\,ds\,dy

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{0.5B^{3R}}\ell(R)\sup_{x\in\gamma(y,z)}\nabla u(x)\,dy

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(R)N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi)\approx|\xi-\zeta|N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi).

Since the term
A_{2}
in (
17.7
) can be estimated in a similar way, this gives (
17.6
) in the current case.

•

Both
\zeta
and
\xi
satify Case 1(b).
Recall that
\xi
satisfy Case 1(b) if the limit
\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)
converges up to two sequences. In this case, we defined

f(\xi)=Tu(\xi)=\dfrac{L^{+}(\xi)+L^{-}(\xi)}{2},

where
L^{\pm}(\xi)
is the limit of the sequence
\{\Phi(Q)\}_{Q\in\mathcal{T}^{\pm}(\xi)}
. For the
\varepsilon>0
as fixed at the beginning, pick
j^{\pm}(\xi)
sufficiently small so that if
Q^{\pm}\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j^{\pm}(\xi)}
with
Q^{\pm}\ni\xi
, then
|\Phi(Q^{\pm})-L^{\pm}(\xi)|<\varepsilon
. Choose
j^{\pm}(\zeta)
in the analogue way. Then let
j\geq\max\{j^{\pm}(\xi),j^{\pm}(\zeta)\}
. From now on in this paragraph, we will only consider cubes with sidelength
\leq 2^{-j}
. Given
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}
with
Q\ni\xi
, suppose without loss of generality that
v_{Q}\subset H^{+}(\xi)
