. Denote
Q
by
Q^{+}
. Let
P\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma,j}
so that
P\ni\zeta
. Then either

(a)

\displaystyle v_{P}\subset H^{-}(\zeta)\mbox{ or }

(b)

\displaystyle v_{P}\subset H^{+}(\zeta).

Assume that the first instance (a) is the true one. Now we look at the pairs
(Q,P)
with
\ell(Q)=\ell(P)
,
\xi\in Q
,
\zeta\in P
, and decreasing sidelength. Because both
\xi
and
\zeta
satisfy Case 1(b), it must happen that for some pair
(Q_{1},P_{1})
, we must have that

(i)

\displaystyle v_{Q_{1}}\subset H^{-}(\xi)\mbox{ or }

(ii)

\displaystyle v_{P_{1}}\subset H^{-}(\zeta).

Assume that (i) (and recall of (a)) holds and that
v_{P_{1}}\subset H^{+}(\zeta)
. Then the conclusions of Lemma
16.9
hold for the two pairs of good corkscrews
(B^{Q^{+}},B^{P})
and
(B^{Q_{1}},B^{P_{1}})
. Since
B^{Q}\subset H^{+}(\xi)
,
B^{P}\subset H^{-}(\zeta)
, and
B^{Q_{1}}\subset H^{-}(\xi)
and
B^{P_{1}}\subset H^{-}(\xi)
, we compute

(17.8)

\displaystyle|L^{+}(\xi)+L^{-}(\xi)-L^{+}(\zeta)-L^{-}(\zeta)|

(17.9)

\displaystyle\leq|L^{+}(\xi)-\Phi(Q)|+|\Phi(Q)-\Phi(P)|+|\Phi(P)-L^{+}(\zeta)|

(17.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt+|L^{-}(\xi)-\Phi(Q_{1})|+|\Phi(Q_{1})-\Phi(P_{1})|+|\Phi(P_{1})-L^{-}(\zeta)|

(17.11)

\displaystyle<4\varepsilon+|\Phi(Q)-\Phi(P)|+|\Phi(Q_{1})-\Phi(P_{1})|.

The last two term in the last display can be estimated as the term in (
17.7
), to give

(17.12)

|f(\xi)-f(\zeta)|\lesssim\varepsilon+|\xi-\zeta|\left(N_{D}^{3}(\xi)+N_{D}^{3}(\zeta),\right)

which is the desired (
17.6
). This, whenever (a), (i) and
v_{P_{1}}\subset H^{-}(\zeta)
happens to holds. Now assume (a), (i) and
v_{P_{1}}\subset H^{+}(\zeta)
. We look at successive pairs of the same generation
(Q_{2},P_{2})
so that
\xi\in Q_{2}\subset Q_{1}
and
\zeta\in P_{2}\subset P_{1}
, and we look at the first one so that
v_{P_{2}}
"switches" side, i.e.
v_{P_{2}}\subset H^{+}(\zeta)
. If
v_{Q_{2}}\subset H^{-}(\xi)
and
v_{P_{2}}\subset H^{+}(\zeta)
, then we can carry out the computations (
17.8
) and (
17.12
) using
\Phi(Q)
,
\Phi(P)
,
\Phi(Q_{2})
and
\Phi(P_{2})
. If, on the other hand, we have

v_{Q_{2}}\subset H^{+}(\xi)
, then we compute as in (
17.8
) and (
17.12
) with
\Phi(Q_{1}),\Phi(P_{1})
and
\Phi(Q_{2})
and
\Phi(P_{2})
instead. This let us conclude that the estimate (
17.6
) holds whenever we have (a), (i), and
v_{P_{1}}\subset H^{+}(\zeta)
. All the remaining cases can be dealt with in a similar fashion. With the reader’s help, we conclude that (
17.6
) holds whenever both
\xi
and
\zeta
satisfy Case 1(b).

•

Both
\xi
and
\zeta
satisfy Case 2.
Recall that this is the case where there is a sequence of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)
and
P\in\mathcal{T}(\zeta)
whose sidelength converges to
0
. Recall from (
17.4
) that

f(\xi)=\lim_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(\xi)\,:\,\ell(Q)\to 0}\Phi(Q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\lim_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\xi)}u_{v_{Q}}+\lim_{v_{Q}\in\mathsf{U}^{-}(\xi)}u_{v_{Q}}\right),

and similarly for
f(\zeta)
.
For the
\varepsilon>0
given above, choose
j
so large so that for all cubes
Q\ni\xi
and
P\ni\zeta
with
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-j}
, we have that

\left|\lim_{v_{Q^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\xi)}u_{v_{Q^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{Q}^{+}}\right|<\varepsilon\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 11.74988pt\left|\lim_{v_{Q^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{U}^{-}(\xi)}u_{v_{Q^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{Q}^{-}}\right|<\varepsilon,

where
v_{Q}^{\pm}
is the good well connected component lying in
H^{\pm}(\xi)
. Choose
j
so that the same is true for the limits in
\zeta
. Now, given a pair
(Q,P)
with the properties above, let
B^{Q}_{\pm}\in\mathcal{B}(Q)
and
B^{P}_{\pm}\in\mathcal{B}(P)
(recall that
N_{Q}=N_{P}=2
). Then we see that
B^{Q}_{+}
connects to
B^{P}_{+}
(in the sense of Lemma
16.9
), or to
B^{P}_{-}
. In the first case, we must have that either
B^{Q}_{-}
connects to
B^{P}_{-}
, or that
B^{Q}_{+}
also connects to
B^{P}_{-}
(otherwise
B^{P}_{-}
wouldn’t belong to
\mathcal{B}(P)
, as a compatible choice, in the sense of Lemma
16.10
). We then may argue as in the computations (
17.8
), and obtain (
17.12
). The other case can be dealt with in the same way, and, again, we leave the details to the reader.

•

\xi
satisfies Case 1(a) and
\zeta
satisfies Case 1(b)
. Let us give just a brief sketch: pick
j
large enough, also depending on
\varepsilon
, in particular so that, if
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-j}
, then
|f(\xi)-u_{v_{Q}}|<\varepsilon
; if
P^{\pm}\in\mathcal{T}^{\pm}(\zeta)
is so that
\ell(P^{\pm})\leq 2^{-j}
then
|L^{\pm}(\xi)-u_{v_{P^{+}}}|<\varepsilon
. For one such triple
(Q,P^{+},P^{-})
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\ell(Q)=\ell(P^{+})
and that
\ell(P^{-})<\ell(P^{+})
. It suffices to estimate the difference

u_{v_{Q}}-\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{P^{+}}}-\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{P^{-}}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(u_{v_{Q}}-u_{v_{P^{+}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(u_{v_{Q}}-u_{v_{P^{-}}}\right).

The first term on the right hand side of the latest display may be estimated as in (
17.7
) and the three displays below that. As for the second one, let
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
be a descendant of
Q
containing
\xi
and such that
\ell(Q^{\prime})=\ell(P^{-})
. Note that since
\xi
satisfies case 1(a), and that
j
was chosen sufficiently large, we have that
0.5B^{Q^{\prime}}\subset v_{Q^{\prime}}\subset H^{+}(\xi)
, where
B^{Q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{B}(Q^{\prime})
, which is a corkscrew ball belonging to a compatible choice (see Remark
16.10
). Thus we can join
x_{B^{Q}}
with
x_{B^{Q^{\prime}}}
with a
\theta
-carrot path in
{\Omega}
with length
\lesssim\ell(Q)
. We then write

(u_{v_{Q}}-u_{v_{P^{-}}})=(u_{v_{Q}}-u_{v_{Q^{\prime}}})+(u_{v_{Q^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{P^{-}}}).

Once again, the two terms on the right hand side are easily estimated as in (
17.7
) and the three display below it.

•

\xi
satisfies Case 1(a) and
\zeta
satisfies Case 2.
This case is similar to the previous one, but in fact easier. We leave the details to the reader.

•

\xi
satisfies Case 1(b) and
\zeta
satisfies Case 2.
Choose
j
sufficiently large, depending also on
\varepsilon>0
. Recall that, since
\xi
satisfies Case 1(b),

f(\xi)=\frac{L^{+}(\xi)+L^{-}(\xi)}{2},

and that, since
\zeta
satisfies Case 2,

f(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{v_{P}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\xi)\atop\ell(P)\to 0}u_{v_{P}}+\frac{1}{2}\lim_{v_{P}\in\mathsf{U}^{-}(\xi)\atop\ell(P)\to 0}u_{v_{P}}

Then we pick
j
so large so that we can find a triple
(Q^{+},Q^{-},P)
of cubes with sidelength
\leq 2^{-j}
,
\ell(Q^{+})=\ell(P)
, and such that

|L^{+}(\xi)-\Phi(Q^{+})|<\varepsilon,\hskip 11.74988pt|L^{-}(\xi)-\Phi(Q^{-})|<\varepsilon

and

\left|\lim_{v_{P^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{U}^{+}(\zeta)\atop\ell(P^{\prime})\to 0}u_{v_{P^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{P}^{+}}\right|<\varepsilon,\hskip 11.74988pt\left|\lim_{v_{P^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{U}^{-}(\zeta)\atop\ell(P^{\prime})\to 0}u_{v_{P^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{P}^{-}}\right|,

where
v^{\pm}_{P}\subset\mathsf{U}^{\pm}(\zeta)
. It then suffices to estimate

(17.13)

\left|\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{Q^{+}}}+\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{Q^{-}}}-\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{P}^{+}}-\frac{1}{2}u_{v_{P}^{-}}\right|.

Note that, by Lemma
16.9
,
B^{Q^{+}}\in\mathcal{B}(Q^{+})
is connected with a
\theta
-carrot path to both
B^{P}_{\pm}
. On the other hand, let
P^{\prime}\subset P
be a descendant of
P
containing
\zeta
and such that
\ell(P^{\prime})=\ell(Q^{-})
. Then there is at least one good corkscrew ball
B^{P^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{B}(P^{\prime})
. By virtue of being a good corkscrew ball belonging to a compatible choice, we see that
B^{P^{\prime}}
is connected by a
\theta
-carrot path to
B^{Q^{-}}
(recalling also that
N_{Q^{-}}=1
). Without loss of generality, suppose that
B^{P^{\prime}}\subset H^{+}
(the argument is symmetric). Then
v_{P}^{+}
and
B^{P^{\prime}}
are connected in the usual sense. We then split (
17.13
) as

\frac{1}{2}\left|u_{v_{Q^{+}}}-u_{v_{P}^{-}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|u_{v_{Q^{-}}}-u_{B^{P^{\prime}}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|u_{B^{P^{\prime}}}-u_{v_{P}^{+}}\right|.

As usual, these terms can be estimated as in (
17.7
) and the subsequent three displays.

Since we considered all the possible cases (note that we can always switch the roles of
\xi
and
\zeta
), we conclude the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 17.9
.

Lemma
17.7
says that the limit function
f
we obtained as trace of
u
has an Hajlasz upper gradient. To conclude the proof of our trace theorem, we need to check that
f\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})
.

Lemma 17.10
.

f\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})
.

Proof.

Pick
\xi_{0}\in{\partial\Omega}
such that
N(\nabla u)(\xi_{0})<+\infty
, and such that
|f(\xi_{0})|<\infty
is well defined as a limit. Then, by Lemma
17.7
, we have that

|f(\xi)-f(\xi)|\lesssim|\xi-\xi_{0}|(N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi)+N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)(\xi_{0})).

Thus

\displaystyle\|f-f(\xi_{0})\|_{p}^{p}\lesssim\diam({\Omega})^{p}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}|N(\nabla u)(\xi)|^{p}\,d\sigma(\xi)+N(\nabla u)(\xi_{0})^{p}\sigma({\partial\Omega})\right)<+\infty.

∎

Remark 17.11
.

Since
f\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})
and
N_{\text{\tiny{W}}}(\nabla u)\in L^{p}({\partial\Omega})
is an Hajlasz uppser gradient of
f
, we conclude that
f\in M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
. Equation (
16.5
) follows now immediately:

\|\mathcal{T}u\|_{\dot{M}^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})}\leq\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
16.1
(4).

18.
Construction of the extension

In this section, we gives proofs for Theorem
16.1
(1,3,5).

18.1.
Definition of
u

We start off by constructing the extension
u
.
Suppose that
{\partial\Omega}
is Ahlfors
d
-regular and consider the dyadic lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sigma}
defined in Section
5
.
Then, for each Whitney cube
P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
(as in Section
16.2
) there is some cube
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\sigma}
such that
\ell(Q)\approx\ell(P)

and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(P,Q)\approx\ell(Q)
, with the implicit constants depending on the parameters of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sigma}
and on the Whitney decomposition.

\displaystyle\mbox{Given }\,P\in{\mathcal{W}}({\Omega}),\,

\displaystyle\mbox{ we denote by }\,Q_{P}\mbox{ the cube }Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}

(18.1)

\displaystyle\mbox{ s.t. }\ell(Q)\approx\ell(P)\mbox{ and }\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,P)\approx\ell(P).

Conversely,
given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\sigma}
, we let

(18.2)

w(Q)=\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)\atop Q_{P}=Q}P.

It is immediate to check that
w(Q)
is made up at most of a uniformly bounded number of cubes
P
, but it may happen
that
w(Q)=\varnothing
.

Next, for each Whitney cube
P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
we consider
a
C^{\infty}
bump function
\varphi_{P}
supported on
1.1P
such that the functions
\{\varphi_{P}\}_{P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)}
,
form a partition of unity of
\chi_{\Omega}
. That is,

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)}\varphi_{P}=\chi_{\Omega}.

Also, if we set

(18.3)

A_{P}:=A_{M\cdot B_{Q_{P}}},

where
A_{M\cdot B_{Q_{P}}}
is the affine map as in Lemma
6.14
- roughly speaking the affine map that minimises
\gamma_{f}^{1}(M\cdot B_{b(P)})
, and
M
is a sufficiently large constant, as in Proposition
10.9
. Finally,
we define the extension
u:\overline{\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of
f
as follows:

\displaystyle u|_{{\partial\Omega}}=f

(18.4)

\displaystyle u|_{\Omega}=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)}\varphi_{P}\,A_{P}.

We note immediately that
u
is smooth in
\Omega
.

Below we will need the following fact, which we recall from Lemma
6.18
.

•

If
B
is a ball centered on
{\partial\Omega}
, then

(18.5)

|\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\lesssim\gamma^{1}_{f}(B)+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},

where
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
and
s\leq p
.

•

If
B\subset B^{\prime}
are two balls centered on
{\partial\Omega}
so that
r_{B}\approx_{c}r_{B^{\prime}}
, then

(18.6)

|A_{B}(x)-A_{B^{\prime}}(x)|\lesssim_{c}\gamma_{f}^{1}(B^{\prime})\left(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,B^{\prime})+r_{B^{\prime}}\right),

and also

(18.7)

|\nabla A_{B}-\nabla A_{B^{\prime}}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(B^{\prime}).

Furthermore, from Lemma
6.17
, we have that, if again
B
is centered on
{\partial\Omega}
, then

(18.8)

\gamma_{f}^{1}(B)\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},

whenever
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
and
1\leq s\leq p
.

18.2.
A technical lemma

In this subsection we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 18.1
.

There exists a constant
C\geq 1
, depending
13
13

13

Recall that
\Lambda
is the constant from Theorem
4.2
.
on
M
,
\Lambda
, and the parameters of the Whitney and boundary lattices, so that, for each
P_{0}\in\mathcal{W}(\Omega)
, if
x\in P_{0}
, then

(18.9)

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x)|\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-9.12212pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-7.06155pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.04713pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.771pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda CB_{Q_{P_{0}}}}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},\,\,;1\leq s\leq p;

(18.10)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}u(x)|\lesssim\gamma_{f}(CB_{b(P_{0})})\,\ell(P_{0})^{-1}.

Proof.

Now let
x\in P_{0}
. Note that

\displaystyle\nabla u(x)

\displaystyle=\nabla\left(u(x)-A_{P_{0}}(x))+\nabla A_{P_{0}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{W}(\Omega)}\nabla\varphi_{P}(x)\left(A_{P}(x)-A_{P_{0}}(x)\right)+\sum_{P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})}\varphi_{P}(x)\nabla A_{P}(x).

Then,

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x)|\leq\sum_{P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})}|\nabla\varphi_{P}(x)||A_{P}(x)-A_{P_{0}}(x)|+\sum_{P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})}|\varphi_{P}(x)||\nabla A_{P}(x)|=S_{1}+S_{2}.

We estimate the first sum
S_{1}
. Since
x\in P_{0}
, then
\nabla\varphi_{P}(x)\neq 0
only whenever
1.1P\cap P_{0}\neq\emptyset
. Thus, for these Whitney cubes
P
,
\ell(P)\approx\ell(P_{0})
, where the implicit constant depend (only) on the construction of the Whitney cubes. In particular, this implies that
\ell(Q_{P})\approx\ell(Q_{P_{0}})
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{P},P)\approx\ell(P)
, it is also easy to see that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{P},Q_{P_{0}})\lesssim\ell(Q_{P_{0}})\approx\ell(P_{0})
. We conclude that there exists a constant
C\geq 1
so that

CB_{Q_{P_{0}}}\supset 2B_{Q_{P}}\,\mbox{ for each }\,P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})\,\mbox{ such that }\,1.1P\cap P_{0}\neq\emptyset.

Moreover, for obvious reasons,
\#\{P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})\,|\,1.1P\cap P_{0}\neq\emptyset\}\lesssim_{d}1
. Using (
18.6
) and recalling that
\nabla\varphi_{P}\lesssim\ell(P)^{-1}
, we compute

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,C}\frac{\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})\left(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})+C^{\prime}\ell(P_{0})\right)}{\ell(P_{0})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ext:form3}}}{{\lesssim}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda CB_{Q_{P_{0}}}}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},

for
1\leq s\leq p
.
This takes care of
S_{1}
. The bound

S_{2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda CB_{Q_{P_{0}}}}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}

follows via a similar reasoning and using (
18.5
) and (
18.8
). This and the preceeding estimate prove (
18.9
).

We now turn our attention to (
18.10
). Set
{\partial}_{i}={\partial}_{x_{i}}
,
1\leq i\leq d+1
. Recalling that
\sum_{P}\varphi(x)=1
, we compute

\displaystyle\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(x){\partial}_{j}A_{P}(x)=\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(x){\partial}_{j}(A_{P}-A_{P_{0}})(x)-{\partial}_{j}A_{P_{0}}(x).

Since
A_{P_{0}}
and
A_{P}
are affine, we get

\displaystyle{\partial}_{i}\left(\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(x){\partial}_{j}A_{P}(x)\right)=\sum_{P}{\partial}_{i}\varphi_{P}(x)\,{\partial}_{j}(A_{P}-A_{P_{0}})(x),

and hence

\displaystyle{\partial}_{i}{\partial}_{j}u(x)

\displaystyle={\partial}_{i}\left[\sum_{P}{\partial}_{j}\varphi_{P}(x)\left(A_{P}(x)-A_{P_{0}}(x)\right)+\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(x){\partial}_{j}A_{P}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{P}{\partial}_{i}{\partial}_{j}\varphi_{P}(x)\left(A_{P}(x)-A_{P_{0}}(x)\right)+\sum_{P}{\partial}_{j}\varphi_{P}(x)\,{\partial}_{i}\left(A_{P}-A_{P_{0}}\right)(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{P}{\partial}_{i}\varphi_{P}(x)\,{\partial}_{j}(A_{P}-A_{P_{0}})(x)

\displaystyle=:S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}.

Using that
|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{P}|\lesssim\ell(P)^{-2}
and (
18.6
), and reasoning as above, we obtain that
|S_{1}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})\ell(P_{0})^{-1}
. Similarly, using (
18.7
), we have
|S_{2}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})\ell(P_{0})^{-1}
and also
|S_{3}|\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P_{0}}})\ell(P_{0})^{-1}
.
∎

18.3.
Proof of Theorem
16.1
(1): the
L^{p}
estimates (
16.2
)

To ease some computations, we introduce the following dyadic versions of
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{S}
. For a point
x\in{\partial\Omega}
, we set

(18.11)

\Gamma_{D}(x)=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\atop Q\ni x}w(Q)=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}\atop Q\ni x}\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})\atop Q=b(P)}P.

This is a ‘dyadic’ version of the cone
\Gamma(x)
. Then, for possibly vector valued function
F
on
\Omega
, we put

\mathcal{N}_{D}(F)(x):=\sup_{y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)}|F(y)|,

and

\mathcal{S}_{D}(F)(x):=\left(\int_{\Gamma_{D}(x)}\frac{|F(y)|^{2}}{d_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lemma 18.2
.

Assumptions and notation as in Theorem
16.1
. It suffices to show the estimate (
16.2
) with
\mathcal{N}_{D}
and
\mathcal{S}_{D}
.

Proof.

It is not difficult to see that
\|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\approx\|\mathcal{N}_{D}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{S}_{D}
and
\mathcal{S}
. See
[
HT20
]
, Lemma 1.27 and its proof.
∎

Lemma 18.3
.

With assumptions and notation of Theorem
16.1
, for any
g\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f)
,

\|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

Proof.

Let
x\in{\partial\Omega}
and
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)
. Then there is a
P=P(y)\in\mathcal{W}({\Omega})
such that
Q_{P}\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
has center
x_{Q_{P}}
satisfying
|x-x_{Q_{P}}|\lesssim_{C^{\prime}}\ell(Q_{P})\approx\ell(P)
, where the implicit constant
C^{\prime}
depends only on the Whitney decomposition and on the parameters of
\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
. Hence,

|\nabla u(y)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ext-lem1}}}{{\lesssim}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda CB_{Q_{P(y)}}}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{C^{\prime\prime}B(x,\ell(P(y)))}g^{s}\,d\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{s}},

where
C^{\prime\prime}
depends on
C^{\prime}
,
C
and
\Lambda
. Clearly then, for any
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)
,

(18.12)

|\nabla u(y)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x).

Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{{\partial\Omega}}|\mathcal{N}(\nabla u)(x)|^{p}\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{{\partial\Omega}}|\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)|^{p}\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=C\int_{{\partial\Omega}}(M_{C\diam({\Omega})}(g^{s}))^{\frac{p}{s}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p/s}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}g^{p}\,d\sigma.

∎

Lemma 18.4
.

With the assumptions and notation of Theorem
16.1
, we have

\|\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\,\nabla^{2}u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}(d_{\Omega}(\cdot)\nabla^{2}u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{D}(x)}\frac{|\nabla^{2}u(y)|^{2}d_{\Omega}(y)^{2}}{d_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\,dy\right|^{\frac{p}{2}}\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left|\sum_{Q\ni x}\sum_{P\in w(Q)}\int_{P}\frac{|\nabla^{2}u(y)|^{2}d_{\Omega}(y)^{2}}{d_{\Omega}(y)^{d+1}}\,dy\right|^{\frac{p}{2}}\,d\sigma(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:ext-lem2}}}{{\lesssim}}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left|\sum_{Q\ni x}\sum_{P\in w(Q)}\int_{P}\frac{\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P}})^{2}}{\ell(Q_{P})^{d+1}}\,dy\right|^{\frac{p}{2}}\,d\sigma(x).

It is immediate from the definitions that if
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
and
P\in w(Q)
, then
\ell(Q_{P})\approx\ell(Q)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{P},Q)\lesssim\ell(Q)
. Hence there is a constant
C^{\prime}\geq 1
, depending on the constant
C
in the last display, and possibly the parameters of the Whitney cubes, so that
\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P}})\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(C^{\prime}B_{Q})
. We can then conclude that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}(d_{{\Omega}}(\cdot)\nabla^{2}u)\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left|\sum_{Q\ni x}\gamma^{1}_{f}(C^{\prime}B_{Q})^{2}\right|^{\frac{p}{2}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d}(x)

\displaystyle\approx\|\mathcal{G}_{f}^{1}\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}^{p}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}({\partial\Omega})}^{p},

where the last inequality is Theorem
A
.
∎

18.4.
Proof of Theorem
16.1
(3): convergence in the Lipschitz case

We now prove Theorem
16.1
(3) and in the next subsection we prove Theorem
16.1
(5), that is, the non-tangential convergence almost everywhere of the extension
u
to
f
.

Hypotheses as in Theorem
16.1
(3).

Remark 18.5
.

The fact that
u\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}(\overline{\Omega})
, equation (
16.3
) is proven in
[
MT21
, Lemma 4.2]
. The extension is denoted there by
\widetilde{f}
, but the definition is precisely the one used here.

It remains to show the non-tangential convergence of
\nabla u
, i.e. (
16.4
). To this end, we briefly recall Proposition
10.9
. If
\partial\Omega
is uniformly
d
-rectifiable,
M>1
sufficiently large and
f:{\partial\Omega}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
L
-Lipschitz, the, for
\sigma
-almost all
x\in{\partial\Omega}
, the following holds. If we take a sequence of cubes
\{Q_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
such that
\ell(Q_{j})\to 0
and
x\in Q_{j}
for each
j
, then

(18.13)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\sup_{y\in MB_{Q_{j}}}\left[A_{Q_{j}}(y)-f(x)-\langle\nabla_{t}f(x),y-x\rangle\right]}{\ell(Q_{j})}=0.

Now let
x\in\partial\Omega
be so that (
18.13
) holds and let
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)
. Since
y\to x
in
\Gamma_{D}(x)
, then there is a sequence
\{P_{j}\}
of Whitney cubes so that
P_{j}\subset\Gamma_{D}(x)
,
\ell(P_{j})\to 0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{j})\to 0
. The corresponding boundary cubes
\{Q_{j}\}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition
10.9
. For
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)
, call
P(y)
the Whitney cube containing it. We then compute

\displaystyle|\nabla u(y)-\nabla_{t}f(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{P}\nabla\varphi_{P}(y)\left(A_{P}(y)-A_{P(y)}(y)\right)\right|+\left|\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(y)\left(\nabla A_{P}-\nabla_{t}f(x)\right)\right|

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

Using (
18.7
), the fact that
\varphi_{P}(y)\neq 0
only whenever
1.1P\cap P(y)\neq\emptyset
, and
\nabla\varphi_{P}(y)\lesssim\ell(P)^{-1}\approx\ell(P(y))^{-1}
, we immediately obtian that
I_{1}\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(CB_{Q_{P(y)}})
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(y)(\nabla A_{P}-\nabla A_{P(y)})\right|+\left|\sum_{P}\varphi_{P}(y)(\nabla A_{P(y)}-\nabla_{t}f(x))\right|

\displaystyle:=I_{2,1}+I_{2,2}.

The term
I_{2,1}
is dealt with just like
I_{1}
, using (
18.6
) instead of (
18.7
). As for the other one,

I_{2,2}\lesssim|\nabla A_{P(y)}-\nabla_{t}f(x)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:A_j-gradf}}}{{\to}}0

as
y\to x
in
\Gamma_{D}(x)
. This concludes the proof of the non-tangential convergence of
\nabla u
and thus that of Theorem
16.1
(3).

18.5.
Non-tangential convergence of
u
to
f
for general
f

What is left is the proof Theorem
16.1
(5). The next lemma takes care of this.

Lemma 18.6
.

Hypotheses as in Theorem
16.1
(5). Then

(18.14)

u\to f(\xi)\,\,\mbox{non-tangentially a.e.}\,\,\xi\in{\partial\Omega}.

18.5.1.
Assume first that
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}({\partial\Omega})\cap M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})

The statement in general will follow by density of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}({\partial\Omega})\cap M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
in
M^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
(see
[
HKST15
, Lemma 10.2.7]
).
But if
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}({\partial\Omega})
, then
u\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Lip}(\overline{{\Omega}})
by Remark
18.5
. Thus non-tangential convergence holds by continuity.

General case: let
f\in\mathcal{M}^{1,p}({\partial\Omega})
.
For a sequence
\lambda_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
, set

(18.15)

E_{k}:=\{x\in E\,|\,g(x)>\lambda_{k}\}.

Note that
f|_{E\setminus E_{k}}
is
C\lambda_{k}
-Lipschitz. We denote by
f_{k}
the standard
C\lambda_{k}
-Lipschitz extension to the whole of
E
of
f|_{E\setminus E_{k}}
. It is shown in the proof of
[
HKST15
, Lemma 10.2.7]
(but see also the original paper
[
Haj96
, Theorem 5]
) that

(18.16)

g_{k}=(g-C\lambda_{k})\mathds{1}_{E_{k}}\in{\rm Grad}_{p}(f_{k}).

We extend
f_{k}
to
{\Omega}
as in (
18.1
), and call the extension
u_{k}
.

Let
x_{0}\in G
, where
G\subset E
is a subset of full measure, i.e.
|E|=|G|
, which we will implicitly re-define several times below, so that all the statement that hold
\sigma
-a.e. in
E
, hold everywhere in
G
. To start with, we assume that
G
is a full measure subset of
E\setminus\cap_{k\geq 1}E_{k}
, so that if
x_{0}\in G
, then for some
k=k(x_{0})
sufficiently large,
\mathds{1}_{E_{k}}(x_{0})=0
. Now let
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x)<+\infty

\sigma
-a.e., we may assume that
\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{0})<+\infty
, and hence we can find a
\delta=\delta(x_{0})>0
sufficiently small so that

(18.17)

\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{0})\cdot\delta<\varepsilon.

Let
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)\cap B(x,\delta)
. We denote by
A^{k}_{P}
the best approxiating affine maps defining
u_{k}
- they approximate
f_{k}
. Let also
P_{y}
be the Whitney cube in
\mathcal{W}({\Omega})
containing
y
. We compute

\displaystyle|u_{k}(y)-u(y)|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{P}\varphi(y)\left(A_{P}^{k}(y)-A_{P}(y)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{P}\varphi(y)(A_{P}^{k}(y)-A^{k}_{P_{y}}(y))\right|+\left|\sum_{P}\varphi(y)(A^{k}_{P_{y}}(y)-A_{P_{y}}(y))\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\left|\sum_{P}\varphi(y)(A_{P_{y}}(y)-A_{P}(y))\right|

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

Since
1.1P\cap P_{y}\neq\emptyset
, then
\ell(P)\approx P_{y}
, and therefore there is a
C\geq 1
, depending on
M
, so that
M\cdot B_{Q_{P_{y}}},M\cdot B_{Q_{P}}\subset B_{x_{0},y}:=B(x_{0},C\ell(P_{y}))
- recall the definition of
A_{P}
in (
18.3
). Hence, using (
18.6
), we have

(18.18)

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{W}}({\Omega})\atop 1.1P\cap P\neq\varnothing}|A^{k}_{P}(y)-A^{k}_{P_{y}}(y)|\lesssim\gamma_{f_{k}}^{1}(B_{x_{0},y})\ell(P(y))\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}}g_{k}\,d\sigma\cdot\delta.

Proceeding analogously,

(18.19)

I_{3}\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(B_{x_{0},y})|x_{0}-y|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{0})\cdot\delta<\varepsilon.

Before going further, we want to bound (
18.18
). Set
B=5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}
, just for the present calculation. We see that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}g_{k}\,d\sigma=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}g_{k}-g\,d\sigma+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int g\,d\sigma.

Now,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B}g-g_{k}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{r(B)^{d}}\int_{B\cap E_{k}}g-g_{k}\,d\sigma+\frac{1}{r(B)^{d}}\int_{B\setminus E_{k}}g\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda_{k}\frac{|B\cap E_{k}|}{|B|}+\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{0}).

Thus

(18.20)

I_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ext-form505}}}{{\lesssim}}\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|B\cap E_{k}|}{|B|}+\varepsilon.

We are left with
I_{2}
. We compute

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|A_{P_{y}}^{k}(y)-A_{P_{y}}(y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim|A_{P_{y}}^{k}(y)-A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x_{0})|+|A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x_{0})-A_{P_{y}}(x_{0})|+|A_{P_{y}}(x_{0})-A_{P_{y}}(y)|

\displaystyle=:I_{2,1}+I_{2,2}+I_{2,3}.

Using again (
18.6
), and reasoning as in the paragraph above (
18.18
), we obtain that

I_{2,1}\lesssim\delta\cdot\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}}g_{k}\,d\sigma,

and hence, as in (
18.20
),

I_{2,1}\lesssim\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}\cap E_{k}|}{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}|}+\varepsilon.

On the other hand, as for
I_{3}
above, in (
18.19
), we get

I_{2,3}\lesssim\gamma_{f}^{1}(B_{x_{0},y})\cdot|x_{0}-y|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{s}f(x_{0})\cdot\delta.

To deal with
I_{2,2}
we compute

\displaystyle I_{2,2}

\displaystyle=|A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x_{0})-A_{P_{y}}(x_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq|A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x_{0})-f_{k}(x_{0})|+|f_{k}(x_{0})-f(x_{0})|+|f(x_{0})-A_{P_{y}}(x_{0})|

\displaystyle=:I_{2,2,1}+I_{2,2,2}+I_{2,2,3}.

Assuming that we were working with a subsequence, as we may, we have that

I_{2,2,2}\to 0\mbox{ as }k\to\infty.

Let us look at
I_{2,2,1}
. We have

\displaystyle I_{2,2,1}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{x_{0},y}}A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x_{0})-A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x)\,d\sigma(x)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt+\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{x_{0},y}}A_{P_{y}}^{k}(x)-f_{k}(x)\,d\sigma(x)\right|+\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{x_{0},y}}f_{k}(x)-f_{k}(x_{0})\,d\sigma(x)\right|

\displaystyle=:I_{2,2,1}^{1}+I_{2,2,1}^{2}+I_{2,2,1}^{3}.

Now, recalling that
r(B_{x_{0},y})\approx\ell(P_{y})\approx|x_{0}-y|<C\delta
,

I_{2,2,1}^{1}\lesssim\delta\cdot|\nabla A_{P_{y}}^{k}|.

We use (
18.5
) to bound the right hand side of the display above, thus obtaining,

I_{2,2,1}^{1}\lesssim\delta\cdot\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}}g_{k}\,d\sigma\lesssim\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}\cap E_{k}|}{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}|}+\varepsilon,

where the last inequality follows as in (
18.20
).
On the other hand,

I_{2,2,1}^{2}\lesssim\ell(P_{y})\gamma_{f_{k}}^{1}(B_{x_{0},y})\lesssim\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}\cap E_{k}|}{|5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}|}+\varepsilon,

where the last inequality is as in (
18.18
) and (
18.20
). Finally, using the definition of Hajłasz upper gradient, we find that

I_{2,2,1}^{3}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{B_{x_{0},y}}|x_{0}-x|(g(x)+g(x_{0}))\,d\sigma(x)\lesssim\ell(P_{y})\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-8.95775pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.89719pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.88277pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.60663pt}}\!\int_{5\Lambda B_{x_{0},y}}g_{k}\,d\sigma+\ell(P_{y})g_{k}(x_{0}).

If we assume
k
sufficently large, then
g_{k}(x_{0})=0
(see the paragraph above (
18.17
). The other term on the right hand side of the last display can be dealt with as in (
18.18
)-(
18.20
). The term
I_{2,2,3}
is dealt with in a similar manner.

To conclude, for any
\varepsilon>0
, for all
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)\cap B(x_{0},\delta)
,

|u_{k}(y)-u(y)|\lesssim\varepsilon+\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|B\cap E_{k}|}{|B|}.

It is show in the proof of
[
HKST15
, Lemma 10.2.7]
that

\lambda_{k}|E_{k}|\to 0\mbox{ as }k\to\infty.

We can now conclude the proof of Lemma
18.6
. Let
x_{0}\in GE\setminus E_{0}
, where
E_{0}
is a subset of
E
with
|E_{0}|=0
, chosen so that all a.e. requirements in the computations above are true. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we find a
\delta>0
so that if
y\in\Gamma_{D}(x)\cap B(x_{0},\delta)
, then

\displaystyle|u(y)-f(x_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq|u(y)-u_{k}(y)|+|u_{k}(y)-f_{k}(x_{0})|+|f_{k}(x_{0})-f(x_{0})|

\displaystyle<2\varepsilon+\delta\cdot\lambda_{k}\frac{|B\cap E_{k}|}{|B|}+|f_{k}(x_{0})-f(x_{0})|.

Letting
k\to\infty
, we are left with

(18.21)

|u(y)-f(x_{0})|<2\varepsilon,

and this ends the proof of Lemma
18.6
, and thus of Theorem
16.1
(5).
